### [1] [Per-Stroke Temporal Control for Text-to-Motion via Action Units and Action-Detection Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15717)
*Euijun Jung,Youngki Lee*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces Action Units (AUs) as typed temporal events for precise per-stroke control in text-to-motion generation, using a lightweight gated adapter and training-free classifier gradient to correct timing errors, achieving superior stroke accuracy and motion quality on StrokeBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-to-motion models struggle with precise temporal control (e.g., when each stroke lands in a sequence like "four punches"), often failing to produce separable strokes even when they correctly execute the named action.

Method: Introduces Action Units (AUs) as typed temporal events specifying body track, action class, time window, and impact timing. Uses a lightweight gated adapter to inject two streams (per-stroke tokens + per-frame phase channel) into a frozen text-to-motion backbone, and a training-free classifier gradient from a frozen frame-level detector to correct residual timing errors at inference.

Result: The AU grounding approach markedly improves the rate of correctly placed single strokes compared to the strongest prior interface, while achieving the best motion quality among text-, interval-, and frame-level baselines on the StrokeBench benchmark.

Conclusion: Action Units enable precise per-stroke temporal control in text-to-motion generation, with the prompted core frame emerging as an additional steerable axis beyond stroke count, ordering, and track.

Abstract: Text-to-motion models are competent at the action a prompt names but unreliable at when each stroke lands: four punches alternating left and right rarely return four separable strokes. We introduce typed temporal events called Action Units (AUs) that make the individual stroke -- its body track, action class, time window, and impact timing -- an explicit conditioning signal. We ground a frozen text-to-motion backbone on the AU set through a lightweight gated adapter injecting two streams (per-stroke tokens and a per-frame phase channel), and at inference close residual timing errors with a training-free classifier gradient from a frozen frame-level detector. We measure per-stroke control on StrokeBench, whose prompts specify count, ordering, track, and core-frame placement, paired with an audited stroke corpus. AU grounding markedly raises the rate of correctly placed single strokes over the strongest prior interface, at the best motion quality among text-, interval-, and frame-level baselines. The prompted core frame emerges as a further steerable axis.

</details>


### [2] [EgoExoMoCap: Distributed Ego-Exo Human Motion Capture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15868)
*Jiaxi Jiang,Bharat Lal Bhatnagar,Nan Yang,Lingni Ma,Sebastian Starke,Robin Kips,Nadine Bertsch,Christian Holz,Federica Bogo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EgoExoMoCap is a distributed framework that jointly leverages ego- and exocentric multi-modal signals from HMDs (smart glasses) for human motion estimation, combining head/wrist tracking with DINOv3-based visual features to achieve robust 3D motion reconstruction without bulky setups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches treat egocentric body tracking (estimating the wearer's motion) and exocentric tracking (capturing surrounding people's movements) in isolation. The paper aims to unify these paradigms for scalable, real-world human motion capture using minimal hardware.

Method: A distributed framework where two or more people each wear smart glasses; leverages head (and optionally wrist) tracking signals for global 3D motion estimation, combined with DINOv3 context-aware image features for robustness against noise and occlusions.

Result: Extensive experiments on two in-the-wild datasets demonstrate robust motion reconstruction even in challenging scenarios.

Conclusion: Joint ego- and exocentric multimodal signals from simple HMDs can effectively replace bulky multi-camera setups or mocap suits for human motion capture.

Abstract: Human motion capture from head-mounted devices (HMDs) offers a scalable way to acquire real-world human motion and interaction data, which is crucial for applications in embodied AI and VR/AR. Existing approaches focus on either egocentric body tracking, estimating the motion of the subject wearing the device, or exocentric tracking, capturing the movements of people in the wearer's surroundings. So far, these two paradigms have largely been explored in isolation. In this paper, we propose a novel distributed framework that jointly leverages ego- and exocentric multi-modal signals for human motion estimation from HMDs. Unlike traditional motion capture systems requiring bulky multi-camera setups or obtrusive mocap suits, our approach, EgoExoMoCap, is as simple as two (or more) people, each wearing a pair of smart glasses. The method leverages head (plus potentially wrist) tracking signals for accurate estimation of global motion in the 3D world and combines context-aware image features based on DINOv3 to achieve robustness in the presence of noise and occlusions. Extensive experiments on two in-the-wild datasets show that our approach can robustly reconstruct motion even in challenging scenarios.

</details>


### [3] [An Empirical Study of Handcrafted Feature Learning and Convolutional Neural Networks for Facial Expression Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15288)
*Chethiya Galkaduwa*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study compares HOG+SVM, LBP+Logistic Regression, and a lightweight CNN across FER-2013, CK+, and KDEF datasets for facial expression recognition. CNNs perform best overall, especially on complex data; HOG works well in controlled settings; LBP performs poorly across all datasets. Dataset complexity significantly impacts performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically compare classical handcrafted feature descriptors (HOG, LBP) against modern lightweight CNNs for facial expression recognition, and to evaluate how dataset complexity affects their relative performance.

Method: Three pipelines are evaluated: HOG features with SVM classifier, LBP features with Logistic Regression classifier, and a lightweight CNN trained end-to-end. Experiments are conducted on three datasets: FER-2013 (in-the-wild), CK+ (lab-controlled), and KDEF (posed expressions).

Result: CNNs achieve the best overall accuracy, particularly on complex in-the-wild data (FER-2013). HOG performs competitively on controlled datasets (CK+). LBP yields poor performance across all datasets. Dataset complexity is a major factor influencing method ranking.

Conclusion: Robust feature learning (via CNNs) is essential for real-world facial expression recognition. Handcrafted features like HOG remain viable in constrained settings, but LBP is ineffective for this task. Future work should focus on handling dataset variability and improving generalization.

Abstract: Facial expression recognition is an important computer vision task with applications in human--computer interaction, mental health monitoring, driver alert systems, and behavioral analysis. While convolutional neural networks (CNNs) dominate modern facial expression recognition, handcrafted feature descriptors such as Histogram of Oriented Gradients (HOG) and Local Binary Patterns (LBP) remain useful classical baselines. This study compares HOG with Support Vector Machine (SVM), LBP with Logistic Regression, and a lightweight CNN across three facial expression datasets: FER-2013, CK+, and KDEF. The results show that CNNs achieve the best overall performance, particularly on more complex data, while HOG performs strongly in controlled environments. LBP performs poorly across all datasets. The study highlights that dataset complexity significantly affects performance and that robust feature learning is essential for real-world facial expression recognition.

</details>


### [4] [Rethinking the Readout: Unlocking Video Backbones for AI-Generated Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15321)
*Manni Cui,Ziheng Qin,ZiAn Wang,Ruiqi Liu,Dianyuan Zou,Jianglan Wei,Han Zhou,Yu Liu,Jingrui Xu,Wenhao Wang,Zhenyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes V-PVP, a lightweight plug-and-play readout module that captures patch-level temporal dynamics in video backbones, improving AIGV detection by replacing standard global aggregation with dual-stream velocity profiling. Achieves 95.28 AUC on AIGVDBench with frozen backbone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video pretrained backbones fail to outperform image pretrained ones on AIGV detection due to excessive spatiotemporal aggregation in their readout, which compresses frames into global descriptors and suppresses local patch-level temporal dynamics and inter-patch relations that are crucial for detecting inter-frame inconsistencies.

Method: Velocity Gated Patch Velocity Profiling (V-PVP): a lightweight readout module with dual parallel streams operating on patch velocity fields, replacing only the aggregation layer. Adds ~0.5M parameters and works as plug-and-play under both end-to-end fine-tuning and linear probing.

Result: Achieves 95.28 AUC on AIGVDBench with fully frozen backbone, consistently improves performance across diverse video backbones.

Conclusion: Simply replacing the aggregation layer with V-PVP reactivates the temporal potential of frozen video backbones, restoring their advantage over image-only pretrained probes for AIGV detection.

Abstract: AI-generated videos (AIGVs) typically contain subtle temporal artifacts that arise from inter-frame inconsistencies rather than within individual frames. A detector that captures such artifacts should therefore benefit from video pretrained backbones over image only ones. In practice, however, video backbones with standard global readouts often fail to outperform strong image pretrained probes on AIGV benchmarks. We attribute this gap to excessive spatiotemporal aggregation in the readout. Video pretrained backbones tend to compress each frame into a single global descriptor. This compression suppresses local patch level temporal dynamics and discards inter patch relations, which are precisely the cues that AIGV detection most reliably depends on. Based on this, we propose Velocity Gated Patch Velocity Profiling (V-PVP), a lightweight readout that replaces only the aggregation layer with two parallel streams over the patch velocity field, adding only about $0.5$M trainable parameters. V-PVP serves as a general plug-and-play module that consistently improves performance across diverse video backbones under both end-to-end fine-tuning and linear probing settings. Our method reaches \textbf{95.28} AUC on AIGVDBench while keeping the backbone fully frozen. The results show that simply replacing the aggregation layer reactivates the temporal potential of frozen video backbones, restoring their advantage on AIGV detection. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/PVP-81B3/.

</details>


### [5] [Training-Free Open-Vocabulary 3D Point-Cloud Segmentation on the Generalized Few-Shot Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15331)
*Silas kwabla Gah,Ebenezer Owusu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a fully training-free, open-vocabulary pipeline for generalized few-shot 3D point-cloud segmentation that uses no training, no 3D labels, and no few-shot support annotations. It reconciles a frozen 3D vision-language model (RegionPLC) with a frozen promptable concept segmenter (SAM3) via cross-view consistency, achieving competitive results on ScanNet200 and ScanNet++ benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate how far the reconciliation of dense 3D vision-language priors with few-shot support can go without any training, 3D labels, or few-shot support annotations, challenging the reliance on extensive supervision in current GFS-PCS methods.

Method: Pairs a frozen 3D vision-language model (RegionPLC) as a dense prior with a frozen promptable concept segmenter (SAM3) prompted by novel class names lifted from posed RGB views, then reconciles them via cross-view consistency—a point becomes novel only when enough views that see it agree. A fusion gate and prototypical dense classifier for few-shot support were also tested but found to add nothing over consistency alone.

Result: On ScanNet200: +2.6 novel mIoU over training-free dense prior, base accuracy within 0.5, recovers 33% of novel-class gap to trained SOTA. On ScanNet++: nearly doubles novel mIoU (+15.7, from 16.2 to 31.9) with 1.7 base cost, lifting harmonic mean from 21.5 to 31.1.

Conclusion: Cross-view consistency alone is sufficient for effective open-vocabulary GFS-PCS without any training, 3D labels, or few-shot support; injecting few-shot support via a classifier actually degrades performance, explaining why the method needs no support at all.

Abstract: Generalized few-shot 3D point-cloud segmentation (GFS-PCS) asks a model to segment a scene into many base classes seen at training time and a set of novel classes. The state of the art reaches novel classes by reconciling a dense but noisy 3D vision-language prior with the few-shot support, but it pays for this with base 3D labels, per-episode training, and the support annotations themselves. We ask how far the same reconciliation can go with none of these: no training, no 3D labels, and not even the few-shot support. We pair a frozen 3D vision-language model (RegionPLC) as a dense prior with a frozen promptable concept segmenter (SAM3), prompted by the bare novel class names and lifted from posed RGB views, and reconcile the two by cross-view consistency: a point becomes novel only when enough of the views that see it agree. On the ScanNet200 GFS-PCS benchmark this fully training-free, open-vocabulary pipeline improves novel mIoU by +2.6 over the training-free dense prior while holding base accuracy within 0.5, and recovers a third (33%) of the novel-class gap to the trained state of the art that uses far more supervision. We further show that injecting the few-shot support into the pipeline, as a fusion gate and as a prototypical dense classifier, adds nothing over consistency alone and in fact degrades it through the classifier, which is why the method needs no support at all. On the harder ScanNet++ benchmark, where the dense prior is far weaker on novel classes, the same pipeline nearly doubles novel mIoU (+15.7, from 16.2 to 31.9) at a 1.7 base cost, lifting the harmonic mean from 21.5 to 31.1

</details>


### [6] [Reasoning-Guided Part-Level Visual Grounding via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15374)
*Kazi Sajeed Mehrab,Hani Alomari,Najibul Haque Sarker,Chia-Wei Tang,Zaber Ibn Abdul Hakim,Anuj Karpatne,Chris Thomas*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MLLMs struggle with grounding object parts; OP-HRG solves this via coarse-to-fine hierarchical reasoning with self-check and part-aware GRPO training, achieving SOTA results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MLLMs ground whole objects well but fail when queries name object parts due to missing object-part hierarchy—both are localized in the same single step.

Method: Proposes Object-Part Hierarchical Reflective Grounding (OP-HRG): a coarse-to-fine pipeline that first localizes the parent object, then the part within it, followed by a self-check that re-encodes the predicted crop for correction. Trained with a part-aware GRPO framework using stage-wise rewards.

Result: A 4B model outperforms 7B grounding LLMs and SAM3 on PascalPart, PartImageNet, and InstructPart, and transfers to reasoning segmentation.

Conclusion: Introducing explicit object-part hierarchy and reflective self-checking significantly improves part-level grounding in MLLMs without scaling model size.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) ground whole objects well from free-form language queries, but they struggle when the query names a part rather than the object. We trace this to a missing object-part hierarchy, since parts are localized in the same single step used for objects. We propose Object-Part Hierarchical Reflective Grounding (OP-HRG), a coarse-to-fine reasoning-guided grounding strategy that first localizes the parent object and then the part within it. A self-check then reflects on the result, with an extension to re-encode the predicted crop to inspect the region it is correcting. We introduce a part-aware GRPO framework to train our pipeline with stage-wise rewards. A 4B model trained this way outperforms 7B grounding LLMs and SAM3 across PascalPart, PartImageNet, and InstructPart, and transfers to reasoning segmentation.

</details>


### [7] [Debiasing Text-to-Image Evaluation via Implicit Cultural Alignment Reward Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15740)
*Bo-An Chang,Yu-Chih Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces an Implicit Cultural Alignment Reward Model (4.2B parameters) that uses an Implicit Cultural Probe and Skip-connection Cross-Attention (SkipCA) to evaluate cultural authenticity in T2I outputs. It achieves 80.54% pairwise accuracy on the CulturalFrames benchmark and runs 10× faster (0.21s per evaluation) than VQA-based methods by avoiding autoregressive text generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing T2I evaluation metrics underrepresent implicit cultural norms, leading to biased preference judgments, and VQA-based evaluators suffer from scalability issues due to autoregressive text generation.

Method: A lightweight 4.2B-parameter MLLM with an Implicit Cultural Probe and Skip-connection Cross-Attention (SkipCA) mechanism that allows late-stage semantic features to attend to early-stage visual representations, preserving culturally salient details.

Result: 80.54% pairwise accuracy, Pearson r=0.546, Kendall τ=0.377 on 3,323 image pairs from CulturalFrames; 0.21s per evaluation (10× speedup over VQA-based evaluators).

Conclusion: The proposed reward model provides an efficient, culturally aware scalar signal suitable for preference optimization pipelines like RLHF and DPO.

Abstract: As Text-to-Image (T2I) systems rapidly advance, evaluating the cultural authenticity of synthesized content has become increasingly important for fair and trustworthy generative AI. Existing T2I evaluation metrics and multimodal judges often rely on visual-semantic representations that underrepresent implicit cultural norms, leading to biased preference judgments and the omission of fine-grained cultural cues. In addition, visual question answering (VQA)-based evaluators typically depend on autoregressive text generation, which limits their scalability for real-time reward modeling. To address these limitations, we introduce an Implicit Cultural Alignment Reward Model built upon a lightweight 4.2-billion-parameter Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM). Our framework integrates an Implicit Cultural Probe with a Skip-connection Cross-Attention (SkipCA) mechanism, enabling late-stage semantic features to directly attend to early-stage visual representations and better preserve culturally salient details. Evaluations on 3,323 challenging and carefully curated image pairs from the CulturalFrames benchmark show that our approach achieves 80.54% pairwise accuracy, with Pearson and Kendall correlation coefficients of 0.546 and 0.377, respectively, outperforming representative vision-language metrics and MLLM-based evaluators. Moreover, by bypassing autoregressive text generation, our model processes each evaluation in 0.21 seconds under our local inference setup, achieving a $10\times$ speedup over standard VQA-based evaluators. These results suggest that the proposed reward model can provide an efficient and culturally aware scalar signal for preference optimization pipelines such as Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback and Direct Preference Optimization.

</details>


### [8] [Partial Information Decomposition as a Multi-Contrast 3D MRI Selection Strategy for Resource-Constrained Deep Neural Network Training in Brain Tumor Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15396)
*Agamdeep Chopra,Mehmet Kurt*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A Partial Information Decomposition (PID) framework pre-selects the most informative MRI sequence pair (T1c+T2-FLAIR) for 3D brain tumor segmentation, achieving near-full-input performance with significantly reduced computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-contrast 3D MRI segmentation is computationally demanding when using all available sequences; there is a need to identify compact, informative input subsets before costly model development.

Method: A pre-training Partial Information Decomposition (PID) framework ranks input pairs by redundant, unique, and synergistic information about regional tumor burden, selecting the highest-ranked pair (T1c+T2-FLAIR). Eleven architecturally identical lightweight 3D U-Nets were trained with different input configurations and evaluated on an independent test cohort. Independent Shapley analysis validated the findings.

Result: T1c+T2-FLAIR was the strongest two-input configuration (mean Dice 0.676) and ranked second overall, marginally behind the full four-input model (0.687). Shapley analysis confirmed T2-FLAIR and T1c as the most influential inputs with the strongest pairwise interaction.

Conclusion: PID-based pre-training selection has practical value for identifying compact, informative MRI input sets before expensive 3D model training, enabling efficient segmentation with minimal performance loss.

Abstract: Multi-contrast 3D MRI segmentation can be computationally demanding when all available sequences are used. We evaluate a pre-training Partial Information Decomposition framework that ranks input pairs according to their redundant, unique, and synergistic information about regional tumor burden and selects the highest-ranked pair for downstream training. Applied to T1n, T1c, T2w, and T2-FLAIR MRI, the framework selected T1c+T2-FLAIR. We then trained eleven architecturally identical lightweight 3D U-Nets using different input configurations. On an independent test cohort, T1c+T2-FLAIR was the strongest two-input configuration and ranked second overall in mean Dice (0.676 versus 0.687 for all four inputs). Independent Shapley analysis on the full-input model also identified T2-FLAIR and T1c as the most influential inputs and their pairwise interaction as the strongest. These findings demonstrate the practical value of PID based pre-training selection for identifying compact, informative MRI input sets before costly 3D model development.

</details>


### [9] [Toward Semantic Communication for Real-time Mobile 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16128)
*Fangzhou Zhao,Yao Sun,Xuesong Liu,Runze Cheng,Shang Kai,Yi Sun*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes a SemCom framework for real-time mobile 3D reconstruction that transmits semantic information with pixel-wise confidence maps, enabling robust geometric estimation under noisy channels by integrating confidence into RANSAC and bundle adjustment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing semantic communication designs are optimized at the image or single-view level without providing explicit reliability information for geometric estimation, limiting their applicability to real-time mobile 3D reconstruction where multi-view consistency is critical and communication-induced distortions degrade performance.

Method: A semantic communication framework with a semantic transceiver that outputs reconstructed images alongside pixel-wise confidence maps; a confidence-guided geometric estimation method integrating confidence into RANSAC-based pose initialization and bundle adjustment to mitigate unreliable regions.

Result: The framework maintains high image quality while significantly improving pose estimation accuracy and 3D structural consistency compared to existing SemCom and traditional separate source and channel coding approaches under noisy channels.

Conclusion: Providing explicit pixel-wise confidence information alongside semantic communication enables more robust real-time mobile 3D reconstruction, effectively reducing sensitivity to communication-induced distortions.

Abstract: Real-time mobile 3D reconstruction is fundamental to many emerging applications such as autonomous navigation and digital twin construction, where a moving platform continuously captures an image stream and transmit to a computing server for scene understanding. Unlike offline reconstruction, camera poses and scene geometry are estimated on-the-fly during acquisition, making multi-view consistency a real-time requirement and rendering geometric estimation highly sensitive to communication-induced distortions. Semantic communication (SemCom) transmits compact semantic information, offering a promising way to preserve task-critical data over unreliable links. However, existing designs are optimized at the image or single-view level and without providing explicit reliability information for geometric estimation, limiting their applicability to real-time mobile 3D reconstruction. In this context, we propose a SemCom framework for real-time mobile 3D reconstruction. The framework includes a semantic transceiver that outputs a reconstructed image alongside a pixel-wise confidence map, quantifying the reliability of each region. We further introduce a confidence-guided geometric estimation method, incorporating confidence into RANSAC-based pose initialization and bundle adjustment to reduce the influence of unreliable regions and enhance robustness under noisy channels. Simulations show that, compared to existing SemCom and traditional seperate source and channel coding, our framework maintains high image quality while significantly improving pose estimation accuracy and 3D structural consistency.

</details>


### [10] [Unsupervised Keypoints for Real-Time Fall Detection: Comparative Analysis Under Real-world Conditions with Predictive Bandwidth Reduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15400)
*Tasmiah Haque,Jacob Kosinski,Sumit Mohan,Srinjoy Das,Mohammad Abdullah Al-Mamun*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a privacy-preserving fall detection framework using unsupervised keypoints and temporal predictive modeling, finding that unsupervised keypoints outperform supervised ones under occlusion and partial body visibility conditions, especially under bandwidth constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Falls among older adults pose a major safety challenge, but continuous monitoring is difficult due to privacy, computation, and bandwidth limitations. Video captures useful motion data but deployment is limited, and supervised pose estimation is vulnerable to occlusion and partial body visibility.

Method: A privacy-preserving framework that replaces RGB transmission with compact motion representations based on unsupervised keypoints and variational recurrent prediction. Local processing performs segmentation and keypoint extraction, then variational recurrent prediction and sequence classification detect falls from observed and forecasted motion. Evaluated on UR Fall Detection and Human Fall datasets using random, subject-disjoint, and occlusion-based splits.

Result: Under random splits, no consistent dominance. Under subject-disjoint splits, supervised keypoints show statistically significant advantage when landmarks are visible, but unsupervised keypoints are more robust to occlusion. Under occlusion-based splits, supervised keypoints miss nearly half of all falls while unsupervised keypoints retain strong sensitivity. The gap widens under bandwidth constraints.

Conclusion: Representation choice should reflect expected visual conditions; unsupervised keypoints offer a distinct advantage when body visibility is compromised, while supervised keypoints perform better when anatomical landmarks are clearly visible.

Abstract: Falls among older adults are a major safety challenge, but continuous monitoring is difficult to sustain. Video captures fall-related posture and motion, yet deployment is limited by privacy, computation, and bandwidth. Supervised pose estimation is anatomically interpretable but vulnerable to occlusion and partial body visibility. We propose a privacy-preserving framework that replaces RGB transmission with compact motion representations based on unsupervised keypoints and predictive temporal modeling. Local processing performs segmentation and keypoint extraction; variational recurrent prediction and sequence classification then detect falls from observed and forecasted motion. We evaluate the framework on the UR Fall Detection and Human Fall datasets using random, subject-disjoint, and occlusion-based splits. Under random splits, neither representation consistently dominates, suggesting that standard protocols may hide meaningful differences. Under subject-disjoint evaluation, supervised keypoints show a statistically significant advantage, but performance varies by subject: they perform better when anatomical landmarks are visible, whereas unsupervised keypoints are more robust to occlusion and partial visibility, though they produce more false positives for complex activities. Under occlusion-based evaluation, supervised keypoints miss nearly half of all falls, while unsupervised keypoints retain strong sensitivity and substantially outperform them. Their anatomical independence allows spatial anchors to adapt to visible body structure rather than fail on absent landmarks. The gap widens under bandwidth constraints, where supervised localization errors compound through the temporal model. These findings show that representation choice should reflect expected visual conditions and that unsupervised keypoints offer an advantage when body visibility is compromised.

</details>


### [11] [GS-RealBlur: A Flexible Data Acquisition Framework for Real-World Image Deblurring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15401)
*Mingyang Chen,Zhilu Zhang,Honglei Xu,Renlong Wu,Xiaohe Wu,Wangmeng Zuo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GS-RealBlur is a data acquisition framework that uses handheld cameras for blurry images and gimbals for sharp images, reconstructs 3D sharp scene representations, and employs Blur-aware Pose Refinement to align rendered sharp images with blurry counterparts, producing a high-quality deblurring dataset that enables superior model generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing paired data for image deblurring suffers from a trade-off: synthetic blur lacks realism, while real-world captured pairs require complex and inflexible camera systems. There is a need for a method that achieves both blur realism and acquisition flexibility.

Method: GS-RealBlur captures blurry images with a handheld camera and dense sharp images of the same scene using a gimbal. It reconstructs a 3D representation of sharp images and calibrates the camera pose of each blurry frame within this 3D representation. The sharp counterpart is rendered from the 3D according to the pose. A Blur-aware Pose Refinement (BPR) module refines poses using appearance consistency and centroid alignment constraints to better align rendered and blurry images.

Result: A high-quality, diverse deblurring dataset was constructed. Deblurring models trained on this dataset achieve superior generalization performance across various real-world deblurring benchmarks, consistently outperforming models trained on existing synthetic and real-world datasets.

Conclusion: GS-RealBlur effectively resolves the realism-vs-flexibility dilemma in deblurring data acquisition, offering a practical framework for generating high-quality paired data that leads to better real-world deblurring performance.

Abstract: High-quality, large-scale paired data is essential for training learning-based image deblurring models. However, synthetic blurry images generally lack realism, while real-world captured images require complex and inflexible camera systems. In this work, we propose GS-RealBlur, a data acquisition framework for real-world image deblurring, achieving both blur realism and acquisition flexibility. Specifically, we use a handheld camera to capture blurry images, and deploy a gimbal to densely capture sharp images of the same scene. We reconstruct the 3D representation of sharp images and calibrate the camera pose of each blurry frame within this 3D. The image rendered from this 3D according to the pose serves as the sharp counterpart. To better align the rendered image with the blurry image, we introduce a Blur-aware Pose Refinement (BPR) module that refines the pose using appearance consistency and centroid alignment constraints. Leveraging GS-RealBlur, we construct a high-quality and diverse dataset. Extensive experiments demonstrate that a deblurring model trained on our dataset achieves superior generalization performance across various real-world deblurring benchmarks, consistently outperforming models trained on existing synthetic and real-world datasets. The code and dataset will be made publicly available.

</details>


### [12] [Explicit Over Implicit: Enhancing CNNs Via Complex Structure Tensor Representations for Periocular Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15410)
*Kevin Hernandez-Diaz,Josef Bigun,Fernando Alonso-Fernandez*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that CNNs struggle with orientation features, and using Complex Structure Tensor (compact orientation features with certainties) as input significantly improves identification accuracy. Mini-complex convnets with reduced CNN sizes outperformed full-fledged architectures. Experiments on periocular datasets (Cross-Eyed and PolyU) showed 5-26% EER reduction in both Open-World and Close-World scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CNNs have limitations in extracting orientation features effectively, and the authors aim to mitigate this by incorporating explicit orientation priors inspired by mammalian vision, enhancing explainability and suitability for thin-clients.

Method: The paper uses Complex Structure Tensor containing compact orientation features with certainties as input to CNNs. Mini-complex convnets provide these inputs, combined with reduced CNN sizes, tested on six CNN architectures. Experiments used periocular images from Cross-Eyed and PolyU datasets.

Result: Complex Structure Tensor inputs consistently improved identification accuracy over grayscale inputs. Mini-complex convnets with reduced CNN sizes outperformed prevailing full-fledged CNN architectures. A 5-26% reduction in Equal Error Rate (EER) was achieved on both datasets.

Conclusion: Explicit orientation priors mitigate CNN representational limits in both Open-World and Close-World scenarios. The strategy of upfront orientation feature extraction, as seen in mammalian vision, enhances CNN performance, explainability, and relevance for thin-client applications.

Abstract: Our study provides evidence that CNNs struggle to extract orientation features effectively. We show that using the Complex Structure Tensor, which contains compact orientation features with certainties, as input to CNNs consistently improves identification accuracy compared to grayscale inputs alone. Experiments also demonstrated that our inputs, provided by mini-complex convnets, combined with reduced CNN sizes, outperformed full-fledged, prevailing CNN architectures. This suggests that the upfront use of orientation features in CNNs, a strategy seen in mammalian vision, not only mitigates their limitations but also enhances their explainability and relevance to thin-clients. Experiments were conducted on publicly available datasets comprising periocular images (Cross-Eyed and PolyU) for biometric identification and verification in both Close-World and Open-World Scenarios using six CNN architectures. Our experiments on the Cross-Eyed and PolyU datasets yield a 5-26% reduction in EER, providing strong empirical evidence that explicit orientation priors mitigate CNN representational limits in Open-World and Close-World scenarios.

</details>


### [13] [Dataset-Origin Signatures and Shortcut Learning in Screening Mammography AI: A Cross-Dataset Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15416)
*Parham Hajishafiezahramini,Matthew Hamilton,Oscar Meruvia-Pastor,Edward Kendall*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Adding abnormal-enriched external mammography datasets to a screening dataset (NLBSD) consistently degraded model performance (AUC-ROC dropped from 0.737 to 0.620-0.644) due to domain shift, as confirmed by a dataset-of-origin classification task that showed near-perfect separation between datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether supplementing screening mammography data (low cancer prevalence, subtle abnormalities) with biopsy-confirmed cases from abnormal-enriched external datasets improves AI performance for breast cancer screening.

Method: Used EfficientNet-B5 encoder with Mammo-CLIP weights as a frozen linear probe; trained on NLBSD alone vs. NLBSD supplemented with CBIS-DDSM and CMMD; consistent preprocessing and patient-level splits; also framed a diagnostic task predicting dataset of origin.

Result: NLBSD-only model achieved AUC-ROC 0.737; adding external positive cases reduced AUC-ROC to 0.620-0.644 (significant degradation, p<0.05). Dataset-of-origin classification achieved near-perfect separation, confirming domain shift.

Conclusion: Naively pooling abnormal-enriched mammography datasets introduces domain shift that outweighs the benefit of additional positive cases. Differences in acquisition, intensity mapping, and dataset construction persist after normalization, motivating domain-aware strategies.

Abstract: Reliable AI for screening mammography requires training data representative of the low cancer prevalence and subtle abnormalities found in screening populations. We examined whether supplementing such data with biopsy-confirmed cases from abnormal-enriched external datasets improves performance. Using the Newfoundland and Labrador Breast Screening Dataset (NLBSD) alongside CBIS-DDSM and CMMD, we evaluated an EfficientNet-B5 encoder initialized with Mammo-CLIP weights as a frozen linear probe under consistent preprocessing and patient-level splits.
  The NLBSD-only model achieved an AUC-ROC of 0.737 (95% CI [0.686, 0.785]). Adding external positive cases reduced performance in every configuration (AUC-ROC = 0.620--0.644; DeLong test, Holm-corrected $p < 0.05$), with degradation increasing as additional sources were introduced. Domain-matched evaluation produced modest gains only when the training and test domains coincided, and no configuration surpassed the NLBSD-only model.
  As a diagnostic, we reframed the task as predicting each examination's dataset of origin. The datasets were separated almost perfectly despite identical preprocessing, indicating that dataset-specific characteristics strongly influence the learned representation. These findings show that naïvely pooling abnormal-enriched mammography datasets can introduce domain shift that outweighs the benefit of additional positive cases. Differences in acquisition, intensity mapping, and dataset construction persist after normalization, motivating domain-aware strategies for combining heterogeneous mammography datasets.

</details>


### [14] [Trajectory-aware Cross-view Geo-localization with Sequential Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15491)
*Tianyi Gao,Jiayu Lin,Danielle Beaulieu,Nathan Jacobs*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SeqGeo-VL dataset (~39K video-text-satellite triplets) and TrajLoc framework unify video clips and route descriptions for cross-view geo-localization, with TrajMod module for geometry-aware embeddings, achieving SOTA gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cross-view geo-localization methods overlook route descriptions as a complementary sequential modality to video, despite their practical value when only abstract trajectory descriptions are available (e.g., user directions to autonomous vehicles).

Method: Proposes SeqGeo-VL dataset with 39K video-text-satellite triplets; TrajLoc framework processes both video clips and route descriptions via dense visual and abstract linguistic semantics; TrajMod module conditions query embeddings on trajectory geometry for spatially-aware representations.

Result: TrajLoc achieves substantial gains over state-of-the-art methods on both video and text geo-localization.

Conclusion: Leveraging both visual and linguistic sequential modalities with trajectory geometry awareness improves cross-view geo-localization performance.

Abstract: Cross-view geo-localization matches ground-level observations against geo-tagged satellite imagery. Recent methods show that sequential queries such as video clips yield richer spatiotemporal cues than single images, yet they overlook a complementary sequential modality: route descriptions -- which capture the same trajectory at a higher level of abstraction and are often the only input available (e.g., a user directing an autonomous vehicle to a pickup point). To bridge this gap, we introduce SeqGeo-VL, a dataset of $\sim$39K video-text-satellite triplets, and TrajLoc, a unified framework capable of processing both video clips and route descriptions. By leveraging both dense visual and abstract linguistic semantics, TrajLoc enables these modalities to mutually reinforce cross-view matching. We further propose TrajMod, a lightweight module that conditions query embeddings on trajectory geometry, yielding spatially-aware representations. Experiments show that TrajLoc achieves substantial gains over state-of-the-art methods on both video and text geo-localization. The project page is available at https://humblegamer.github.io/trajloc/.

</details>


### [15] [LLM-Driven AutoML for Cross-Lingual Handwritten OCR: Closed-Loop Neural Architecture Search with GPT-5, GPT-4o, and Claude Sonnet 4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15509)
*Mobina Kashaniyan,Amirhossein Ghassemi,Nasser Mozayani*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: An AutoML framework using GPT-5, GPT-4o, and Claude Sonnet 4 as autonomous neural architecture designers for cross-lingual handwriting recognition achieves >93% mean accuracy (98.1% best) and 41-44ms inference latency across Arabic, Persian, and English datasets without manual design or tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To eliminate manual neural architecture design, domain-specific preprocessing, and hyperparameter tuning in cross-lingual handwriting recognition by leveraging large language models as automated neural architecture search agents.

Method: A closed-loop AutoML framework where GPT-5, GPT-4o, and Claude Sonnet 4 independently generate, train, evaluate, and iteratively refine neural network architectures using performance feedback from previous trials across 270 experiments on Arabic, Persian, and English handwriting datasets.

Result: Mean test accuracies above 93%, best accuracy of 98.1%, inference latency between 41-44 milliseconds across all three languages without manual intervention.

Conclusion: Large language models can function as effective AutoML agents for neural architecture search, enabling scalable, script-adaptive, and reproducible handwriting recognition across different languages.

Abstract: We present a fully automated closed-loop AutoML framework that uses GPT-5, GPT-4o, and Claude Sonnet 4 as autonomous neural architecture designers for cross-lingual handwritten optical character recognition. Each large language model independently generates, trains, evaluates, and iteratively refines neural network architectures using performance feedback from previous trials. The framework is evaluated on Arabic, Persian, and English handwriting datasets through 270 independent experiments. It consistently discovers accurate and computationally efficient models without manual architecture design, domain-specific preprocessing, or hyperparameter tuning. The generated models achieve mean test accuracies above 93 percent, a best accuracy of 98.1 percent, and inference latency between 41 and 44 milliseconds. The results demonstrate that large language models can function as effective AutoML agents for neural architecture search, enabling scalable, script-adaptive, and reproducible handwriting recognition across languages.

</details>


### [16] [Intentional Electromagnetic Interference Attacks on Facial Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15512)
*Tyler Fitzsimmons,Adam Czajka*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that intentional electromagnetic interference (EMI) using commonly accessible RF equipment can compromise facial recognition systems by attacking the physical-to-digital pipeline of biometric sensors, and provides a standardized benchmark dataset for testing face matchers against such RF-based hardware attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While digital attacks on computer vision algorithms are common, physical presentation attacks (e.g., masks, photos) dominate biometric security concerns. The vulnerability in the physical-to-digital pipeline of biometric sensors—specifically via electromagnetic interference—remains underexplored and poses significant risk to facial recognition systems.

Method: The authors (a) conduct intentional EMI using commonly accessible RF equipment to interfere with biometric sensors, (b) assess robustness of state-of-the-art face recognition methods against such RF-based attacks, and (c) create a dataset of face images captured with and without EMI to serve as a benchmark for testing face matchers.

Result: Demonstrated that EMI-based hardware attacks are feasible using readily available RF equipment, and provided a standardized dataset for evaluating face recognition robustness against such interference, potentially complementing ISO/IEC 30107 presentation attack definitions.

Conclusion: EMI-based attacks represent a critical and overlooked vulnerability in biometric facial recognition pipelines, and the proposed standardized approach and benchmark dataset enable systematic testing of face matcher robustness against hardware-level RF interference.

Abstract: Attacks on general computer vision algorithms are often relegated to the digital domain, with the optimization performed purely in the digital world and then translated to physical mediums for implementation. In the field of biometrics, including facial recognition, physical presentation attacks targeting biometric sensors are dominant and present significant opportunity and risk. This paper highlights a critical vulnerability in the physical-to-digital pipeline of biometric sensors and provides a standardized approach for testing facial recognition system robustness against hardware attacks, going beyond and potentially complementing presentation attacks (as defined in ISO/IEC 30107 standard series). Specifically, in this work we (a) demonstrate that intentional electromagnetic interference is possible to be conducted with commonly accessible radio frequency (RF) equipment, (b) assess the robustness of state-of-the-art face recognition methods against RF-based attacks, and (c) provide a dataset composed of face images captured with and without electromagnetic interference to serve as a new benchmark for testing modern face matchers against RF-sourced interference.

</details>


### [17] [SLAPBench: Benchmarking Multimodal Large Language Models for Four-Finger SLAP Fingerprint Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15517)
*Bibesh Pyakurel,M. G. Sarwar Murshed*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SLAPBench is the first benchmark for evaluating MLLMs on four-finger SLAP fingerprint identity verification. Using NIST SD302b (7,832 pairs), it tests 5 MLLMs under different prompting strategies. Prompting governs collapse behavior; similarity scoring prevents collapse and reveals capability gaps. Qwen3-VL-8B achieves AUC=1.000, attributed to near-duplicate detection rather than genuine capability. Claude Opus 4.8 is most robust under binary prompts (FAR=20.2%). Fairness disparities appear as discrimination weakens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: No existing benchmark evaluates whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can verify identity from four-finger SLAP fingerprint images, despite SLAP fingerprints being used in border control and law enforcement.

Method: Built SLAPBench from NIST SD302b (7,832 pairs: 176 mated, 7,656 non-mated). Evaluated four open-source MLLMs (InternVL3-8B, Qwen2.5-VL-7B, Qwen3-VL-8B, Gemma-3-12B) and proprietary Claude Opus 4.8 under zero-shot, task-description, and similarity-scoring prompts. Conducted matched-resolution control and fairness probe over gender, race, and age.

Result: Task-description prompting collapsed open-source models to near-100% FAR; Claude Opus 4.8 resisted collapse (FAR=20.2%). Similarity scoring removed collapse: Claude AUC=0.953, Gemma-3-12B 0.837, InternVL3-8B inverted (0.590), Qwen2.5-VL-7B near random (0.567), Qwen3-VL-8B perfect separation (AUC=1.000). Perfect score persisted under matched-resolution control, ruling out resolution shortcut but not near-duplicate detection. Fairness disparities grow as discrimination weakens.

Conclusion: SLAPBench establishes the first SLAP-specific MLLM baseline. Prompting governs collapse behavior while model capability governs discrimination. Qwen3-VL-8B's perfect AUC is a diagnostic artifact of near-duplicate detection in SD302b, not genuine verification capability.

Abstract: Four-finger SLAP fingerprints are flat live-scan impressions of the index, middle, ring, and little fingers of one hand, used for identity verification in border control and law enforcement. No benchmark has evaluated whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can verify identity from SLAP images. We introduce SLAPBench, the first benchmark for MLLM-based four-finger SLAP fingerprint verification, built from NIST SD302b with 7,832 pairs (176 mated, 7,656 non-mated). We evaluate four open-source MLLMs (InternVL3-8B, Qwen2.5-VL-7B, Qwen3-VL-8B, Gemma-3-12B) and the proprietary Claude Opus 4.8 under zero-shot, task-description, and similarity-scoring prompts.
  Prompting governs verification behavior. Task-description prompting collapses all four open-source models to near-100% False Accept Rate (FAR), and Gemma-3-12B collapses under zero-shot as well; Claude Opus 4.8 alone resists collapse under both binary prompts, giving the best binary result (FAR = 20.2%). Similarity scoring removes collapse across the open-source models and exposes wide capability gaps: Claude reaches AUC = 0.953 and Gemma-3-12B 0.837, while InternVL3-8B is inverted (AUC = 0.590) and Qwen2.5-VL-7B near random (0.567). Qwen3-VL-8B attains perfect separation (AUC = 1.000), which we treat as a diagnostic rather than as capability: SD302b holds one SLAP capture per finger position, so mated pairs are cross-resolution. A matched-resolution control leaves the perfect score intact, ruling out the resolution shortcut; what cannot be excluded within SD302b is near-duplicate detection, since a mated pair is one capture rendered twice. A fairness probe over gender, race, and age suggests disparity grows as discrimination weakens. SLAPBench establishes the first SLAP-specific MLLM baseline and shows that prompting governs collapse while model capability governs discrimination.

</details>


### [18] [Physics-aware Masked Diffusion-based Flood Simulation for Urban Fisheye Disaster Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15527)
*Sodtavilan Odonchimed,Tsogt Enkhbayar,Oyunzul Munkhtamga,Munkhjargal Gochoo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PhysFlood is a physical simulation system using Diffusion Models to synthesize realistic flood scenarios from a single fisheye-lens image, with controllable water levels and other physical variables, enabling urban disaster simulation and anomaly detection model development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Severe shortage of real-world flood data and inherent distortions of fisheye lens images make high-precision urban flood simulation challenging, hindering disaster prevention and anomaly detection.

Method: A physical simulation system (PhysFlood) leveraging Diffusion Models to synthesize realistic floods from a single fisheye-lens image, with free control over physically meaningful variables like water levels to generate diverse flood scenarios.

Result: Qualitative human study demonstrated that simulation images generated by PhysFlood exhibit acceptable realism and robustness.

Conclusion: PhysFlood enables high-precision flood simulation from a single fisheye image with controllable physical variables, addressing data scarcity and image distortion challenges in urban disaster simulation.

Abstract: Physical simulations that predict the behavior of urban disasters, such as climate-related flooding, play a crucial role in disaster prevention and the development of anomaly detection models. However, the severe shortage of flood data in real-world environments, combined with the inherent distortions of fisheye lens images, which are used for urban surveillance, has made high-precision simulations challenging. To address this, we propose a new physical simulation system PhysFlood that leverages Diffusion Models to synthesize realistic floods from just a single image captured by a fisheye lens. Our system not only enables simulation from a single image, but also features the ability to freely control and generate diverse flood scenarios by manipulating physically meaningful variables, such as water levels. In our evaluation experiments, we conducted a qualitative human study and demonstrated that the simulation images generated by PhysFlood exhibit both acceptable realism and robustness.

</details>


### [19] [E3DGS: Unified Geometric-Photometric Equivariance for 3D Gaussian Splatting via Color-as-Geometry Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15536)
*Chankyo Kim,Maani Ghaffari*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: 3D Gaussian Splatting has a representation bottleneck when trying to achieve SE(3)-equivariance because color (spherical harmonics) transforms differently from geometry. The authors prove that for SH degrees ≤2, photometry is isomorphic to a rank-2 geometric tensor, allowing them to reformulate the Wigner-D action as matrix conjugation. This leads to E3DGS, a unified SE(3)-equivariant architecture that maps all Gaussian attributes into a gl(3) carrier space without Clebsch-Gordan products, improving robustness and data efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3DGS methods fail to maintain strict SE(3)-equivariance because color (modeled via Spherical Harmonics) transforms heterogeneously compared to geometry (position, covariance), creating a representation bottleneck. Prior works either discard or flatten SH coefficients, breaking symmetry.

Method: The authors prove that for SH degrees ℓ≤2, photometry is algebraically isomorphic to a rank-2 geometric tensor, and the Wigner-D action on SH coefficients can be reformulated as conjugation on 3×3 matrices. They introduce a Unified Matrix Embedding that lifts all Gaussian attributes into a gl(3) carrier space, enabling E3DGS—a rigid-body SE(3)-equivariant architecture that avoids Clebsch-Gordan tensor products.

Result: On object vision and action-conditioned Gaussian world modeling tasks, E3DGS achieves strong robustness under camera-frame changes and improved data efficiency compared to prior methods.

Conclusion: The "Color-as-Geometry" unification resolves the symmetry mismatch in 3DGS, enabling a principled SE(3)-equivariant architecture that is both robust to viewpoint changes and more data-efficient.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) captures scenes by coupling explicit geometry (position, covariance) with view-dependent photometry (Spherical Harmonics). However, building $\mathrm{SE}(3)$-equivariant architectures on these primitives presents a fundamental representation bottleneck. Color has been treated as a signal rather than a geometric entity, making it nontrivial to unify symmetry across geometry and appearance as the camera frame changes. While translations are handled by relative coordinates, rotations act heterogeneously across attributes: $μ\mapsto Rμ$, $Σ\mapsto RΣR^\top$, and $f_\ell\mapsto D^\ell(R)f_\ell$. This mismatch complicates strict equivariance, leading existing methods to either discard or flatten SH coefficients, thereby breaking symmetry. We propose a unified solution rooted in representation theory: for SH degrees $\ell\le2$, photometry is algebraically isomorphic to a rank-2 geometric tensor. We prove that the Wigner-$D$ action on these SH coefficients can be exactly reformulated as the conjugation action on $3\times3$ matrices. Leveraging this, we introduce the Unified Matrix Embedding, a lifting that maps all Gaussian attributes into a unified carrier space, $\mathfrak{gl}(3)$. Building on the "Color-as-Geometry" formulation, we present E3DGS, a rigid-body ($\mathrm{SE}(3)$) equivariant architecture that processes 3D Gaussians without Clebsch-Gordan tensor products. Evaluations on object vision and action-conditioned Gaussian world modeling demonstrate that our unified approach yields strong robustness under camera-frame changes and improved data efficiency.

</details>


### [20] [ImprovedVBGS: Real-time Continual Variational Bayes Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15542)
*Damani Mguni-Coker*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ImprovedVBGS accelerates on-the-fly 3D reconstruction from ~84s to ~0.05s per frame (1680x speedup) via spatially truncated variational inference and improved reassignment, enabling real-time continual learning without replay buffers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VBGS achieves continual learning via CAVI but is too slow for real-time robotics due to per-frame iterations over all observed points, violating strict memory and latency requirements.

Method: (i) Spatially truncated variational inference to localize updates, (ii) improved reassignment using forwarding and truncation while eliminating wasteful dynamic recompilation.

Result: On NeRF synthetic dataset (RTX 3070 Ti): mean per-frame latency reduced from ~84.0s to ~0.050s (1680x speedup) with maintained reconstruction quality.

Conclusion: Spatial truncation and optimized reassignment make variational Gaussian splatting practical for real-time on-the-fly reconstruction in robotics and autonomous navigation.

Abstract: On-the-fly reconstruction is a key requirement for many applications in robotics and autonomous navigation. Variational Bayes Gaussian Splatting (VBGS) enables continual learning without replay buffers using Coordinate Ascent Variational Inference (CAVI), but its per-frame iterations over all observed points make it too slow for real-time use with strict memory and latency requirements. We present ImprovedVBGS, an accelerated framework for on-the-fly continual reconstruction. This is achieved primarily through (i) spatially truncated variational inference, and (ii) improved reassignment that uses forwarding, truncation and eliminates wasteful dynamic recompilation. On the NeRF synthetic dataset, we reduce mean per-frame latency from ~84.0 s to ~0.050 s on an RTX 3070 Ti, a 1680x speed-up while maintaining reconstruction quality.

</details>


### [21] [When Can Test-Time Adaptation Help Zero-Shot CT Vision-Language Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15556)
*Ailar Mahdizadeh,Puria Azadi Moghadam,Xiangteng He,Leonid Sigal*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper studies when test-time adaptation (TTA) helps zero-shot 3D CT vision-language models (VLMs) for multi-label abnormality classification, and proposes CARVE, the first TTA method that estimates sample-specific cardinality and performs memory-efficient multi-view adaptation, showing consistent improvements when the base model is already discriminative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-shot 3D CT VLMs enable cross-institution deployment for abnormality classification, but real CT scans contain multiple co-occurring abnormalities and the reliability of multi-label prediction under distribution shift is poorly understood. No existing TTA method addresses unsupervised multi-label adaptation for zero-shot 3D CT VLMs.

Method: The authors first conduct diagnostic analysis showing TTA is conditional on preserving depth structure and base representation transferability. They then introduce CARVE (Cardinality-Aware Retained-View Entropy), which estimates sample-specific positive-label cardinality, optimizes a top-k objective to preserve co-occurring abnormalities, and performs memory-efficient multi-view adaptation by scoring weak 3D views without gradients before updating on a retained subset.

Result: Across contrastive CT-CLIP and anatomy-aware fVLM, CARVE provides the most consistent improvements across multi-label, three-class, and binary CT tasks when the base model is already discriminative.

Conclusion: Multi-label TTA for zero-shot 3D CT VLMs is established as a distinct problem, and CARVE serves as a cardinality-aware solution for this setting.

Abstract: 3D CT vision-language models (VLMs) classify abnormalities from text prompts in a zero-shot manner, enabling cross-institution deployment where labels are scarce and clinical tasks shift faster than supervised models can be retrained. A real CT scan, however, typically contains several co-occurring abnormalities, and the reliability of zero-shot multi-label prediction under distribution shift remains poorly understood. Test-time adaptation (TTA) updates a model on unlabeled target scans without source data or target annotations, yet existing TTA methods target multi-class softmax prediction on natural images or 2D medical segmentation, and none addresses unsupervised multi-label adaptation for zero-shot 3D CT VLMs. We study when TTA helps zero-shot 3D CT VLMs. A controlled diagnostic analysis shows that TTA is conditional: the volumetric input must preserve the encoder's depth structure, and the base representation must transfer to the target cohort, with depth reduction alone lowering internal AUROC by more than 0.12. We then focus on the regime where the base model already separates present from absent abnormalities. We introduce CARVE (Cardinality-Aware Retained-View Entropy), the first TTA method for this setting. CARVE estimates a sample-specific positive-label cardinality $\hat{k}$, optimizes a top-$\hat{k}$ objective to preserve co-occurring abnormalities, and performs memory-efficient multi-view adaptation by scoring weak 3D views without gradients before updating on a retained subset. Across contrastive CT-CLIP and anatomy-aware fVLM, CARVE provides the most consistent improvements across multi-label, three-class, and binary CT tasks when the base model is already discriminative. These results establish multi-label TTA for zero-shot 3D CT VLMs as a distinct problem and CARVE as a cardinality-aware solution.

</details>


### [22] [Are All Tokens Necessary for Visual Place Recognition? An Empirical Study of Token Reduction for Efficient Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15563)
*Tong Jin,Yunpeng Liu,Shuyu Hu,Qinghua Zhang,Ruize Han,Song Wang,Feng Lu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents the first systematic benchmark of token reduction techniques for efficient visual place recognition (VPR), evaluating pruning, merging, and hybrid methods across multiple VPR models and datasets, and showing that token reduction can reduce computation by 29% and improve throughput by 44% with less than 1% accuracy degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision transformer-based VPR methods achieve high accuracy but suffer from computational overhead due to processing all visual tokens throughout the network, hindering deployment in real-time and resource-constrained scenarios. The paper questions whether all visual tokens are necessary for VPR.

Method: The authors present a systematic benchmark evaluating token reduction techniques (pruning, merging, and hybrid pruning-merging) across multiple state-of-the-art VPR models and diverse benchmark datasets. They analyze recognition performance under different reduction configurations, computational complexity, inference speed, qualitative visualization, and deployment efficiency on edge devices.

Result: Token reduction can reduce computational cost by up to 29% and improve throughput by up to 44%, while incurring less than 1% degradation in recognition accuracy. Multiple important characteristics and practical insights into accuracy-efficiency trade-offs in VPR are revealed.

Conclusion: This work establishes a comprehensive foundation for future research on token-efficient VPR and efficient visual retrieval systems, with codes and models made publicly available.

Abstract: Recent visual place recognition (VPR) methods based on vision transformers, particularly foundation models, have achieved remarkable recognition performance. However, these models process all visual tokens throughout the entire network, resulting in substantial computational overhead, which hinders their deployment in real-time and resource-constrained scenarios. A natural question thus arises: are all visual tokens necessary for VPR? To answer this question, we present the first systematic benchmark of token reduction for efficient visual place recognition. Our benchmark comprehensively evaluates representative token pruning, token merging, and hybrid pruning-merging methods across multiple state-of-the-art VPR models and diverse benchmark datasets covering urban, suburban, and natural environments. We further investigate token reduction from multiple perspectives, including recognition performance under different reduction configurations, computational complexity, inference speed, qualitative visualization, and deployment efficiency on edge devices. Through extensive experiments and in-depth analysis, our benchmark reveals multiple important characteristics of token reduction in VPR and provides several practical insights into the trade-offs between accuracy and inference efficiency. For example, token reduction can reduce computational cost by up to 29\% and improve throughput by up to 44\%, while incurring less than 1\% degradation in recognition accuracy. Overall, this work establishes a comprehensive foundation for future research on token-efficient VPR and efficient visual retrieval systems. Our codes and models will be available at https://github.com/Tong-Jin01/TokenReduction4VPR

</details>


### [23] [Ask Twice, Look Twice: Prompt Echoing Resolves the Question-First Paradox in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15565)
*Rakshanda Hassan Abhinandan,John Galeotti,Deva Ramanan,Gautam Rajendrakumar Gare*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Placing the question before the image in VLM prompts steers perception but hinders answer token access; echoing the question on both sides of the image resolves this paradox without training, improving performance across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The question-first paradox: intuition suggests placing the question before the image should help VLMs know where to look, yet empirical results consistently show image-first prompting outperforms question-first, despite the latter's intuitive appeal.

Method: Logit-lens and attention probing analyses revealed that question-first prompts effectively steer image patch representations toward relevant concepts (perception benefit), but the answer token fails to attend to the question stranded behind hundreds of image tokens. A causal attention knockout confirmed this. The proposed fix is "question echoing": restating the question on both sides of the image so one copy guides perception and the other remains accessible for answer decoding.

Result: The question-first paradox costs up to 17.5 group-accuracy points across five open VLMs. Echoed prompts close this gap and surpass the best single-pass ordering on NaturalBench, POPE, Winoground, and open-ended VQAv2, achieving up to 19 Winoground group-accuracy points improvement, with no training, fine-tuning, or architecture changes.

Conclusion: The paradox reveals a fundamental trade-off between steering perception and preserving question access in VLM prompt design. Echoing resolves this trade-off purely through prompt engineering, and parallels a fifty-year-old finding on human "adjunct questions" where repeating a question before and after a passage aids comprehension.

Abstract: Where should the question go in a vision-language model (VLM) prompt: before the image or after it? Intuition says before: knowing what is asked should tell the model where to look. Yet across visual question answering benchmarks, question-first prompting consistently underperforms the image-first ordering recommended for frontier VLMs, a phenomenon we term the question-first paradox. We trace the paradox to a conflict between two stages of VLM computation. Logit-lens and attention probes show the intuition is half right: a question placed before the image genuinely steers perception, moving image patch representations toward question-relevant concepts. The failure lies downstream. Stranded behind hundreds of image tokens, the question is barely attended by the answer token, which instead commits to image-driven (often wrong) answers; a causal attention knockout confirms that the answer reads the question only when the question follows the image. The diagnosis yields a training-free fix: question echoing, restating the question on both sides of the image so that one copy steers perception while the other is read out at answer time. The same division of labor appears in a fifty-year-old finding on human ``adjunct questions'', where repeating a question before and after a passage aids comprehension more than either position alone. Echoing the image as well brings further gains, restoring the whole-image view a causal decoder otherwise loses. The paradox holds across five open VLMs, costing up to 17.5 group-accuracy points. Echoed prompts close it and surpass the best single-pass ordering on NaturalBench, POPE, Winoground, and open-ended VQAv2, by up to 19 Winoground group-accuracy points, with no training, fine-tuning, or architecture change. The paradox reveals a trade-off between steering perception and preserving question access; echoing resolves it through prompt design alone.

</details>


### [24] [Geometric Distillation from Rectified Stereo: Leveraging Epipolar Cues for Monocular Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15600)
*Jung-Hee Kim,Xiaoming Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes Epipolar Distillation (EpiDistill), a framework that transfers scale-aware geometric priors from multi-view models to monocular depth foundation models using Rectified Stereo Tokens, enabling zero-shot metric depth estimation without requiring multi-view inputs at inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monocular depth foundation models struggle with metric depth estimation due to scale ambiguity in single-view inference, while multi-view models lose their cross-view geometric benefits during single-image inference due to the absence of explicit geometric constraints.

Method: Epipolar Distillation (EpiDistill) using Rectified Stereo Tokens, which transfers epipolar attention patterns from multi-view models to single-view prediction models, enabling geometric consistency without multi-view inputs at inference.

Result: Significant improvement in zero-shot metric depth estimation, especially on challenging datasets (ETH3D, DIODE) where scale alignment is critical; consistently boosts performance of state-of-the-art ViT-based models like UniDepthV2 and DepthPro.

Conclusion: The approach is model-agnostic and effectively bridges the gap between monocular and multi-view depth estimation by transferring scale-aware geometric priors, maintaining improved metric depth estimation without requiring multi-view inputs at inference time.

Abstract: Monocular depth foundation models have demonstrated remarkable generalization capabilities across diverse environments. However, they continue to struggle with metric depth estimation in diverse environments. This limitation stems from the inherent scale ambiguity of single-view inference, leading to misaligned scale predictions even when the relative geometry is accurate. Conversely, recent multi-view foundation models leverage cross-view cues to learn robust scene-level geometry and consistent scale. Yet, these benefits typically vanish during single-image inference, as the absence of explicit geometric constraints causes performance to degrade. To bridge this gap, we propose a novel framework that transfers the scale-aware geometric priors of multi-view models into monocular depth foundation models. Specifically, we introduce an Epipolar Distillation (EpiDistill), an approach utilizing Rectified Stereo Tokens, which enables the single-view prediction model to retain epipolar attention patterns and maintain geometric consistency without requiring multi-view inputs at inference. Experimental results demonstrate that our method significantly improves zero-shot metric depth estimation, particularly on challenging datasets like ETH3D and DIODE where scale alignment is critical. Furthermore, our approach is model-agnostic, consistently boosting the performance of state-of-the-art ViT-based models, including UniDepthV2 and DepthPro.

</details>


### [25] [WREN: Low Light Image Enhancement Using Retinex theory-based Double U-Net-like Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15604)
*Reina Kaneko,Junya Hara,Hiroshi Higashi,Yuichi Tanaka*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: WREN is a retinex-based neural network for low light image enhancement that uses a double U-Net architecture (decomposition network with one encoder/two decoders + illumination enhancement network with a Transformer) and scale-invariant loss to achieve robust performance across varying dynamic ranges, achieving state-of-the-art results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing retinex-based LLIE methods suffer from non-unique decomposition leading to over-enhancement and are sensitive to varying dynamic ranges under different lighting conditions. The paper aims to make LLIE robust for various dynamic range scenes.

Method: WREN uses two U-Net-like sub-networks: (1) a decomposition network with one encoder and two decoders to separate input into reflectance and illumination maps; (2) an illumination enhancement network with a customized Transformer block between encoder and decoder. It follows retinex theory strictly, trains end-to-end with a scale-invariant loss function.

Result: The method achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple datasets for low light image enhancement.

Conclusion: WREN provides robust low light image enhancement across diverse dynamic range scenes by adhering to retinex theory with a tailored double U-Net architecture and scale-invariant loss, outperforming prior methods.

Abstract: This paper proposes a neural network for low light image enhancement (LLIE) based on retinex theory to make LLIE robust for various dynamic range scenes. The retinex theory is an image formulation model inspired by a human color perception hypothesis, where a low light image is decomposed into intrinsic color context (i.e., reflectance map) and scene-dependent illumination (i.e., illumination map). Due to non-uniqueness of its decomposition, existing retinex-based LLIE methods often fail to achieve stable decomposition, which lead to over-enhancement. Typically, they are sensitive to the dynamic ranges that vary in different lighting conditions. To tackle this issue, we propose WREN: An LLIE neural network with double U-Net-like structures. WREN consists of two U-Net-like sub-networks. The first network has one encoder and two decoders that decompose an input image into the reflectance and illumination maps. The second network with a customized Transformer block between an encoder and a decoder only enhances the illumination map obtained from the first network: This completely follows the assumption of the retinex theory. Finally, the enhanced illumination map is recombined with the reflectance map. The network is trained end-to-end with a scale-invariant loss function, which gives robustness against the illumination scaling. Numerical results show that our method achieves the state-of-the-art performance across multiple datasets. Our code is available online.

</details>


### [26] [Benchmarking MRI Representations for Deep Learning-Based Focal Cortical Dysplasia Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15605)
*Soumen Ghosh,John Phamnguyen,Amit Soni Arya,Subhojit Mandal,Tilottama Goswami,Rajat Vashistha*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study benchmarks eight MRI input configurations for automated FCD segmentation using nnU-Net, finding that a four-channel multimodal configuration (T1w+FLAIR+ratio-derived representations) achieves the best Dice score (0.376), a 5% improvement over conventional T1w+FLAIR alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FCD is a leading cause of drug-resistant focal epilepsy but its subtle and heterogeneous imaging characteristics make accurate identification and delineation on conventional MRI challenging. The contribution of different MRI representations to deep learning-based FCD segmentation is poorly understood.

Method: Using nnU-Net framework on a dataset of 85 FCD subjects and 25 healthy controls, the authors evaluated eight input configurations including T1w, FLAIR, ratio-derived representations, and multimodal combinations, with all experiments using identical preprocessing, architecture, optimization, and five-fold cross-validation.

Result: FLAIR alone achieved the strongest single-modality performance. Ratio-derived representations alone were insufficient for subtle FCD detection. The four-channel multimodal configuration (T1w+FLAIR+ratio-derived) achieved the highest Dice score (0.376), a 5.0% relative improvement over conventional T1w+FLAIR.

Conclusion: MRI representation design is an important yet underexplored component of deep learning-based FCD segmentation and should be optimized alongside network architecture.

Abstract: Focal cortical dysplasia (FCD) is one of the leading structural causes of drug-resistant focal epilepsy, yet its subtle and heterogeneous imaging characteristics make accurate identification and delineation challenging on conventional magnetic resonance imaging (MRI). Although T1-weighted (T1w) and fluid-attenuated inversion recovery (FLAIR) images are routinely acquired for presurgical evaluation, the contribution of different MRI representations to deep learning-based FCD segmentation remains poorly understood. In this study, we present a systematic benchmark of MRI representations for automated FCD segmentation using the nnU-Net framework. A publicly available presurgical MRI dataset comprising 85 FCD subjects and 25 healthy controls was used to evaluate eight input configurations, including conventional MRI contrasts (T1w and FLAIR), ratio-derived representations, and their multimodal combinations.
