Conclusion: UAV-DualCog serves both as a challenging evaluation benchmark and a useful data resource for advancing MLLM-based UAV agents, with the automated pipeline enabling scalable benchmark construction.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models have achieved strong performance across diverse vision-language tasks, yet their capabilities in UAV scenarios remain insufficiently explored. Recent UAV-oriented benchmarks have begun to evaluate MLLMs in aerial scenarios, but they typically focus on scene understanding, event recognition, or navigation completion, rather than jointly assessing the dual-cognition capability required for UAV agents: reasoning about both the UAV's own state and the external environment in multiview spatio-temporal contexts. To address this gap, we present UAV-DualCog, a benchmark for aerial multiview spatio-temporal reasoning built on this dual-cognition perspective. UAV-DualCog includes both image and video tasks to jointly evaluate self-state and environment-state reasoning, while requiring spatial or temporal grounding beyond discrete answer prediction. We also develop an automated pipeline that constructs data from scene-level semantic point clouds, yielding a scalable benchmark with diverse scenes, hundreds of landmarks, and thousands of QA samples. Extensive evaluations show that current MLLMs remain far from reliable in UAV dual cognition. Self-state reasoning, viewpoint transformation, precise spatial grounding, and temporal interval localization are persistent bottlenecks, and additional validation with thinking/frontier models and a human baseline confirms that the benchmark is understandable to humans but challenging for existing models. We further construct UAV-DualCog-Train from disjoint scenes and show through a lightweight optimization probe that it provides useful structured supervision, suggesting its value not only as an evaluation benchmark but also as a data resource for advancing MLLM-based UAV agents. Project website and supplementary materials: https://uav-dualcog.lozumi.com

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [77] [Estimating the Reliability of Dynamic Time Warping Alignments Using Circumstantial Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15443)
*Aanya Pratapneni,Alice Yuan,TJ Tsai*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: The paper proposes an unsupervised reliability metric for DTW alignment paths by comparing original DTW paths with those obtained from FlexDTW under relaxed boundary conditions, achieving 0.97 AUROC on audio-audio alignment tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing differentiable DTW variants (e.g., Soft-DTW) handle uncertainty but do not provide localized reliability estimates for alignment paths. The authors aim to develop an unsupervised method to assess how trustworthy each segment of a DTW alignment path is.

Method: For a given DTW alignment path, the reliability metric is computed by: (1) selecting a local segment of the path, (2) re-estimating the alignment using FlexDTW (which allows flexible boundary conditions), and (3) measuring the agreement between the original DTW path and the FlexDTW path. High agreement indicates a prominent/reliable path; low agreement indicates a weak/unreliable path.

Result: The reliability metric correctly identifies reliable vs. unreliable regions of DTW alignment paths with an aggregate AUROC of 0.97 across various scenarios in an audio-audio alignment task.

Conclusion: The proposed metric provides an effective unsupervised approach for estimating local reliability of DTW alignment paths, with strong discriminative performance between matching and non-matching regions.

Abstract: Recent works have explored ways to handle uncertainty in dynamic time warping (DTW) alignment paths through the use of differentiable variants of DTW like Soft-DTW. In this paper, we approach the issue of uncertainty in DTW alignment paths in a different way. Given a DTW alignment path, we propose a metric that indicates how reliable a local segment of the alignment path is. The intuition for our metric is based on the idea of circumstantial evidence. If DTW has found a very prominent path, then if we re-run the alignment with relaxed boundary conditions, it will still pick the same path. If, on the other hand, DTW has found a "weak" path, then re-running the alignment with relaxed boundary conditions will likely yield a different path. Accordingly, our reliability metric is computed by picking a local section of the DTW alignment path, re-estimating the alignment with FlexDTW (which allows flexibility in the boundary conditions), and then measuring how well the DTW and FlexDTW paths agree. We assess the proposed reliability metric on DTW alignment paths containing both matching and non-matching regions across a range of scenarios on an audio-audio alignment task. We find that the reliability metric correctly identifies reliable regions of the alignment path with an aggregate AUROC of 0.97. This approach provides an unsupervised method for estimating the reliability of a DTW alignment path.

</details>


### [78] [Segmental DTW: A Parallelizable Alternative to Dynamic Time Warping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15475)
*TJ Tsai*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: The paper proposes Segmental DTW, a parallelizable alternative to Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) for globally aligning two feature sequences. By breaking the cost matrix into sub-matrices, performing local DTW, and solving a segment-level DP, the method achieves near-DTW accuracy on audio alignment (Chopin Mazurka dataset) while enabling massive parallelization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DTW's quadratic O(N²) computation and memory cost limits scalability; the authors aim to exploit abundant parallel computation resources to approximate DTW with a parallelizable algorithm, rather than reducing total computation.

Method: Two variants of Segmental DTW: (1) partition the global cost matrix into smaller sub-matrices, (2) compute subsequence DTW on each sub-matrix independently (parallelizable), (3) solve a segment-level dynamic programming problem to determine the globally optimal alignment path.

Result: Evaluated on audio-audio alignment with the Chopin Mazurka dataset; both variants closely match regular DTW performance. Almost all computations are parallelizable. One variant is shown to be unilaterally superior both empirically and theoretically.

Conclusion: Segmental DTW offers a practical, parallelizable approximation to DTW that retains alignment quality while overcoming the sequential bottleneck, with one variant clearly outperforming the other.

Abstract: In this work we explore parallelizable alternatives to DTW for globally aligning two feature sequences. One of the main practical limitations of DTW is its quadratic computation and memory cost. Previous works have sought to reduce the computational cost in various ways, such as imposing bands in the cost matrix or using a multiresolution approach. In this work, we utilize the fact that computation is an abundant resource and focus instead on exploring alternatives that approximate the inherently sequential DTW algorithm with one that is parallelizable. We describe two variations of an algorithm called Segmental DTW, in which the global cost matrix is broken into smaller sub-matrices, subsequence DTW is performed on each sub-matrix, and the results are used to solve a segment-level dynamic programming problem that specifies a globally optimal alignment path. We evaluate the proposed alignment algorithms on an audio-audio alignment task using the Chopin Mazurka dataset, and we show that they closely match the performance of regular DTW. We further demonstrate that almost all of the computations in Segmental DTW are parallelizable, and that one of the variants is unilaterally better than the other for both empirical and theoretical reasons.

</details>


### [79] [StemFX: Learning Mixing Style Representations via Autoregressive FX Chain Prediction on Source-Separated Stems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15634)
*Yuan-Chiao Cheng,Jui-Te Wu,Brian Chen,Yen-Tung Yeh,Yu-Hua Chen,Yi-Hsuan Yang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: StemFX is a framework that learns audio mixing styles by autoregressively predicting variable-length FX chains on source-separated stems, using a Transformer decoder and a band-split CNN encoder. It outperforms baselines on mixing style retrieval and transfer tasks, is over 4000x faster than iterative optimization, and was trained on ~105K songs with a unified 85-effect toolkit (MultiAFx).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for modeling FX chains (audio effect chains) are limited: some operate on stereo mixtures without per-stem modeling, others fix the number/type of effects per track, and many require differentiable effects or scarce multitrack datasets. StemFX addresses the need for a flexible, large-scale framework that can learn variable-length, per-stem FX chain representations directly from real-world music.

Method: StemFX uses a Transformer decoder to autoregressively predict tokenized FX chains, and a band-split multi-band CNN encoder with FiLM conditioning to capture per-stem spectral structure. Pseudo-stems are extracted from ~105K songs via source separation and augmented with MultiAFx (a unified toolkit of 85 audio effects from 7 Python libraries).

Result: StemFX outperforms all baseline models on mixing style retrieval across all tested chain lengths. For paired mixing style transfer, it achieves the best spectral fidelity and highest listener preference, and is over 4000 times faster than iterative optimization.

Conclusion: StemFX demonstrates that autoregressive prediction of variable-length FX chains on source-separated stems is an effective and scalable approach for learning and transferring mixing style, significantly outperforming prior methods in both fidelity and speed.

Abstract: Audio mixing style encompasses the artistic and technical decisions a mix engineer makes, including level balancing, spatialization, and the choice, ordering, and parameterization of audio effects (FX) on each stem. FX chains are a key determinant of this style, yet existing approaches to modeling them remain limited. Some operate on stereo mixtures without explicit per-stem FX chain modeling, others fix the number or type of effects per track, and many require differentiable effect implementations or scarce multitrack datasets. We present StemFX, a framework that learns mixing style representations by autoregressively predicting variable-length FX chains on source-separated stems. A Transformer decoder predicts tokenized FX chains autoregressively, while a band-split multi-band CNN encoder with FiLM conditioning captures per-stem spectral structure. To enable large-scale paired training, we extract pseudo-stems from about 105K songs via source separation and augment them using MultiAFx, a toolkit unifying 85 audio effects from 7 Python libraries. Evaluated on mixing style retrieval, StemFX outperforms all baseline models across all tested chain lengths. On paired mixing style transfer, StemFX achieves the best spectral fidelity and the highest listener preference, over 4000 times faster than iterative optimization.

</details>


### [80] [SpeechGuard: Online Defense against Backdoor Attacks on Speech Recognition Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15697)
*Jinwen Xin,Xixiang Lv*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: SpeechGuard is the first online backdoor defense pipeline for speech recognition models, using S-STRIP (adaptive perturbation injection) for detection and T-F masking with autoencoder-based mask generation for purification of poisoned audio samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Backdoor attacks pose critical security risks to neural network models in safety-sensitive applications like voice interaction for autonomous driving, yet existing defense methods for speech recognition models at run-time are insufficient.

Method: Proposes SpeechGuard with two-stage processing: (1) S-STRIP, an improved STRIP method using adaptive perturbation injection to detect and filter poisoned samples; (2) Purification via time-frequency (T-F) masking, where masks are autonomously generated by an autoencoder to suppress trigger signal expression.

Result: SpeechGuard accurately filters out poisoned samples and significantly mitigates backdoor threats through purification while maintaining prediction accuracy.

Conclusion: The two-stage defense pipeline prevents backdoor triggers from being activated in the model, enabling accurate predictions even on poisoned speech inputs.

Abstract: Backdoor attacks pose a critical threat to neural network models, allowing attackers to implant a backdoor during the training phase by manipulating a small portion of the training data. In security-sensitive applications such as voice interaction for autonomous driving, the presence of backdoor attacks introduces substantial security risks. This study focuses on implementing backdoor defense measures for speech recognition models in run-time, taking into account the characteristics of audio signals. We propose SpeechGuard, the first online backdoor defense pipeline designed to identify and purify poisoned audio samples. Specifically, we improve STRIP method to perform adaptive perturbation injection to detect and filter poisoned samples, named as S-STRIP. More importantly, we further consider the purification of poisoned samples. We utilize time-frequency (T-F) masking to suppress the expression of trigger signals and autonomously generate masks based on an autoencoder. The two-stage processing prevents the backdoor in the model from being triggered, and even input speech carrying triggers can be accurately predicted. Extensive experimental demonstrate that SpeechGuard can accurately filter out poisoned samples. Through purification, it can significantly mitigate the backdoor threat while maintaining a certain prediction accuracy.

</details>


### [81] [AuEmoChat: Authentic Emotion Understanding and Rendering for Conversational Speech Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15755)
*Zhenqi Jia,Yuan Zhao,Aruukhan,Rui Liu,Haizhou Li*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: AuEmoChat is a Conversational Speech Synthesis framework that uses a discrete authentic emotion token space (AuEmoCodec) and an emotion-guided token merging algorithm (AuEmoToMe) with Authentic Emotion Flow Matching to generate more expressive and emotionally authentic speech, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines on the NCSSD-EmCap dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CSS methods struggle to render authentic human emotions because they rely on limited predefined emotion label spaces (e.g., seven basic categories) and redundant multimodal tokens in multi-turn dialogue history interfere with context understanding.

Method: (1) AuEmoCodec: learns a discrete authentic emotion token space from large-scale emotional speech via finite scalar quantization. (2) AuEmoToMe: an authentic-emotion-guided token merging algorithm that removes redundant tokens in multimodal dialogue history while preserving emotion-relevant context. (3) Integration into an autoregressive text-speech model to predict target emotion and speech tokens. (4) Authentic Emotion Flow Matching: renders speech by jointly conditioning on merged dialogue context, target authentic emotion, and acoustic priors.

Result: Extensive experiments on the NCSSD-EmCap dataset demonstrate that AuEmoChat outperforms state-of-the-art CSS baselines and generates more expressive and authentic emotional speech.

Conclusion: AuEmoChat effectively addresses the limitations of predefined emotion categories and redundant multimodal context in CSS, enabling more authentic emotional expression and contextual consistency in conversational speech synthesis.

Abstract: Conversational Speech Synthesis (CSS) aims to synthesize speech with human-like emotional expression and contextual consistency in user-agent interactions. Existing CSS methods struggle to render authentic human emotions due to limited predefined emotion label spaces (e.g., seven emotion categories), while redundant multimodal tokens in multi-turn dialogue history interfere with context understanding. To address these issues, we propose AuEmoChat, a CSS framework for authentic emotion understanding and rendering. First, we develop AuEmoCodec, which learns a discrete authentic emotion token space from large-scale emotional speech via finite scalar quantization, enabling a more authentic emotion representation than limited basic emotion categories. We further propose AuEmoToMe, an authentic-emotion-guided token merging algorithm that merges redundant tokens in multimodal dialogue history while preserving emotion-relevant context. We integrate it into an autoregressive text-speech model to predict the target authentic emotion token and speech tokens. Finally, we propose Authentic Emotion Flow Matching, which renders speech by jointly conditioning on merged dialogue context, target authentic emotion, and acoustic priors. Extensive experiments on the NCSSD-EmCap dataset demonstrate that AuEmoChat outperforms state-of-the-art CSS baselines and generates more expressive and authentic emotional speech.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [82] [DebrisTracer: Reliable Tracking in Hypervelocity Impact Fast Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15986)
*Théophane Loloum,Fabien Vivodtzev,David Hébert,Baptiste Reynier,Michel Arrigoni,Julien Tierny*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DebrisTracer is a framework for reliable tracking of debris in hypervelocity impact fast imaging, extending off-the-shelf topology tracking with domain knowledge to automatically produce accurate debris tracking. It achieves improved accuracy over established tools, enables visual analysis, and identifies distinct debris regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable estimation of debris mass and speed distributions from hypervelocity impact fast imaging is critical for aerospace applications, but existing tools face challenges with noisy, highly specific datasets containing large numbers of debris fragments.

Method: Extends an off-the-shelf topology tracking framework (based on critical point extraction and matching) by incorporating domain knowledge and physical assumptions to automatically produce accurate debris tracking.

Result: Demonstrates accuracy improvements over established tools used by domain experts across multiple use cases (varying impact angles and physics), validated via prediction of experimental ejected mass and crater depth profiles. Statistical summaries enable visual identification of distinct debris regimes.

Conclusion: DebrisTracer provides automatic, accurate, and interpretable debris tracking that corroborates and refines prior domain expert expectations, with open-source C++ implementation and database available.

Abstract: This application paper presents DebrisTracer, a framework for the reliable tracking of debris in hypervelocity impact fast imaging. These noisy and highly specific datasets capture the ejection of a large number of debris fragments after the impact of a projectile launched at hypervelocity into a target material. The reliable estimation of debris mass and speed distributions is of major importance in aerospace applications. We document how to extend an off-the-shelf topology tracking framework based on critical point extraction and matching, in order to incorporate domain knowledge and physical assumptions. Our approach automatically produces an accurate and reliable debris tracking, enabling an interpretable visual analysis of this complex space-time phenomenon. Extensive experiments demonstrate the accuracy improvements provided by our approach over established tools used by domain experts in terms of physical validation, specifically via the prediction of the experimental ejected mass and crater depth profiles. We illustrate the utility of our approach across several use cases (with varying impact angles and physics). We show that our statistical summaries enable the visual identification of distinct regimes within the debris population, corroborating and refining prior expectations of domain experts. Our database and our C++ implementation are available at this address: https://github.com/tloloum/DebrisTracer.

</details>


### [83] [Structure of the Circular-Dyadic Convolution Error](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15293)
*Ben Fauber,Alireza Moradzadeh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes the algebraic error when substituting the Hadamard transform for the FFT-computed DFT in dyadic/circular convolution, showing structured error with exact cancellation at four positions, near-full-rank error operator, and expected error governed by alignment scalar that doubles output energy except in a zero-error subspace.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Hadamard transform is computationally preferable to the FFT-computed DFT due to its real-valued sign flips (avoiding complex arithmetic), yet its substitution introduces algebraic error. The paper aims to characterize this error to understand when and why the approximation is acceptable.

Method: Three complementary analyses: (1) exact error cancellation identified at specific input/output positions; (2) rank analysis of the error operator showing nearly full rank with logarithmically small null space; (3) statistical analysis deriving closed-form expected error via an alignment scalar under random filters.

Result: Error cancellation at two input and two output positions (universally error-free) with no reordering fix. Error operator has near-full rank (null space only logarithmic dimension). Expected error governed by a single alignment scalar with closed-form expression. Substitution error asymptotically doubles output energy except for filters in the universal zero-error subspace.

Conclusion: The substitution error is structured, predictable, and governed by alignment rather than random; it can be controlled by understanding the alignment properties of the filter.

Abstract: Dyadic and circular convolution can both be computed in $O(N\log N)$ time using the Hadamard transform and the FFT-computed discrete Fourier transform (DFT), respectively. The Hadamard transform is preferable for its real-valued sign flips, yet its substitution for the DFT introduces algebraic error. We present three complementary results that characterize this error. First, we identify exact error cancellation: two input and two output positions are universally error-free, and no reordering of the output can eliminate this error. Second, the error operator is nearly full rank, while its null space has only logarithmic dimension. Third, the expected error is governed by a single alignment scalar, with a closed-form expression obtained by averaging over random filters. In general, the substitution error asymptotically doubles the output energy, except for filters in the universal zero-error subspace, which incur no error. Collectively, these results show that the substitution error is structured, predictable, and governed by alignment.

</details>


### [84] [Position: Quantum Program Generation Must Prioritize Validity Over Probabilistic Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15313)
*Junhao Song,Yu Zhou,William Knottenbelt,Yudong Cao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper argues that scaling up models is insufficient for quantum circuit synthesis due to the syntax-semantics gap in Hilbert space constraints, proposing instead verification-aware architectures with hierarchical constraints and symbolic proxies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge the prevailing scaling hypothesis when applied to quantum circuit synthesis, highlighting that probabilistic language models cannot capture the strict mathematical constraints of quantum mechanics.

Method: Proposes a paradigm shift from human-centric copilots to verifier-centric agents, integrating hierarchical constraints, topological masks, and symbolic proxies directly into generation, along with theoretical analysis of the validity gap.

Result: Demonstrates that the valid subset of quantum circuit designs decays exponentially with qubit count, making post-hoc filtering intractable, and that scale alone cannot bridge the validity gap.

Conclusion: Verification-aware architectures that encode task-specific rules of quantum information offer a viable path; generation methods should prioritize mathematical constraints over imitation learning from unverified data.

Abstract: The scaling hypothesis assumes that increasing model parameters yields emergent reasoning capabilities. This position paper argues that applying this probabilistic paradigm to generic quantum circuit synthesis is a directional error. Unlike natural languages, quantum circuits require strict adherence to mathematical constraints that manifest a significant syntax-semantics gap. Training on unverified quantum programs means that models learn syntax but fail to capture the physical semantics of the Hilbert space. Since the valid subset of circuit designs decays exponentially with the number of qubits, post-hoc filtering is mathematically intractable. We propose a pivot from human-centric copilots to verifier-centric agents. We integrate hierarchical constraints, topological masks, and symbolic proxies directly into generation. Our analysis suggests that scale alone cannot bridge the validity gap. Verification-aware architectures offer a viable path for modular quantum program generation. These considerations point toward generation methods that encode task-specific rules of quantum information, rather than relying on imitation alone.

</details>


### [85] [A Transportable Threshold-Based Framework for Interpretable Classification of Medical Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15394)
*Antony Garcia,Adrian Noriega,Gabrielle Britton,Xinming Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a statistically grounded, fully interpretable rule-based clinical classification framework using Bernoulli Naïve Bayes with χ²-guided binarization, achieving strong AUC scores (0.800–0.984) on three medical datasets while enabling transparent, software-free reproducibility via basic arithmetic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Black-box AI models limit adoption in medicine due to lack of interpretability and reproducibility; a transparent yet performant alternative is needed for clinical decision support.

Method: The framework uses supervised χ²-guided statistical binarization to convert continuous variables into binary features, then applies Bernoulli Naïve Bayes for probabilistic rule-based classification, with leakage-safe cross-validated calibration (Brier score, calibration intercept/slope, beta calibration).

Result: AUC scores: 0.800 (Pima Diabetes), 0.984 (Wisconsin Breast Cancer), 0.919 (Heart Failure). Calibration analysis showed improved probability reliability after post-hoc beta calibration across datasets.

Conclusion: A statistically interpretable framework can match complex model performance while providing explicit, clinically meaningful decision rules and calibrated risk estimates, supporting trustworthy and generalizable AI in healthcare.

Abstract: Black-box models limit the adoption of artificial intelligence in medicine due to their lack of interpretability and reproducibility. We introduce a statistically grounded framework that provides fully interpretable, rule-based clinical classification using the Bernoulli Naïve Bayes (BNB) model. The method applies supervised $χ^2$-guided statistical binarization to continuous variables, identifying thresholds that maximize association with clinical outcomes within the training data. This transformation allows BNB to operate effectively on continuous medical data without sacrificing its inherent transparency. The approach was evaluated on three benchmark datasets, Pima Indians Diabetes, Wisconsin Breast Cancer, and Heart Failure Prediction, achieving area-under-the-curve (AUC) scores of 0.800 for the Pima analysis, 0.984 for Wisconsin Breast Cancer, and 0.919 for Heart Failure Prediction. In addition to discrimination, probabilistic reliability was assessed using leakage-safe cross-validated calibration analysis including Brier score, calibration intercept/slope, and post-hoc beta calibration, which improved probability calibration across datasets. These results suggest that a statistically interpretable framework can achieve performance comparable to more complex models while providing explicit, clinically meaningful decision rules and calibrated risk estimates. To illustrate this transparency concretely, a complete worked example demonstrates that model inference can be reproduced using only a reference table and basic arithmetic, without access to software or proprietary tools. This work offers a practical approach to supporting trustworthy and generalizable AI in real-world healthcare settings.

</details>


### [86] [Regularity-Aware Stochastic MGDA with Adaptive Conflict-Avoidant Update Direction Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15412)
*Chentong Huang,Lisha Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper identifies that the conflict-avoidant (CA) direction in stochastic multi-gradient descent (SMG) is only 1/2-Hölder continuous (worst-case optimal), leading to suboptimal O(T^{-1/4}) convergence. Under regularity conditions, it becomes Lipschitz continuous, motivating the proposed MoRe method which switches between CA updates (when conflict is large) and scalarization (otherwise), achieving improved O(T^{-1/2}) convergence with conflict-avoidance guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stochastic MGDA suffers from slow convergence due to noise in mini-batch gradients causing bias in the conflict-avoidant update direction, lacking a theoretical understanding of the direction's continuity properties and how to improve convergence rates.

Method: The paper first proves the CA direction is 1/2-Hölder continuous w.r.t. the Jacobian matrix (tight bound), and under additional regularity it becomes Lipschitz continuous. Based on this, they propose MoRe (Multi-objective Regularity-aware method) that uses CA direction when gradient conflict is large and switches to fixed scalarization otherwise, exploiting continuity properties.

Result: Improves convergence rate from O(T^{-1/4}) to O(T^{-1/2}) in nonconvex stochastic settings (hiding log factors), while maintaining per-iterate conflict-avoidance guarantees. Empirical results confirm effectiveness in multi-task performance and matching theoretical convergence rates.

Conclusion: The Hölder continuity analysis reveals the fundamental limitation of vanilla SMG, and the regularity-aware switching strategy of MoRe successfully escapes this limitation to achieve faster convergence, validated both theoretically and empirically.

Abstract: Multi-objective learning (MOL) aims to optimize multiple objectives simultaneously. The multi-gradient descent algorithm (MGDA) is a workhorse that iteratively updates along a common descent or conflict-avoidant (CA) direction across objectives. In stochastic settings, however, the vanilla stochastic MGDA method, SMG, lacks a fast convergence rate because mini-batch sampling introduces noise in the gradients. This causes bias in the update direction, which is controlled by the CA direction continuity. In this paper, we show that the CA direction is $1/2$-Holder continuous with respect to the Jacobian matrix, and the exponent $1/2$ cannot be improved in the worst case. This leads to a suboptimal convergence rate for vanilla stochastic MGDA in prior works. Nevertheless, under additional regularity conditions, we show this can be improved to Lipschitz continuity. Based on this insight, we propose a stochastic multi-objective regularity-aware (MoRe) method that exploits the Lipschitz continuity of the CA direction when the subproblem is regular, and switches to a fixed scalarization weight otherwise. Intuitively, the proposed algorithm employs CA direction update when the gradient conflict is large, and linear scalarization update otherwise. Theoretically, our method improves the convergence rate of SMG in the nonconvex setting from $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(T^{-1/4})$ to $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(T^{-1/2})$, where $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(\cdot)$ hides logarithmic factors. Meanwhile, we also establish the per-iterate conflict-avoidance guarantees. Empirically, experiments demonstrate its effectiveness in multi-task performance and verify convergence behavior consistent with the established theoretical rate.

</details>


### [87] [AI Trading: Evaluating Large Language Models for Technical Market Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15414)
*Geofrey Ntale*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Five LLMs (GPT-4 Turbo, Claude 3 Opus, Gemini 1.5 Pro, Llama 3 70B, FinGPT) are systematically evaluated on technical market analysis tasks. GPT-4 Turbo leads in returns and Sharpe ratio among general-purpose models, while FinGPT excels in risk-adjusted metrics via domain-specific fine-tuning. Both outperform S&P 500 benchmarks. Persistent issues include numerical hallucination, context-window limits, and poor sideways-market performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically evaluate and compare the technical market analysis capabilities of prominent LLMs, identifying their strengths, weaknesses, and deployment requirements for AI trading systems.

Method: Four structured tasks: (1) candlestick pattern recognition from OHLCV data, (2) directional signal generation (BUY/SELL/HOLD), (3) backtesting signal quality via simulated execution pipeline, and (4) financial report comprehension. Quantitative metrics: Sharpe ratio, max drawdown, Sortino ratio, information coefficient, F1-score, BLEU score. Compared against S&P 500 passive benchmark.

Result: GPT-4 Turbo achieves highest annualized return and Sharpe ratio among general-purpose models; FinGPT shows competitive risk-adjusted performance due to domain fine-tuning. Both outperform S&P 500 benchmark. All models exhibit numerical hallucination, context-window limitations, and inconsistent performance in sideways markets.

Conclusion: LLMs show genuine promise for AI trading systems, but robust deployment needs careful task decomposition, rigorous backtesting protocols, and domain-aware fine-tuning strategies.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for processing the heterogeneous information environments of modern financial markets. This paper presents a systematic, comparative evaluation of five prominent LLMs: GPT-4 Turbo, Claude 3 Opus, Gemini 1.5 Pro, Llama 3 70B, and the domain-specialized FinGPT, with respect to their capacity for technical market analysis. The evaluation spans four structured tasks: candlestick pattern recognition from OHLCV data, directional signal generation (BUY/SELL/HOLD), backtesting of signal quality through a simulated execution pipeline, and financial report comprehension. Our experimental framework employs rigorous quantitative metrics, including Sharpe ratio, maximum drawdown, Sortino ratio, information coefficient, F1-score, and BLEU score. Findings from simulated backtesting indicate that GPT-4 Turbo achieves the highest annualized return and Sharpe ratio among general-purpose models, while FinGPT demonstrates competitive risk-adjusted performance due to domain-specific fine-tuning. Both models outperform a passive S&P 500 benchmark under the tested conditions. The study identifies persistent failure modes across all evaluated models, including numerical hallucination, context-window limitations, and inconsistent performance in sideways market regimes. We conclude that while LLMs hold genuine promise within AI trading systems, robust deployment requires careful task decomposition, rigorous backtesting protocols, and domain-aware fine-tuning strategies.

</details>


### [88] [qZACH-ViT: Quantization-Aware Intrinsic Explanations with Recursive Attribution-Stabilized Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15421)
*Athanasios Angelakis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: qZACH-ViT is a quantization-aware, compact medical image classifier that extends the zero-token, position-free ZACH-ViT backbone with recursive intrinsic patch-level evidence. Combined with RASO (a norm-matching, conflict-removing optimization), it achieves superior accuracy over FP32 baselines on MedMNIST, near-perfect prediction agreement (99.9751%) and attribution similarity (cosine 0.999955) after INT8 quantization, 70% smaller ONNX artifacts, up to 2.39× CPU speedup, and improved stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficiency and interpretable evidence for compact medical image classifiers are often addressed separately; there is a need to jointly optimize both goals in a deployable quantized model.

Method: Introduces qZACH-ViT (quantization-aware ZACH-ViT with recursive intrinsic patch-level class evidence) and RASO (Recursive Attribution-Stabilized Optimization), which norm-matches classification and attribution gradients and removes conflicting attribution components. Evaluated on 7 MedMNIST datasets (50 images/class, 10 seeds, 280 runs). Converts 210 checkpoints to mixed-precision ONNX INT8 graphs with 16 signed INT8 MatMulInteger projections.

Result: qZACH-ViT with Adam improves FP32 baseline on all 7 datasets (mean gain 0.0313); with RASO gain is 0.0368. Prediction agreement 99.9751% (964,920 comparisons). Attribution similarity: cosine 0.999955, rank correlation 0.9944, top-10% overlap 0.9692. ONNX artifacts 70% smaller; CPU speedups 1.41× (1 thread) and 2.39× (4 threads). RASO reduces sufficiency error and improves input-noise stability.

Conclusion: qZACH-ViT is established as a deployable compact intrinsically explainable model; RASO is a targeted stability-oriented optimization procedure that does not dominate every predictive or XAI metric but improves stability.

Abstract: Compact medical-image classifiers need efficiency and interpretable evidence, yet these goals are often addressed separately. We introduce qZACH-ViT, a quantization-aware extension of the zero-token (CLS-token-free), position-free ZACH-ViT backbone with recursive intrinsic patch-level class evidence. We also introduce Recursive Attribution-Stabilized Optimization (RASO), which norm-matches classification and attribution gradients and removes attribution components that conflict with classification. We evaluate four controlled conditions on seven MedMNIST datasets using 50 training images per class and ten fixed seeds, completing 280 runs. All 210 qZACH-ViT checkpoints are converted to executable mixed-precision ONNX INT8 graphs containing 16 signed INT8 MatMulInteger projections with INT32 accumulation. Deployed mixed-precision INT8 qZACH-ViT with Adam improves the FP32 ZACH-ViT baseline mean on all seven datasets, with a mean paired gain of 0.0313 in the dataset-specific primary metric; qZACH-ViT with RASO yields a mean gain of 0.0368. Across 964,920 source-to-INT8 test comparisons, prediction agreement is 99.9751\%, with a mean absolute primary-metric change of 0.000133 and a maximum of 0.004386. Across 3,600 matched intrinsic maps, mean cosine similarity is 0.999955, mean rank correlation is 0.9944, and mean top-10\% overlap is 0.9692. ONNX artifacts are 70.0\% smaller than source checkpoints and provide $1.41\times$ and $2.39\times$ end-to-end CPU speedups with one and four threads. RASO significantly reduces sufficiency error and improves input-noise stability over Adam with the same attribution loss, but does not dominate every predictive or explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) metric. These results establish qZACH-ViT as a deployable compact intrinsically explainable model and RASO as a targeted stability-oriented optimization procedure.

</details>


### [89] [From hyperplanes to hyperellipsoids: characterizing the inherent interpretability of linear and single-qubit mixed-state binary classification models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15433)
*Kaitlin Gili*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Compares a single qubit mixed state quantum model to a standard linear model for binary classification, showing the quantum model is an "ellipsoid version" of linear classification, offering distinct geometric and feature importance inductive biases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide an accessible bridge for ML practitioners and educators with no quantum background to understand quantum machine learning concepts through familiar linear classification frameworks.

Method: Side-by-side theoretical comparison of the interpretability, geometric inductive biases (hyperplane vs. hyperellipsoid), and feature importance inductive biases between a standard linear model and a single qubit mixed state model for binary classification.

Result: A single qubit mixed state model is equivalent to learning a hyperellipsoid instead of a hyperplane, with distinct geometric and feature importance inductive biases compared to linear models.

Conclusion: This characterization enables quantum ML concepts to be introduced in undergraduate ML classrooms without requiring quantum background, serving as a pedagogical tool.

Abstract: We characterize and compare the inherent interpretability offerings of a standard linear model with a single qubit mixed state model for the task of supervised binary classification. A side by side comparison reveals that a single qubit mixed state model for binary classification is just the ``ellipsoid version" of standard linear model classification. More precisely, rather than learning a hyperplane to classify data, we learn a hyperellipsoid. We discuss the consequences of the geometric inductive biases of both models, as well as how each model contains a different feature importance inductive bias. This short characterization offers an accessible route to quantum machine learning (ML) ideas for readers who have zero background in quantum and are only familiar with linear classification in ML. In support of ML pedagogy, we encourage instructors to utilize this piece to smoothly introduce quantum ML ideas into the undergraduate ML classroom.

</details>


### [90] [Stochastic Reset Pathfinding: Path-Level Regret for Cascading Bandits over Graph Paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15440)
*Guni Sharon,Wei Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SRP is an episodic pathfinding problem with stochastic edge failures and global resets, analyzed under the CCB framework with Log-Dijkstra meta-algorithms (PathUCB/PathTS), featuring a per-path regret bound and empirical comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To formally model and solve pathfinding problems where edge failures cause full resets to the source, motivated by quantum repeater networks, Lightning Network payment routing, and unreliable mesh networks.

Method: Formulates SRP as a combinatorial cascading bandit (CCB) problem, proposes Log-Dijkstra meta-algorithm with two instantiations: PathUCB (UCB-based) and PathTS (Thompson Sampling-based), and derives a path-level regret bound that decomposes via per-path complexity C(π).

Result: PathUCB achieves a path-level regret bound complementing edge-level CCB bounds, more informative on structured graphs with polynomially many paths. PathTS achieves best empirical performance across quantum-network, layered-DAG, grid-world, and Erdos-Renyi domains, but fails on an adversarial instance due to known exponential obstruction for combinatorial TS on multiplicative rewards.

Conclusion: Recommends PathTS as the practical default algorithm while noting adversarial vulnerability; provides theoretical and empirical understanding of the global-reset pathfinding bandit problem.

Abstract: We introduce Stochastic Reset Pathfinding (SRP), an episodic learning problem on a known directed graph with unknown stationary edge success probabilities. In each episode, the agent commits to a source-to-goal path, and any edge failure during execution resets it to the source. SRP captures settings such as entanglement distribution in quantum repeater networks, payment routing on the Lightning Network, and delivery in unreliable mesh networks. We show that the global-reset structure makes the optimal policy open-loop, placing SRP within the combinatorial cascading bandit (CCB) framework. We propose a Log-Dijkstra meta-algorithm with UCB (PathUCB) and Thompson Sampling (PathTS) instantiations. Our main technical result is a path-level regret bound for PathUCB that decomposes regret over suboptimal paths via a per-path complexity C(pi) combining each edge's prefix and suffix reliability. The bound is complementary to the edge-level CCB bound and more informative on structured graphs with polynomially many source-to-goal paths. Experiments on quantum-network, layered-DAG, grid-world, and Erdos-Renyi domains support the theory and show that PathTS typically achieves the best empirical performance among the algorithms tested. We then exhibit an adversarial instance on which PathTS fails to converge, consistent with a known exponential obstruction for combinatorial Thompson Sampling on multiplicative-reward problems. We recommend PathTS as the practical default while cautioning that adversarial instances exist.

</details>


### [91] [Who Became Financially Vulnerable After COVID-19? A Population-Level Machine Learning Analysis Using MEPS Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15446)
*Alexey Kresin,Zien Cheng,Ammar Ahad,Ebiyomare Kelvin,Manish Sivaratri,Prabhjeet Singh,Omar Aljawfi,Olabisi Ojo,Nawar Shara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study uses MEPS data (2019 vs. 2021) to examine healthcare financial vulnerability (out-of-pocket costs >10% of family income) before and after COVID-19. Using logistic regression, random forest, and gradient boosting, the authors find that poverty, insurance coverage, and prescription drug spending are key predictors. Despite increased burden among vulnerable groups in 2021, pre-pandemic models remained fairly predictive post-pandemic, indicating stable underlying predictors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Healthcare costs remain a concern in the U.S., and the COVID-19 pandemic may have exacerbated financial vulnerability. The study aims to assess changes in financial burden before and after the pandemic.

Method: MEPS data (2019 and 2021); high financial burden defined as out-of-pocket spending >10% of family income. Survey-weighted subgroup analyses, logistic regression (adjusted odds ratios), and machine learning models (random forest, gradient boosting) with temporal generalization testing.

Result: Financial vulnerability strongly associated with poverty, insurance coverage, and prescription drug spending. Persistent disparities with some increased burden among vulnerable groups in 2021. Pre-pandemic models showed only modest performance reduction on post-pandemic data.

Conclusion: Principal predictors of healthcare financial vulnerability remained relatively stable despite COVID-19 disruptions. Combining interpretable statistical modeling with machine learning is valuable for population health research and policy.

Abstract: The cost of healthcare remains a concern in the United States and may have been influenced by disruptions associated with the COVID-19 pandemic. This study examines healthcare financial vulnerability before and after the pandemic using Medical Expenditure Panel Survey (MEPS) data from 2019 and 2021. High financial burden was defined as out-of-pocket healthcare expenditures exceeding 10% of family income. Survey-weighted subgroup analyses were performed to obtain nationally representative estimates across demographic and socioeconomic groups.
  Descriptive analyses were complemented by interpretable logistic regression and machine learning models. Logistic regression was used to estimate adjusted odds ratios, while random forest and gradient boosting models were used to evaluate predictive performance. Temporal generalization assessed whether models trained on pre-pandemic data remained predictive when applied to post-pandemic observations.
  Financial vulnerability was strongly associated with poverty status, insurance coverage, and prescription drug spending. Subgroup analyses indicated persistent disparities across population groups, with some evidence of increased burden among vulnerable populations in 2021. Despite these differences, models trained on pre-pandemic data exhibited only modest reductions in predictive performance when evaluated on post-pandemic data, suggesting that the principal predictors of healthcare financial vulnerability remained relatively stable over time.
  These findings provide a population-level assessment of healthcare financial vulnerability during the COVID-19 period and demonstrate the value of combining interpretable statistical modeling with machine learning for population health research. The results may support future population health surveillance, risk stratification, and healthcare policy research aimed at reducing financial barriers to care.

</details>


### [92] [LLM4EHR: Aligning Clinical Time Series with Medical Event Sequences via Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15447)
*Jingteng Li,Alexander Capstick,Louise Rigny,Iona Biggart,Neil J Sebire,Payam Barnaghi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM4EHR is a clinical foundation model pre-trained on ICU EHR data that aligns clinical events and time series using a domain-adapted LLM and a regularized contrastive objective, achieving improved performance on downstream clinical tasks and enabling few-shot transfer to new cohorts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing clinical foundation models insufficiently explore shared temporal structures between clinical events and time series observations in EHR data, leading to less robust and adaptive models with reduced downstream performance.

Method: LLM4EHR combines a domain-adapted large language model with a transformer-based time series encoder, pre-trained via a regularized contrastive objective that temporally aligns EHR events and time series to learn robust representations.

Result: Learned EHR time series embeddings from LLM4EHR improve performance on various downstream clinical tasks with competitive results, and demonstrate transferability to new cohorts via k-shot adaptation.

Conclusion: Exploiting temporal alignment between clinical events and time series produces more generalizable and performant clinical foundation models, offering a step toward better ICU outcome prediction.

Abstract: Recent research in clinical machine learning, focusing on outcome predictions in intensive care unit (ICU), has shifted from bespoke supervised models to foundation models, utilising modern representation learning methods. Here, foundation models are pre-trained on mixtures of complex clinical data modalities, useful for various downstream tasks. Existing works often utilise Electronic Health Records (EHR) to provide rich and diverse patient observations to train clinical foundation models. However, existing methods do not sufficiently explore the shared temporal structures between clinical events and time series (TS) observations recorded in EHRs. This limitation potentially leads to less robust and adaptive clinical foundation models, resulting in reduced performance on downstream tasks. To fully exploit this temporal structure, we propose LLM4EHR, a new clinical foundation model trained on ICU EHR data. Combining domain adapted large language models with a transformer TS encoder, we pre-trained LLM4EHR by temporally aligning the EHR events and TS. For this, we propose a regularised contrastive objective to learn robust EHR TS representations conditioned on EHR event embeddings produced by the domain adapted LLM. Supported by an ablation study, we find that learnt EHR TS embeddings from LLM4EHR improve performance on various downstream clinical tasks with competitive performance. Further, we empirically demonstrate that LLM4EHR learns transferable clinical TS embeddings that can be deployed to new cohorts via k-shot adaptation. These findings provide a step towards building more generalisable and performant clinical foundation models.

</details>


### [93] [Relevant and Irrelevant: A Renormalization Group Analysis of Transformer Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15449)
*Parviz Haggi-Mani,Irina Rish*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Using Wilsonian RG theory, the paper treats Transformer attention as a perturbation of the MLP fixed point, finding that attention's relevance depends on data correlation length: relevant (driving phase transitions) for long correlations, irrelevant for short correlations, with first-layer heads dominating the shift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether and under what conditions the attention mechanism in Transformers fundamentally changes the learned representation (relevant perturbation) versus being absorbed into the MLP dynamics (irrelevant perturbation), using the principled lens of renormalization group theory.

Method: Derives a fixed-point shift formula from Wilsonian RG, yielding four predictions (fixed-point geometry, effective rank profile, layer specificity, perturbation decay spectrum). Tests these on synthetic Markov chain sequences with controlled correlation length.

Result: (1) Long correlation: attention is strongly relevant, driving a phase transition with rank jump above input dimension. (2) Short correlation: attention is irrelevant, converging to MLP fixed point. (3) First-layer head dominates (4× shift vs. subsequent heads). (4) Long correlation: selective preservation of slow Markov modes (5.4× dynamic range). Short correlation: uniform suppression.

Conclusion: Attention's relevance is not an architectural property but depends on the spectral structure of the data-generating process; a first-order RG perturbation framework predictively captures this difference.

Abstract: Using the language of Wilsonian renormalization group theory (RG), we treat the Transformer's attention mechanism as a perturbation of the trained MLP residual-stack fixed point and ask whether it constitutes a relevant, marginal, or irrelevant operator. We derive a fixed-point shift formula and obtain four testable predictions for the fixed-point geometry, effective rank profile, layer specificity, and perturbation decay spectrum. Testing these on synthetic Markov chain sequences with controlled correlation length, we find: (1) For large chains(long correlation), attention is strongly relevant: it closes a residual loss gap the MLP cannot bridge and drives a phase transition in representation space, with effective rank jumping above input dimensionality at layer 1 and stabilizing at a high-dimensional plateau. (2) For short chains(short correlation), attention is irrelevant: the Transformer converges to the same loss and fixed-point geometry as the MLP, though it contracts perturbations faster. (3) The transition is dominated by the first-layer head (L0H0), which accounts for more than 4 times the representational shift of any subsequent head, consistent with the prediction that the relevant operator acts before the MLP begins integrating out positional variation. (4) Perturbation decay experiments reveal a regime reversal: in the long correlation regime the Transformer selectively preserves slow Markov modes (5.4 times the dynamic range in decay length vs. 1.3 times for the MLP); in the short correlation regime it suppresses all modes faster than the MLP, with no spectral selectivity. Together, these results show that the relevance of attention is not a property of the architecture but of the spectral structure of the data-generating process, and that a first-order RG perturbation framework provides a predictive account of that difference.

</details>


### [94] [Looped Latent Attention: Cross-Loop KV Compression for Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15456)
*James O' Neill,Fergal Reid*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Looped Latent Attention (LLA) compresses the K/V cache of weight-tied Transformers by storing compact latents and reconstructing loop-specific vectors only when needed, achieving up to 21.3x compression with minimal quality loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Weight-tied Transformers reuse blocks across loops but require a separate K/V cache per recurrence step, creating significant memory overhead. The paper identifies that this recurrent cache is highly structured (low-rank across loops, flat across heads/layers), motivating a compression approach.

Method: LLA is a post-training cache codec that stores compact K and V latents and reconstructs loop-specific vectors on-demand. Per-head LLA compresses along the recurrence axis; LLA-2D additionally folds heads into one latent. Codec is initialized via SVD of teacher activations and refined with KL divergence and attention-output distillation.

Result: Per-head LLA outperforms MLA, cross-layer sharing, KV quantization, and final-loop reuse at matched cache budget. On H200, Ouro-1.4B batch capacity at 4k context increases from 32 to 768 sequences (21.3x compression). On-policy refinement raises MATH-500 from 0.43 to 0.66 at 4x compression and reduces no-answer generations.

Conclusion: The recurrent cache is low-rank but not safely collapsible to a single state. The head-axis advantage holds across model families (Ouro, Huginn) and the cache reduction is exact, enabling significant throughput improvements for long-context reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Looped, weight-tied Transformers reduce parameters by reusing a block, but decoding still stores a separate K/V cache for every recurrence step. We show that this loop-indexed cache is highly structured. For a fixed token, layer and head, K/V vectors trace a short low-rank trajectory across loops, while the head and layer axes remain much flatter. We introduce Looped Latent Attention (LLA), a post-training cache codec that stores compact K and V latents and reconstructs loop-specific K/V vectors only when attention reads them. The default per-head codec compresses recurrence, while LLA-2D also folds heads into one latent for the extreme-compression regime. The codec is initialized from SVD of teacher activations and refined with KL and attention-output distillation. At matched cache budget, per-head LLA outperforms head-axis MLA, cross-layer sharing, KV quantization and final-loop reuse, showing that the recurrent cache is low-rank but not safely collapsible to a single state. The same axis advantage holds on Ouro-2.6B-Thinking and transfers to Huginn-3.5B, where an SVD codec remains near-lossless to 32x in decoder-independent evaluation. The cache reduction is exact. On one H200, the latent-store path increases measured Ouro-1.4B batch capacity at 4k context from 32 to 768 sequences at 21.3x compression. For long math rollouts, on-policy refinement on student-generated prefixes raises MATH-500 at 4x from 0.43 to 0.66 and reduces no-answer generations.

</details>


### [95] [Robust Peak-cost Constrained Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15457)
*Shilpa Mukhopadhyay,Sourav Ganguly,Santosh Mohan Rajkumar,Honghao Wei,Debdipta Goswami,Arnob Ghosh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies robust peak-cost constrained RL, showing that peak-cost MDPs may not have zero duality gap, and proposing a surrogate optimization framework with robust value estimation to handle dynamics perturbations while controlling maximum trajectory cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard CMDPs based on expected cumulative cost cannot capture catastrophic single large violations. Peak-cost constraints are needed for safety-critical applications, but their duality properties are unclear, and simulator-to-real-world dynamics mismatch further complicates the problem.

Method: The authors establish that peak-cost constrained MDPs may not admit zero duality gap, propose a surrogate optimization framework, and develop a robust value estimation method based on integral probability metrics to handle dynamics perturbations.

Result: With appropriate hyperparameters, the surrogate solution achieves the same robust reward as the original problem while violating the constraint by at most epsilon. Experiments show effective safety enforcement under dynamics perturbations with strong reward performance.

Conclusion: The proposed method effectively addresses peak-cost constraints under dynamics mismatch, with theoretical guarantees on reward optimality and bounded constraint violation.

Abstract: We study robust peak-cost constrained reinforcement learning (RP-CRL), where the objective is to maximize expected reward while controlling the maximum cost encountered along a trajectory. This setting is motivated by safety-critical applications in which a single large violation can be catastrophic and therefore cannot be adequately captured by the standard CMDP framework based on expected cumulative cost. Existing reachability-constrained RL methods adopt Lagrangian-based approaches, yet the underlying duality properties of peak-cost constrained MDPs remain unclear. We show that, unlike standard CMDPs, peak-cost constrained MDPs may not admit zero duality gap. We further consider a robust formulation to address simulator-to-real-world mismatch in the transition dynamics. To solve this problem, we develop a surrogate optimization framework and a robust value estimation method based on integral probability metrics. We prove that, with appropriate hyperparameter choices, the surrogate solution attains the same robust reward value as the original problem while violating the constraint by at most epsilon. Experiments show that the proposed method effectively enforces safety under dynamics perturbations while retaining strong reward performance.

</details>


### [96] [ADS-C: Antidistillation Sampling for Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15467)
*Khawaja Abaid Ullah,Mohammad Javad Khojasteh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ADS-C is a zero-cost antidistillation defense for classification that preserves teacher accuracy while degrading distilled student accuracy by up to 29.7 points, outperforming existing defenses that cost significant teacher accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge distillation enables adversaries to replicate proprietary classifiers by querying prediction interfaces. Existing antidistillation defenses (designed for LLMs) degrade teacher accuracy when transferred to classification tasks, creating an unfavorable trade-off between defense and utility.

Method: Ads-C adapts gradient-directed perturbation to classification by applying a closed-form, per-input margin budget that provably preserves every served top-1 prediction, ensuring teacher accuracy remains unchanged. Temperature softening rescales the defense's phase transition behavior in closed form.

Result: ADS-C preserves teacher accuracy identically while causing distilled student accuracy to drop by 17.4 points (CIFAR-100), 29.6 (CIFAR-10), and 13.3 (Tiny-ImageNet). Matching this degradation with unmodified defense costs 27.5, 32.9, and 22.2 points of teacher accuracy. Hard-label attackers gain nothing; soft-label student accuracy drops up to 29.7 points below that floor.

Conclusion: ADS-C is the first antidistillation defense for classification with exactly zero utility cost, reversing the incentive to distill served probabilities rather than merely removing it.

Abstract: Knowledge distillation enables an adversary to replicate a proprietary classifier by querying its prediction interface and training a surrogate on the returned probability vectors. Antidistillation sampling, proposed for large language models, counters this threat with an input-dependent, gradient-directed perturbation of the served distribution; its transfer to classification has not been studied. Adapting the defense to classification, we show its behavior is governed by the distribution of the teacher's per-input confidence margins. Because well-trained classifiers are severely overconfident, the direct transfer exhibits an inert window: below a closed-form-predictable threshold, it affects neither attacker nor defender; beyond it, the defense undergoes a phase transition and degrades the teacher faster than the attacker's student. Temperature softening rescales the transition in closed form, and every temperature configuration lies on the same unfavorable trade-off curve. Our method, ADS-C, composes the perturbation under a closed-form, per-input margin budget that provably preserves every served top-1 prediction, so the defended teacher's accuracy equals the undefended teacher's identically. Under this guarantee the distilled student still loses 17.4 percentage points on CIFAR-100, 29.6 on CIFAR-10, and 13.3 on Tiny-ImageNet; matching this degradation with the unmodified defense costs 27.5, 32.9, and 22.2 points of teacher accuracy. Because served labels are unchanged, a hard-label attacker gains nothing, while the defended soft output trains a student up to 29.7 points below that floor: the incentive to distill served probabilities is not merely removed but reversed. To our knowledge, ADS-C is the first antidistillation defense for classification whose utility cost is exactly zero.

</details>


### [97] [Deep Learning Approaches for Sleep Apnea Classification from Polysomnographic EEG Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15477)
*Shashank Manjunath,Mukesh Cheemakurthi,Aarti Sathyanarayana*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper compares deep learning architectures (Vision Transformers, Graph Attention Networks) and EEG feature representations for automated pediatric sleep apnea detection, achieving best AUC of 0.750 with Vision Transformer on topological data analysis features from 2,410 pediatric subjects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Polysomnography-based sleep apnea diagnosis is resource-intensive and requires manual analysis. EEG signals can capture central nervous system effects of apnea events, offering potential for automated screening, but prior work lacks comprehensive comparison across architectures and feature types on a unified pediatric dataset.

Method: Compared Vision Transformers and Graph Attention Networks across five EEG representations: raw temporal signals, STFT spectrograms, coherence-based graphs, and two topological data analysis (TDA) features. Trained on 2,410 age-sex matched pediatric subjects, tested on 575, with stratified analysis by age, sex, AHI severity, and sleep stages.

Result: Best test AUC of 0.750 achieved by Vision Transformer on TDA features. Significant performance variation observed across demographic groups and sleep stages (N1, N2, N3, REM).

Conclusion: EEG-based automated OSA screening is feasible but essential challenges for clinical deployment remain, including performance variation across patient demographics and sleep stages.

Abstract: Sleep apnea diagnosis via polysomnography remains resource intensive and relies on time consuming manual data analysis and scoring. Recent work has demonstrated that central nervous system effects of sleep apnea events can be detected through electroencephalogram (EEG) signals. However, most work uses a single feature type on various datasets combined with different classification algorithms. In this work, we present a comprehensive comparison of deep learning architectures and feature representations for automated sleep apnea detection from multichannel EEG on a single dataset of pediatric subjects. We evaluate Vision Transformers and Graph Attention Networks across distinct signal representations: raw temporal signals, short-time Fourier transform spectrograms, coherence based graphs, and two topological data analysis (TDA) derived features. Using age and sex matching of our train and test sets, we train on 2410 pediatric subjects and test on 575 pediatric subjects. We achieve a best test AUC of 0.750 using a vision transformer based model trained on TDA features. Stratified analysis across patient demographics (age, sex, AHI severity) and sleep stages (N1, N2, N3, REM) reveals significant performance variation. Our results demonstrate the feasibility of EEG based automated OSA screening while highlighting essential challenges for clinical deployment.

</details>


### [98] [Inpainting Insights: Elevating Visual XAI with Photorealistic Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15482)
*Josef Lindl,Mariana Chaves,Damien Garreau*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper improves perturbation-based XAI methods (specifically LIME) for image data by using generative inpainting to create photorealistic perturbed samples, avoiding unrealistic out-of-distribution artifacts that compromise explanation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional perturbation techniques for image XAI generate unrealistic out-of-distribution samples with visible artifacts, which can mislead models and compromise explanation quality.

Method: The authors adjust LIME by incorporating generative inpainting to produce photorealistic perturbed samples that better align with the original data distribution.

Result: The proposed approach generates photorealistic perturbed samples that align better with the original data distribution and enhance explanation quality.

Conclusion: Generative inpainting can improve perturbation-based explanations for images by producing more realistic samples that do not mislead the model.

Abstract: The increasing complexity of state-of-the-art machine learning models has made their behavior progressively harder to interpret, spurring rapid advancements in the field of eXplainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI). Among many methods proposed, perturbation-based approaches play a major role. By systematically altering (perturbing) input features, these approaches measure the impact on the model's predictions. For image data, traditional perturbation techniques, often involve replacing pixel values e.g., with a pre-defined color. However, such approaches, but also more refined deterministic techniques, generate unrealistic out-of-distribution samples and often leave visible artifacts, which can mislead the model and compromise explanation quality. In this work, we adjust LIME, a widely used perturbation-based method, to demonstrate how generative inpainting can improve perturbation-based explanations for images. We achieve photorealistic perturbed samples that align better with the original data distribution and enhance explanation quality.

</details>


### [99] [Diffusion models recover accurate mixture weights despite score function insensitivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15485)
*Andrew Dennehy,Ramchandran Muthukumar,Rebecca Willett,Nisha Chandramoorthy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Score-based generative models can cover all modes of a multimodal distribution but may fail to learn correct mixture weights (mode amplitudes). This paper resolves the paradox by showing that the diffusion score matching (DSM) loss relates to mixture weight estimation error, introduces the Diffusion Score Sensitivity Index (DSSI) to quantify sensitivity of the loss to parameter changes, proves that for Gaussian mixtures weight errors are on the same order as DSM loss, and shows that noise schedules affect sensitivity and mode amplification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the apparent paradox where score-based generative models cover all modes of a multimodal distribution yet fail to learn correct relative mode amplitudes (mixture weights), and to understand the relationship between diffusion score matching loss and mixture weight estimation accuracy.

Method: The authors relate the diffusion score matching (DSM) loss to mixture weight estimation error, define the Diffusion Score Sensitivity Index (DSSI) as the variation in DSM loss relative to parameter changes, prove formally for Gaussian mixtures in arbitrary dimensions that mixture weight errors are on the same order as DSM loss, and empirically study sensitivity emergence under typical noise schedules and how noise schedule choices affect sensitivity and mode amplification.

Result: The DSSI governs the accuracy of estimating target distribution parameters from generated samples. For Gaussian mixtures, mixture weight estimation errors are on the same order as DSM loss under mild conditions. Sensitivity emerges during the noising process of benchmark data distributions, and these sensitivity values predict how well a well-trained model recovers mixture weights. Noise schedule choices can reduce diffusion sensitivity, leading to mode amplification.

Conclusion: The proposed sensitivity framework (DSSI) governs the recovery of any qualitative parameter of the target distribution beyond just mixture weights, providing a theoretical foundation for understanding mode weight learning in score-based generative models and highlighting the role of noise schedules.

Abstract: Score-based generative models exhibit a puzzling behavior: they often appear to cover all modes of a target multimodal distribution and yet may fail to learn the correct relative mode amplitudes, which can be interpreted as mixture weights. We resolve this apparent paradox by relating the diffusion score matching (DSM) loss to the error in estimating mixture weights from generated samples. We show that, even when the target score is insensitive to mixture weights, generated samples can recover the weights accurately if the scores at intermediate noise levels are informative about the weights. Accordingly, we define the diffusion score sensitivity index (DSSI) as the variation in the DSM loss relative to changes in a parameter. We then show that the DSSI governs the accuracy with which the parameter of the target distribution can be estimated from generated samples. For Gaussian mixtures in arbitrary dimensions, we prove that the mixture weight estimation errors are on the same order as the DSM loss under mild conditions. Empirically, we show the emergence of sensitivity during the noising process of benchmark data distributions under typical noise schedules, and that these sensitivity values predict how well a well-trained model recovers mixture weights. Furthermore, we show that the choice of noise schedule can reduce diffusion sensitivity, leading to mode amplification. Although we focus on mixture weights, the proposed sensitivity framework governs the recovery of any qualitative parameter of the target distribution.

</details>


### [100] [An Auto-Scaling Approach for Serverless Environments Based on a Multi-Expert Consensus Mechanism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15511)
*Mobina Kashaniyan,Mehrdad Ashtiani,Amirhossein Ghassemi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A dependency-aware autoscaling framework for serverless computing that uses graph-based bottleneck identification, multi-model forecasting (MLP, LSTM, CNN) with Bayesian-inspired ensemble, and cost-aware scaling control, achieving 99.88% prediction accuracy and consistent cost reductions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Serverless computing faces autoscaling challenges due to dynamic workloads, cold-start latency, and function dependencies; existing solutions lack structural awareness of function dependency graphs and cost-optimized scaling decisions.

Method: Models serverless applications as directed dependency graphs, identifies critical functions via weighted degree centrality, predicts resource demand using MLP/LSTM/CNN ensemble with Bayesian model averaging, and employs a cost-aware controller with cold-start awareness for scale decisions.

Result: Supervised forecasting outperforms unsupervised clustering; ensemble achieves 99.88% prediction accuracy with lower error than hybrid baselines; consistent infrastructure cost savings across multiple cloud pricing models while meeting performance targets.

Conclusion: Combining dependency graph analysis, multi-expert forecasting ensemble, and cost-aware control yields a robust, practical solution for serverless autoscaling.

Abstract: Serverless computing provides automatic resource management and pay-per-use execution, but effective autoscaling remains challenging because of dynamic workloads, cold-start latency, and dependencies among functions. We present a dependency-aware autoscaling framework that integrates graph-based bottleneck identification, short-term workload forecasting, multi-model consensus, and cost-aware scaling control. Serverless applications are represented as directed dependency graphs, and structurally important functions are identified using weighted degree centrality. Resource demand is predicted using lightweight MLP, LSTM, and CNN models. Their outputs are combined through a performance-weighted probabilistic ensemble inspired by Bayesian model averaging. The controller further incorporates cold-start awareness and cost comparison to select among scale-up, scale-down, and hold actions. Experiments using real workload traces show that supervised forecasting substantially outperforms unsupervised clustering for autoscaling decision generation. The proposed ensemble achieves 99.88 percent prediction accuracy and reduces prediction error compared with representative hybrid forecasting methods. Evaluations across multiple cloud pricing models also demonstrate consistent infrastructure cost reductions while maintaining performance targets. The results show that combining dependency analysis, multi-expert forecasting, and cost-aware control provides a robust and practical solution for serverless autoscaling.

</details>


### [101] [Cache-Aware Prompt Compression:A Two-Tier Cost Model for LLM API Caching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15516)
*Yan Song*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies a conflict between prompt caching and query-aware prompt compression in LLM deployments (caching invalidated by per-query prefixes). It characterizes Anthropic's Sonnet cache as having a two-tier architecture with a hit rate plateau (~0.83) below 3,500 tokens. The proposed Cache-Aware Prompt Compression (CAPC) uses query-agnostic compression with cache control and tier-preserving ratio bounds, achieving the cheapest cost in 16/16 LongBench-v2 configurations (49% savings over cache-only, 64% over query-aware compression, 90% over vanilla) with quality within 0.05 of baseline. Validated on three production workloads including enterprise assistant, RAG pipeline, and tau-bench retail benchmark where CAPC matches vanilla reward while query-aware compression costs +40.1%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production LLM deployments commonly use both prompt caching (discounted rate for reused token prefixes) and prompt compression (fewer tokens). However, the existing compression literature standardizes on query-aware methods that produce different compressed prefixes per query, which mechanically invalidates the prefix-strict cache on every call. This conflict is not adequately addressed, and the literature assumes an ideal cache hit rate (rho=1.0) that does not reflect real-world deployments. The paper aims to characterize the real cost of this mismatch and develop a caching-aware compression strategy.
