Method: The authors empirically characterize Anthropic's Sonnet 4.6 API cache, finding a two-tier architecture with a sharp threshold near 3,500 tokens where hit rate plateaus at ~0.83 across 30-call sessions (far from rho=1.0). They develop a cost model predicting that under realistic rho, query-aware compression beats naive caching at high compression ratios (r>=6). They propose Cache-Aware Prompt Compression (CAPC), which pairs query-agnostic compression with explicit cache_control and a tier-preserving ratio bound that prevents over-compression from pushing the cached prefix into the hot tier. They validate CAPC on LongBench-v2 (16 configurations) and three production workloads: enterprise tool-using assistant, graphify knowledge-graph RAG pipeline (FastAPI and httpx codebases), and the tau-bench retail benchmark.

Result: On LongBench-v2, CAPC is the cheapest strategy in all 16/16 configurations, with mean savings of 49% over cache-only, 64% over query-aware compression, and 90% over vanilla, while maintaining quality within 0.05 of the uncompressed baseline. On enterprise assistant (94k-token schema prefix): 51.7% cost reduction at r=3. On RAG pipeline: 9.3x vs cache-all on FastAPI, 2.4x on httpx. On tau-bench retail: CAPC is cheapest of four strategies with reward exactly equal to vanilla (both 36/50, p=1.00), while query-aware compression is most expensive at +40.1% over vanilla — the first production confirmation of the crossover model's negative-ROI prediction on a public benchmark.

Conclusion: Cache-Aware Prompt Compression (CAPC) resolves the fundamental conflict between prompt caching and prompt compression by using query-agnostic compression with cache-aware design. It consistently achieves the lowest cost across diverse production workloads while maintaining quality. The results confirm that under realistic cache hit rates (rho<1.0), query-aware compression can yield negative ROI, and that explicitly designing for cache compatibility is crucial for cost-effective LLM deployment.

Abstract: Production LLM deployments combine two cost-reduction primitives: prompt caching (a discounted rate for re-used token prefixes) and prompt compression (fewer tokens sent). The compression literature has standardized on query-aware methods that produce a different compressed prefix per query, mechanically invalidating the prefix-strict cache on every call. We characterize this cost empirically on Anthropic's Sonnet 4.6 API and find caching is far from the rho=1.0 ideal the literature assumes: Sonnet's cache has a two-tier architecture with a sharp threshold near 3,500 tokens, below which the hit rate plateaus at rho~0.83 across 30-call sessions. Our cost model predicts, and experiments confirm, that under realistic rho, query-aware compression beats naive caching at high compression ratios (r>=6). We propose Cache-Aware Prompt Compression (CAPC), pairing query-agnostic compression with explicit cache_control plus a tier-preserving ratio bound that prevents over-compression from pushing the cached prefix into the hot tier. CAPC is the cheapest strategy in 16/16 configurations on LongBench-v2, with mean savings of 49% over cache-only, 64% over query-aware compression, and 90% over vanilla, at quality within 0.05 of the uncompressed baseline. We validate CAPC on three production workloads: an enterprise tool-using assistant with a 94k-token schema prefix (51.7% cost reduction at r=3); a graphify knowledge-graph RAG pipeline across two codebases (9.3x vs cache-all on FastAPI, 2.4x on httpx); and the public tau-bench retail benchmark (50 tasks), where CAPC is the cheapest of four strategies with reward exactly equal to vanilla (both 36/50, p=1.00) while query-aware compression is the most expensive at +40.1% over vanilla -- the first production confirmation of the crossover model's negative-ROI prediction on a public benchmark.

</details>


### [102] [Recursive Harness Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15524)
*Hyunin Lee,Jinglue Xu,Jeffrey Seely,Donghyun Lee,Matei Zaharia,Yujin Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RHI iteratively refines user-constructed agent harnesses (prompt-level specifications) using pairwise feedback, improving execution-trace quality and agent performance with low cost. On 30 ML research tasks, it matches or exceeds high-reasoning-effort agents while reducing inference cost by up to 60%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Under model-harness co-evolution, harnesses are data-generating components that shape future models. Optimizing them for both immediate performance and trace quality is desirable, but updating provider-built scaffolds is costly. The paper investigates whether lightweight, task-specific optimization of user-constructed harnesses can improve trace quality with few iterations.

Method: Recursive Harness Self-Improvement (RHI): represents the harness as a prompt-level agent-loop specification, iteratively refines it using pairwise feedback over its own revision history. Tested on 30 synthetic ML research tasks across quantitative finance, robotics, and pharmacy.

Result: A few RHI iterations substantially raise performance of low-reasoning-effort agents, exceeding maximum-reasoning-effort settings while reducing inference cost by up to 60%. Gains arise from improved task-specific context management via better inter-agent information flow, not longer reasoning traces.

Conclusion: RHI is a practical algorithm for continual learning within model-harness co-evolution, formalized as an information-theoretic hypothesis for its implicit optimization objective.

Abstract: Under model--harness co-evolution, harnesses are not merely inference-time scaffolds but data-generating components whose execution traces can shape future foundation models. This motivates harness-in-the-loop learning: optimizing harnesses for both immediate agent performance and the quality of traces used for future model training. However, continually updating provider-built scaffolds is costly and labor-intensive. We therefore investigate whether optimizing user-constructed harnesses in a task-specific manner can improve execution-trace quality while remaining computationally lightweight and requiring only a few update iterations. To this end, we introduce Recursive Harness Self-Improvement (RHI), which represents the harness as a prompt-level specification of the agent loop and iteratively refines it using pairwise feedback over its own revision history. Across 30 synthetic machine-learning research tasks spanning quantitative finance, robotics, and pharmacy, a few RHI iterations suffice to substantially raise the performance ceiling of low-reasoning-effort agents, exceeding the corresponding maximum-reasoning-effort setting while reducing inference cost by up to 60%. We show that these gains arise primarily from improved task-specific context management through more effective inter-agent information flow rather than longer reasoning traces. Finally, we formalize this behavior as an information-theoretic hypothesis for RHI's implicit optimization objective, suggesting RHI as a practical algorithm for continual learning within the paradigm of model--harness co-evolution.

</details>


### [103] [Kolmogorov--Arnold Networks for Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15525)
*Felippe Alves,Renato Vicente*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KANs replace fixed activations with learned edge functions, offering interpretability but showing no consistent advantage over MLP baselines across multiple benchmarks, with benefits largely from low-capacity settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test claims that Kolmogorov–Arnold Networks (KANs) provide interpretable interfaces and can serve as alternatives to transformer feed-forward networks.

Method: Reconstructed feed-forward edges in a 6-layer B-spline KAN; pruned low-activity edges; replicated on BabyLM; evaluated MLP, SwiGLU, Chebyshev, and GR-KAN networks; tested on BLiMP, EWoK, and Wikitext-103 benchmarks.

Result: 87.8% of edges exceed NLS>0.1, 0.4% inactive; pruning 20-25% causes negligible loss; small-basis KANs offer interpretability; but no consistent benchmark, quality, or latency advantage over MLP baselines.

Conclusion: Small-basis KANs provide a practical interface for auditing learned transformations, but tested replacements lack consistent advantage over strong MLP baselines.

Abstract: Kolmogorov--Arnold Networks (KANs) replace fixed node activations with learned one-dimensional edge functions, offering an explicit interface for interpretation and a possible alternative to transformer feed-forward networks. We test these claims separately. In a six-layer, 10M-parameter B-spline KAN, we reconstruct all 884,736 feed-forward edges: 87.8\% exceed (NLS>0.1) and 0.4\% are inactive. Pruning the lowest-activity 20--25\% causes negligible loss increase, although structured MLP neuron pruning tolerates comparable sparsity. The audit replicates on BabyLM, but grid-size sweeps show that near-total fPCA compression and high closed-form-fit coverage are properties of the low-capacity grid-2 basis, not universal KAN behavior. For replacement, we evaluate MLP, SwiGLU, grouped Chebyshev, and rational GR-KAN networks on BabyLM. The KAN-family and gated variants improve validation loss over the GELU MLP, but this ordering does not transfer to standardized benchmarks: across ten seeds and 59,875 BLiMP pairs, accuracies span 62.4--63.1\%, EWoK remains at chance, and a (+0.7)-point GR-KAN effect on BLiMP reverses on the supplement. Larger tests are also cautionary: parameter-matched MLPEdge underperforms the MLP on Wikitext-103, and 286M-parameter GR-KAN remains below a SwiGLU ClimbMix baseline after stabilization. Thus, small-basis KANs provide a practical, corpus-transferable interface for auditing learned scalar transformations, but the tested replacements show no consistent benchmark, quality, or latency advantage over strong MLP baselines.

</details>


### [104] [Publicly-Verifiable Certificates for Statistical Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15528)
*Michael Ngo,Michael P. Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Publicly-Verifiable Certificates of Statistical Validity (pvCSVs), a non-interactive proof system allowing learners to publish a hypothesis and certificate, enabling any user to efficiently verify the hypothesis's validity under their own distribution. For Adaptive SQ Algorithms, the certificate sample complexity scales with O(log k) versus Ω̃(√k) for learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extending interactive proofs of learning (Goldwasser et al., ITCS'21) to the non-interactive setting, aiming for public, distributionally-robust certification of learning algorithm validity.

Method: Define pvCSVs, then construct them for Adaptive Statistical Query (SQ) Algorithms; analyze sample complexity and study proof systems for learning in the SQ model.

Result: For k-adaptive SQ algorithms, pvCSVs achieve certificate sample complexity O(log k), whereas learning sample complexity is Ω̃(√k).

Conclusion: Non-interactive proofs of learning are feasible in the SQ model with significantly lower sample complexity for certification than for learning; the study also reveals strengths and limitations of SQ proof systems.

Abstract: Following Goldwasser, Rothblum, Shafer, and Yehudayoff, who defined a framework for interactive proofs of learning [ITCS'21], we initiate the study of non-interactive proofs of learning. We define and study a new notion: Publicly-Verifiable Certificates of Statistical Validity (pvCSVs), which allow for public, distributionally-robust certification that the result of a learning algorithm is valid. In a pvCSV, a learner publishes a hypothesis $h$ and corresponding certificate $π$; then, any user, who holds a user-specific distribution, can read the pair $(h,π)$ and determine efficiently whether the hypothesis is valid according to the user-specific distribution.
  We construct pvCSVs in the context of Adaptive Statistical Query (SQ) Algorithms. To certify SQ algorithms that makes $k$ adaptive queries, we construct pvCSVs where the sample complexity scales with $O(\log k)$, whereas the sample complexity of the best learning algorithms scale with $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{k})$. More generally, we study proof systems for learning in the SQ model, demonstrating the model's strengths as well as its limitations.

</details>


### [105] [From Feasibility to Desirability: Plan, Learn, Adapt (PLA) Framework for Personalized On-Device Itinerary Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15552)
*Himel Dev,Tanmoy Sen,Madhusudan Basak,Bashima Islam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PLA is a three-stage framework (Plan, Learn, Adapt) for personalized on-device itinerary generation that combines lightweight planners, a Bradley-Terry reward model learned from pairwise human comparisons, and feasibility-preserving local refinement. It achieves 67.8% win rate over single planners, 100% feasibility (vs. 0% for frontier LLMs), 67.6% cross-city reward accuracy, and 91% higher completion rates in production with ~110ms on-device latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical optimization enforces hard constraints but cannot capture subjective traveler preferences, while learning-based approaches model preferences but cannot guarantee feasibility. Mobile deployment adds further resource constraints, creating a need for a framework that jointly handles combinatorial feasibility, latent desirability, and on-device efficiency.

Method: A three-stage framework: (1) Plan — heterogeneous ensemble of lightweight planners generates structurally diverse feasible candidates; (2) Learn — fits a compact Bradley-Terry reward model from pairwise itinerary comparisons to capture emergent schedule properties (pacing, coherence, balance); (3) Adapt — feasibility-preserving local refinement within a device-aware compute budget, ensuring every intermediate state is feasible.

Result: On 2,519 pairwise human comparisons across 100+ U.S. cities: 67.8% win rate (11.2 pp above best single planner), 100% feasibility. Frontier LLMs (GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.5, Gemini 3 Pro) achieved 0% feasibility. Reward model: 67.6% leave-one-city-out accuracy. Production deployment in FlyEnJoy: 91% higher itinerary completion rates, 109.9 ms average on-device latency.

Conclusion: PLA demonstrates that combining diverse feasible planning with learned preferences and constrained refinement can simultaneously achieve high feasibility, strong preference alignment, and practical on-device deployment, outperforming both classical optimizers and large language models for personalized itinerary generation.

Abstract: Generating personalized trip itineraries is a complex planning task and involves a tension between hard combinatorial feasibility and soft latent desirability. Classical optimization enforces constraints but fails to capture subjective traveler preferences. While learning-based approaches model preferences, they cannot guarantee feasibility. Mobile deployment imposes additional resource constraints on both. To address this, we propose Plan, Learn, Adapt (PLA), a three-stage framework for personalized on-device itinerary generation. The Plan stage builds a heterogeneous ensemble of lightweight planners that produces structurally diverse feasible candidates. From pairwise itinerary comparisons, Learn fits a compact Bradley-Terry reward model that captures emergent schedule properties such as pacing, geographic coherence, and day balance, which per-POI signals miss. Finally, Adapt applies feasibility-preserving local refinement within a device-aware compute budget; every intermediate state is feasible by construction. On 2,519 pairwise human comparisons across more than 100 U.S. cities, the reward-guided ensemble achieves a 67.8% win rate, 11.2 percentage points above the best single planner, with 100% feasibility. Three frontier LLMs, GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.5, and Gemini 3 Pro, achieve 0% feasibility under the same constraints. The reward model generalizes across held-out cities, with a 67.6% mean leave-one-city-out accuracy. In production deployment within FlyEnJoy, PLA increased itinerary completion rates by 91%, with 109.9 ms average on-device latency.

</details>


### [106] [Hard Rules, Soft Preferences: Bridging Reasoning, Learning, and Optimization for Personalized Packing Checklist Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15562)
*Himel Dev,Madhusudan Basak,Tanmoy Sen,Paromita Shome,Bashima Islam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A reasoning-guided learning framework (symbolic engine → preference learner → CP-SAT optimizer) for generating personalized, regulation-compliant packing checklists, achieving 99.7% recall, 0.96 validity, 100% constraint satisfaction, and doubling completions in a production travel app.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Packing for air travel is recurring and error-prone; existing assistants are either template-driven (generic) or recommendation-driven (unconstrained), forcing users to manually address safety rules, item dependencies, and luggage limits.

Method: Three-stage pipeline: (1) symbolic engine for regulation-aware seed checklists with explicit dependencies, (2) two-stage preference learner (gradient-boosted trees + LambdaMART) that estimates inclusion/priority utilities from user actions while mitigating survivorship bias, (3) CP-SAT optimizer for compact compliant subset selection.

Result: On 604 trip scenarios (29K labels, 343K comparisons): symbolic engine 99.7% recall & 0.96 validity (vs. 0.78-0.81 for LLMs); preference learner AUC-ROC 0.943 & NDCG@5 0.923; CP-SAT 100% constraint satisfaction (vs. 28% greedy, 10% random). Production deployment doubled checklist completions, reduced editing and completion time.

Conclusion: The architecture instantiates a general pattern for constrained personalization where hard feasibility coexists with sparse preference signals, validated through both offline metrics and real-world production impact.

Abstract: Packing for air travel is recurring and error-prone: the checklist must be personal and context-aware, yet feasible under safety rules, item dependencies, and luggage limits. Existing packing assistants are template-driven and generic, or recommendation-driven but unconstrained, leaving users to manually patch regulatory and capacity violations. We propose a reasoning-guided learning framework with three stages: (1) a symbolic engine that generates a regulation-aware seed checklist with explicit dependency structure, (2) a two-stage preference learner that estimates inclusion and priority utilities from user add and remove actions while mitigating survivorship bias, and (3) a CP-SAT optimizer that selects a compact, compliant subset. The architecture instantiates a general pattern for constrained personalization, applicable wherever hard feasibility coexists with sparse preference signals. On 604 labeled trip scenarios, comprising 29K inclusion labels and 343K pairwise comparisons, the symbolic engine attains 99.7% recall and 0.96 rubric validity, compared with 0.78 to 0.81 for frontier LLMs. Gradient-boosted trees and LambdaMART reach an AUC-ROC of 0.943 and an NDCG@5 of 0.923. CP-SAT attains 100% constraint satisfaction, compared with 28% for greedy selection and 10% for random selection. Deployment in FlyEnJoy, a production iOS travel app, doubled checklist completions and reduced editing and completion time.

</details>


### [107] [Information-Directed Sampling for Causal Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15577)
*Muhammad Qasim Elahi,Murat Kocaoglu,Mahsa Ghasemi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper studies contextual causal bandits with non-manipulable variables, developing Thompson Sampling and Information-Directed Sampling algorithms that exploit causal structure to share information across interventions, with theoretical regret bounds and empirical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing causal bandits assume all variables are manipulable, but in many real-world applications some variables cannot be directly manipulated yet influence reward and provide useful information. The authors aim to handle these non-manipulable variables while still exploiting causal structure.

Method: Bayesian formulation with known causal graph, no latent confounding, and conditional probability tables as unknown parameters. Develops causal Thompson Sampling and Information-Directed Sampling with entropy-dependent sublinear regret bounds, including analysis of Monte Carlo approximation error for IDS.

Result: Entropy-dependent sublinear Bayesian regret bounds for both Thompson Sampling and IDS. For IDS, explicit quantification of Monte Carlo approximation error; exact computation recovers standard sublinear IDS rate. High-probability confidence bounds for Monte Carlo estimates. Experiments show outperformance over causal and non-causal baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed causal bandit methods effectively exploit shared information across interventions even with non-manipulable variables, outperforming baselines both theoretically and empirically.

Abstract: Causal bandits exploit structural relationships among variables to share information across interventions and accelerate the identification of high-reward decisions. In many applications, however, some variables cannot be directly manipulated, even though they influence the reward and provide useful information about the underlying causal system. We study contextual causal bandits with non-manipulable variables, where context variables are observed before action selection and additional variables are observed after each intervention. Assuming a known causal graph without latent confounding, we adopt a Bayesian formulation in which the conditional probability tables of the observational distribution constitute the unknown parameter. This representation allows observations collected under one intervention to update reward estimates for other interventions through their shared causal mechanisms. We develop causal variants of Thompson Sampling and Information-Directed Sampling (IDS) for this setting. For Thompson Sampling, we establish an entropy-dependent sublinear Bayesian regret bound. For IDS, we derive an entropy-dependent regret bound that explicitly quantifies the additional error introduced by Monte Carlo approximation of the expected regret and information gain; when these quantities are available exactly, the bound recovers the standard sublinear IDS rate. We further provide high-probability confidence bounds for the Monte Carlo estimates used by the algorithm. Experiments on several synthetic causal bandit tasks show that the proposed methods outperform causal and non-causal baselines by more effectively exploiting information shared across interventions.

</details>


### [108] [Rethinking Transfer in Continual Learning: A Replay-Based Realisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15587)
*Yang Meng,Zhenya Liu,Zhuokai Zhao,Yuxin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper argues continual learning should explicitly understand when forward transfer is possible before designing mechanisms. It proposes Transfer-Selective Replay (TSR), which selects replay data predicted to benefit the incoming task using a zero-training task signature, improving forward transfer while maintaining stability under low-budget continual learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing continual learning methods overwhelmingly focus on preventing catastrophic forgetting while ignoring forward transfer (past helping future), and when transfer should exist is not formally accounted for. The authors aim to understand the conditions under which transfer is possible before engineering transfer mechanisms.

Method: The paper proposes a framework of three measurable conditions for transfer: (1) target task has room for improvement beyond limited supervision, (2) transferable information survives continued optimization, (3) replay comes from compatible previous tasks. This is instantiated as Transfer-Selective Replay (TSR), which uses a zero-training task signature to select replay data predicted to benefit the incoming task, combined with distillation to preserve stability on previous tasks.

Result: Under the standard continual learning protocol in the low-budget regime, TSR consistently improves forward transfer while maintaining stability, outperforming existing replay baselines across both heterogeneous and homogeneous task streams.

Conclusion: Transfer should be treated as a first-class objective of continual learning, to be understood before it is engineered.

Abstract: Continual learning studies how deployed language models can continually acquire new tasks without expensive retraining from scratch. Existing methods, whether rehearsal-based (replaying stored past data) or rehearsal-free (regularising or isolating parameters), overwhelmingly target one objective: preventing catastrophic forgetting. Forward transfer, the past helping the future, has meanwhile been pursued almost exclusively through parameter reuse, with no explicit account of when transfer should be expected at all. We begin one step earlier: before designing a transfer mechanism, we ask when transfer should exist at all. We answer with a framework of three measurable conditions: the target task must leave room for improvement beyond its own limited supervision, transferable information must survive continued optimisation, and replay must come from compatible previous tasks. We instantiate this view as Transfer-Selective Replay (TSR), which selects replay data predicted to benefit the incoming task rather than replaying past examples indiscriminately. Selection is guided by a zero-training task signature, while distillation preserves stability on previous tasks. Under the standard continual learning protocol in the low-budget regime, TSR consistently improves forward transfer while maintaining stability, outperforming existing replay baselines across heterogeneous and homogeneous task streams. More broadly, the results argue for treating transfer as a first-class objective of continual learning, to be understood before it is engineered.

</details>


### [109] [Field-Aware RankMixer with Dual-Stream Bilinear Fusion for the Tencent UNI-REC Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15590)
*Yufeng Zhang,Zhengqi Xu,Jiajun Cui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents FA-RankMixer, a model for multi-domain user behavior modeling and pCVR prediction in the KDD Cup 2026 Tencent UNIREC Challenge, achieving 9th place. It uses target-aware DIN modules, separate modeling of recent/earlier interests, RankMixer blocks for cross-token interaction, and dual-stream bilinear fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenge of joint modeling of multi-domain user behavior sequences and non-sequential multi-field features for target-ad pCVR prediction in the KDD Cup 2026 Tencent UNIREC Challenge.

Method: FA-RankMixer with dual-stream bilinear fusion: (1) Target-aware DIN modules extract user interests from multiple behavior domains; (2) Separate modeling of recent and earlier interests for the longest behavior sequence; (3) Semantic tokens formed from feature fields and behavior domains, processed by RankMixer blocks for cross-token interaction; (4) Shallow MLP stream complements deep RankMixer stream; (5) Group-wise bilinear module fuses representations from both streams.

Result: Ranked ninth on the official leaderboard of the KDD Cup 2026 Tencent UNIREC Challenge.

Conclusion: The proposed FA-RankMixer with dual-stream bilinear fusion effectively handles multi-domain user behavior sequences and non-sequential multi-field features, achieving competitive performance in the challenge.

Abstract: This paper presents our solution to the KDD Cup 2026 Tencent UNIREC Challenge. The task requires joint modeling of multi-domain user behavior sequences and non-sequential multi-field features for target-ad pCVR prediction. We develop a Field-Aware RankMixer (FA-RankMixer) with dual-stream bilinear fusion. The model first applies target-aware DIN modules to extract user interests from multiple behavior domains. It also models recent and earlier interests separately for the longest behavior sequence. The model then forms semantic tokens based on feature fields and behavior domains and uses RankMixer blocks for cross-token interaction. A shallow MLP stream complements the deep RankMixer stream, and a group-wise bilinear module fuses their representations. Our final solution ranks ninth on the official leaderboard. Our code is available at https://github.com/PixelCookie-zyf/TAAC-2026-SeRankMixer.

</details>


### [110] [Do Generative Models Keep Time? A Time-Aware Evaluation of Synthetic Sequential Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15606)
*Kiwan Kwon,Kangmin Kim,Hojin Lee,Yeseong Jung,Hyeongwoo Kong,Vamsi K. Potluru,Saerom Park,Yongjae Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Synthetic sequential tabular data evaluation needs temporal fidelity measurement, not just pooled record distributions. The paper proposes a taxonomy-guided protocol that characterizes datasets along four properties (time representation, sampling regularity, trajectory dependence, schema linking) to determine which temporal evaluation dimensions apply, then measures timestamp validity, cross-sectional structure, dynamics, and time-varying relations across eight models and thirteen datasets, showing rankings differ substantially from conventional evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional tabular evaluation pools records into static distributions and is blind to temporal failures like backward timestamps, repeated timestamps, and unrealistic entity trajectories, yet synthetic sequential data is increasingly used for privacy-preserving sharing.

Method: A taxonomy-guided evaluation protocol where datasets are first characterized along four properties (time representation, sampling regularity, trajectory dependence, schema linking), which determine the applicable evaluation dimensions (timestamp validity, cross-sectional structure, within-entity dynamics, time-varying relational structure). Applied to eight generative models across thirteen datasets from six domains.

Result: Rankings under conventional evaluation disagree substantially with temporal evaluation rankings; failures are architecture-coherent rather than random.

Conclusion: Temporal fidelity must be measured on the time axis itself, not inferred from pooled record distributions.

Abstract: Synthetic sequential tabular data are increasingly used for privacy-preserving data sharing, yet a generator can reproduce every marginal and every foreign-key relationship while emitting timestamps that run backwards or repeat, and while sending entities along paths that no real entity followed. Conventional tabular evaluation, which pools records into static distributions, is blind to such failures. We present a taxonomy-guided evaluation protocol for temporal fidelity, in which the applicable measurements are determined by the data rather than fixed in advance. Each dataset is first characterized along four properties: how time is represented, whether observations are regularly sampled, whether trajectories are mutually dependent, and how the schema links entities to their histories. These properties determine which evaluation dimensions are meaningful. The protocol then measures timestamp validity, cross-sectional structure at aligned time points, within-entity dynamics, and time-varying relational structure, and recasts utility and privacy evaluation over trajectories rather than isolated rows. We apply the protocol to eight generative models across thirteen datasets spanning six domains. Rankings under conventional evaluation disagree substantially with those obtained under temporal evaluation, and the resulting failures are architecture-coherent rather than random. Temporal fidelity must therefore be measured on the time axis itself, rather than inferred from pooled record distributions.

</details>


### [111] [ASK-NN: An Asymmetric Nearest-Neighbor Test that detects Distribution Drifts in Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15607)
*Sergey Zakharov,Rodion Oblovatny,Alexey Zaytsev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes ASK-NN, an asymmetric two-sample test based on directed k-nearest-neighbor graphs, to detect distributional deviations between prompt and response hidden states in LLMs, useful for hallucination and artificial text detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hallucinations and artificial text in LLM outputs manifest as distributional deviations between prompt (reference) and response (query) hidden-state distributions. These asymmetries arise due to length differences, motivating the need for an asymmetric two-sample test.

Method: ASK-NN is a directed k-nearest-neighbor graph-based statistic that counts reference points whose nearest neighbor in the pooled sample is also a reference point. It provides exact finite-sample conditional mean and variance under permutation null, and asymptotic normality and consistency under fixed alternatives.

Result: ASK-NN is computationally effective and easy to implement. Empirically, it is competitive with kernel and graph-based baselines on synthetic benchmarks, artificial-text detection, and LLM hallucination detection from token-level hidden states.

Conclusion: ASK-NN offers a principled, computationally efficient asymmetric two-sample test for detecting distributional shifts in LLM outputs, with strong theoretical guarantees and competitive empirical performance.

Abstract: Hallucinations and artificial text in LLM-generated outputs often appear as distributional deviations between prompt and response hidden-state distributions. Since prompts or retrieved contexts typically serve as reference samples and responses as query samples, with major differences in length, these asymmetries motivate the use of change test statistics that treat the two samples differently. We consider an asymmetric two-sample test ASK-NN based on the directed k-nearest-neighbor graph. Our statistic counts reference points whose nearest neighbor in the pooled sample is also a reference point. Under the permutation null, it admits an exact finite-sample conditional mean and variance; we further establish asymptotic normality and consistency under fixed alternatives. ASK-NN is computationally effective and easy to implement. Empirically, it is competitive with kernel and graph-based baselines on synthetic benchmarks, artificial-text detection, and LLM hallucination detection from token-level hidden states.

</details>


### [112] [Neural Non-Equilibrium Hamiltonian Monte Carlo for Corrected Boltzmann Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15682)
*Moxian Qian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural Non-Equilibrium Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (NHMC) is a train-then-correct method that learns stochastic Hamiltonian-style proposals from a tractable base distribution, then corrects them using non-equilibrium work to sample from unnormalized Boltzmann densities, with applications to double-well and lattice φ⁴ targets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling from unnormalized Boltzmann densities is challenging because proposals must move probability mass globally while retaining path-probability information for statistical correction.

Method: NHMC learns stochastic Hamiltonian-style paths from a base distribution toward the target. After training, the learned proposal generates paths and endpoints, which are statistically corrected using the dimensionless generalized work—the probability ratio between forward and reverse paths. The same work quantity enables path-SNIS, normalizing constant estimation, and path-IMH. A shared-bridge round-trip Metropolis kernel acting on configurations is also defined.

Result: On double-well and finite-volume lattice φ⁴ targets, NHMC provides corrected estimates when path overlap is sufficient; poor overlap reveals weight degeneracy, low acceptance, and long autocorrelation. A molecular internal-coordinate feasibility study using an MD prior and learned-force path proposal is also reported.

Conclusion: NHMC's effectiveness depends on path overlap quality; the framework unifies training, importance sampling, and Metropolis-Hastings correction within a single non-equilibrium work formalism.

Abstract: Sampling from an unnormalized Boltzmann density requires proposals that move probability mass globally while retaining enough path-probability information for statistical correction. We introduce Neural Non-Equilibrium Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (NHMC), a train-then-correct learned Hamiltonian sampler. Starting from a tractable base distribution, NHMC learns stochastic Hamiltonian-style paths toward the target. Once training is complete, the learned proposal parameters are fixed; the proposal then generates complete paths and endpoint configurations, which are statistically corrected using the recorded non-equilibrium work. This dimensionless generalized work is determined by the probability ratio between the forward proposal path and a reverse reference path. During training, minimizing its mean reduces a path-space KL divergence and controls an upper bound on endpoint mismatch. During evaluation, the same quantity defines weights for self-normalized importance sampling on paths (path-SNIS), estimates normalizing constants or free-energy differences, and gives the acceptance ratio for path-space independent Metropolis-Hastings (path-IMH). The same forward-reverse laws also define a shared-bridge round-trip Metropolis kernel that acts directly on configurations and preserves the Boltzmann target. On double-well and finite-volume lattice $φ^4$ targets, the NHMC construction gives corrected estimates when path overlap is sufficient; when overlap is poor, weight degeneracy, low acceptance, and long autocorrelation expose proposal failure. We additionally report a molecular internal-coordinate feasibility study using an MD prior and learned-force path proposal.

</details>


### [113] [Toward Federated Multimodal Graph Foundation Models: A Topology-Aware Multimodal Alignment Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15687)
*Xunkai Li,Guohao Fu,Yuming Ai,Zhengyu Wu,Hongchao Qin,Rong-Hua Li,Guoren Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedGAMMA is a federated learning framework for multimodal-attributed graphs that addresses both multimodal graph learning and federated constraints, using a two-stage pre-training and prompt-based fine-tuning approach with semantic-structural alignment, achieving state-of-the-art results across 12 datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal-attributed graphs (MAGs) are widespread but fragmented across privacy-restricted silos, requiring collaborative training without exposing raw data. Existing methods handle either multimodal graph learning or federated learning, but not both simultaneously.

Method: FedGAMMA formulates federated multimodal graph foundation learning as a two-stage semantic-structural alignment problem: (1) Pre-training with a shared-private semantic enhancer (using optimal transport for cross-modal alignment), a topology-aware graph fusion module (decoupling semantic and structural views via semantic residual graphs and dual positional encodings), and a dual-channel affinity-aware aggregation mechanism (estimating client similarity from feature/graph centroids without raw data exposure). (2) Fine-tuning with lightweight graph-aware prompts, a shared prompt pool with controlled exploration, and channel-wise prompt synchronization.

Result: Outperformed baselines across 12 multimodal graph datasets on downstream tasks with gains up to 12.96%; further outperformed baselines on multi-domain datasets under few-shot learning scenarios with gains up to 5.71%.

Conclusion: FedGAMMA effectively bridges multimodal graph learning and federated learning, enabling privacy-preserving collaborative training on fragmented MAGs while achieving superior performance across diverse tasks and datasets.

Abstract: Multimodal-attributed graphs (MAGs), whose nodes carry modalities such as images and text alongside topological structure, now pervade applications including social platforms, e-commerce, and biomedical networks, offering richer semantic signals than single-modality graphs. In practice, such graphs are fragmented across privacy-restricted silos owned by different platforms and institutions, so learning a broadly transferable model over them demands collaborative training that never exposes raw data. This places the task at the intersection of multimodal graph learning and federated learning, yet existing methods cover only one side of it. To address the challenges from these two perspectives, we propose FedGAMMA, casting federated multimodal graph foundation learning as a two-stage semantic-structural alignment problem of federated pre-training and prompt-based fine-tuning. During pre-training, a shared-private semantic enhancer disentangles cross-modal commonality from modality-specific information, aligning it through optimal transport, a topology-aware graph fusion module decouples semantic and structural views via semantic residual graphs and dual positional encodings, and a dual-channel affinity-aware aggregation mechanism estimates client similarity from feature and graph centroids without exposing raw data. During fine-tuning, FedGAMMA adapts the pretrained encoder through lightweight graph-aware prompts, a shared prompt pool with controlled exploration, and channel-wise prompt synchronization. Experiments on twelve multimodal graph datasets show FedGAMMA consistently surpassing a broad range of baselines across downstream tasks, with gains of up to 12.96%. FedGAMMA further outperforms competitive baselines accross multi-domain datasets on multiple tasks with up to 5.71% under few-shot learning scenario.

</details>


### [114] [A Benchmark for Electrical Load Forecasting Across Grid Levels: Time-Series Transformers Outperform Established Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15705)
*Matthias Hertel,Sebastian Pütz,Jonathan Kolar,Benjamin Schäfer,Ralf Mikut,Veit Hagenmeyer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks load forecasting methods across three grid levels (TSO control area, low-voltage feeders, individual consumers), finding Transformer-based models consistently outperform established methods by 6.6-10.7%. The standard Transformer outperforms YAformer (a flexible architecture integrating prior modifications), and the foundation model Chronos-2 shows competitive zero-shot performance but fails on special events. Ablations highlight the importance of long input contexts, covariates, and continuous retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate load forecasting at multiple grid levels is essential for smart grid operations including supply-demand balancing, demand-side management, and energy management systems, yet a comprehensive benchmark across grid levels with consistent evaluation is lacking.

Method: The paper presents a benchmark across three datasets (TSO control area, low-voltage feeders, individual consumers), evaluates ten short-term load forecasting methods, introduces YAformer as a flexible Transformer architecture with hyperparameter optimization, and compares against the Chronos-2 foundation model with ablation studies on input contexts, covariates, and retraining.

Result: Transformer-based approaches outperform established methods with 6.6-10.7% error reduction. The standard Transformer outperforms YAformer despite YAformer's architectural modifications. Chronos-2 shows competitive zero-shot performance on two datasets but fails on special events in TSO data.

Conclusion: Standard Transformer architectures are sufficient for accurate load forecasting; architectural modifications from prior work are not necessary. Long input contexts, covariates, and continuous retraining are critically important but often overlooked aspects in time-series forecasting.

Abstract: Accurate load forecasting at multiple grid levels is essential for future smart grids, ranging from aggregated control area forecasts for balancing supply and demand to forecasts of individual end-consumer loads for demand-side management and energy management systems. We present a comprehensive benchmark for load forecasting across grid levels, comprising three datasets that represent a transmission system operator control area, low-voltage grid feeders, and individual end consumers. We evaluate ten methods for short-term load forecasting and find that Transformer-based approaches consistently outperform established methods, reducing forecast error by 6.6-10.7 %. To analyze the impact of architectural design, we introduce YAformer, a flexible Transformer architecture that integrates modifications from prior work and is optimized via hyperparameter optimization. However, the standard Transformer achieves superior performance, suggesting that these architectural modifications are not required for accurate load forecasting. We further evaluate the Transformer-based time-series foundation model Chronos-2, which demonstrates competitive zero-shot performance on two datasets but fails to accurately capture special events in the TSO data. Detailed analyses reveal model-specific strengths and weaknesses, and ablation studies highlight the importance of long input contexts, covariates and continuous retraining - aspects that are often overlooked in the time-series forecasting literature.

</details>


### [115] [CardioMeta: Calibrated Multi-Task Prediction of Diabetes, Hypertension, and Cardiovascular Disease Across Population and EHR Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15721)
*S M Asif Hossain,Ruksat Khan Shayoni,M. F. Mridha,Jungpil Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CardioMeta is a calibrated multi-task framework for joint prediction of diabetes, hypertension, and cardiovascular disease that emphasizes leakage control, calibration, and reliability reporting across NHANES and MIMIC-IV datasets, achieving macro-AUROC 0.839 and ECE 0.024 in temporal validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ML studies for chronic disease prediction overemphasize single-dataset discrimination while underreporting label leakage, calibration, temporal robustness, external transportability, and subgroup reliability.

Method: CardioMeta uses a shared cardiometabolic encoder with disease-specific gated heads and post-hoc probability calibration. It excludes disease-defining variables from corresponding prediction heads to reduce circular label reconstruction, and evaluates on NHANES (temporal validation) and MIMIC-IV (domain shift).

Result: In leakage-reduced temporal validation: macro-AUROC 0.839, macro-AUPRC 0.536, macro-F1 0.614, ECE 0.024. External MIMIC-IV evaluation showed degradation under domain shift, partially recovered by limited fine-tuning.

Conclusion: The principal value of multi-task cardiometabolic modeling lies in reproducible leakage control, calibrated probabilities, and transparent reliability reporting rather than inflated accuracy.

Abstract: Cardiometabolic diseases remain among the most persistent drivers of preventable morbidity because diabetes, hypertension, and cardiovascular disease frequently co-occur and share metabolic, vascular, demographic, and behavioral determinants. Existing machine learning studies for chronic disease prediction often emphasize discrimination on a single dataset, while underreporting label leakage, calibration, temporal robustness, external transportability, and subgroup reliability. This paper presents CardioMeta, a calibrated multi-task framework for joint prediction of diabetes, hypertension, and cardiovascular disease across population survey and electronic health record (EHR) data. The study uses NHANES for population-level model development and temporal validation, and MIMIC-IV for EHR-domain evaluation under substantial distribution shift. To reduce circular label reconstruction, the primary analysis excludes disease-defining variables from the corresponding prediction heads, while a full-clinical feature setting is retained only as sensitivity analysis. CardioMeta combines a shared cardiometabolic encoder with disease-specific gated heads and post-hoc probability calibration. In the leakage-reduced temporal validation setting, the model achieved a macro-AUROC of 0.839, macro-AUPRC of 0.536, macro-F1 of 0.614, and expected calibration error of 0.024, with modest but consistent improvements over strong gradient-boosting and neural tabular baselines. External evaluation on MIMIC-IV showed clear degradation under domain shift, while limited fine-tuning partially recovered performance. The findings indicate that the principal value of multi-task cardiometabolic modeling lies not in inflated accuracy, but in reproducible leakage control, calibrated probabilities, and transparent reliability reporting across heterogeneous healthcare data sources.

</details>


### [116] [Learning Faster without Deeper Networks: A*-Inspired Batch Selection for Efficient CNN Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15745)
*Anxhelo Shehu,Enes Stastoli,Arben Cela*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A*-BS is a lightweight, model-agnostic batch selection strategy that treats mini-batch scheduling as a heuristic search problem, using an A*-like score combining loss-based difficulty and reuse penalty to improve convergence and learning signal. On MedMNIST-v2, it enables a simple 2.25×10⁵ parameter model to outperform ResNet-18/50 on half the tasks (up to +15%), trains faster, and beats random shuffling on all 12 tasks under identical settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Random mini-batch shuffling in CNNs causes slower convergence and diminishing learning signals as many samples become easy early in training, leaving gradient updates uninformative.

Method: A*-Inspired Batch Selection (A*-BS) formulates mini-batch scheduling as a heuristic search: each batch is a node scored by an A*-like function combining a loss-based difficulty measure and a reuse penalty to encourage diverse, informative gradient updates throughout training.

Result: On 12 MedMNIST-v2 tasks, a lightweight CNN (~2.25×10⁵ params) with A*-BS outperforms ResNet-18/50 on 6 tasks (up to +15% gains), outperforms random shuffling on all 12 tasks under identical settings, and trains substantially faster than ResNet baselines in wall-clock time.

Conclusion: Intelligent batch ordering can partially compensate for reduced architectural complexity, offering a computationally efficient alternative to deeper models, with reliability confirmed even against more sophisticated architectures.

Abstract: Common practice when training Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) is to use randomly shuffled mini-batches. This creates two limitations: slower convergence, and a diminishing learning signal, since many samples are quickly classified as easy during training. We address these inefficiencies with A*-Inspired Batch Selection (A*-BS), a lightweight, model-agnostic strategy that formulates mini-batch scheduling as a heuristic search problem. Each batch is treated as a node in a search space and ranked using an A*-like score combining a loss-based difficulty measure with a reuse penalty. This encourages informative gradient updates and batch diversity throughout training, without modifying network architectures or optimization algorithms, so it integrates seamlessly into existing pipelines. We evaluate A*-BS on the twelve 2D classification tasks of the MedMNIST-v2 benchmark, using a deliberately simple architecture of approximately 2.25x10^5 parameters, compared against the ResNet-18 and ResNet-50 baselines reported by the benchmark. On half of these tasks, the lightweight model with A*-BS reaches higher accuracy and AUC than both ResNet baselines, with relative gains of up to 15%. An ablation under identical architecture and hyperparameters shows A*-BS outperforms random batch shuffling on all twelve tasks. Wall-clock measurements further show the lightweight CNN with A*-BS trains substantially faster than ResNet-18 and ResNet-50 on identical hardware. These results indicate that intelligent batch ordering can partially compensate for reduced architectural complexity, offering a computationally efficient alternative to deeper models, with reliability reinforced by strong performance even against deeper, more sophisticated architectures.

</details>


### [117] [Trainable Spline Representations for Physics-Informed Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15751)
*Giovanni Canali,Nicola Demo,Gianluigi Rozza*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PI-Splines replaces neural networks with tensor-product B-spline expansions for physics-informed learning, offering compact support, analytical derivatives, explicit smoothness control, and strong boundary condition imposition, demonstrating competitive and stable performance on benchmark problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations of Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) such as lack of interpretability, costly derivatives, and difficulty enforcing boundary conditions, by proposing a structured spline-based architecture that preserves the residual-based training paradigm.

Method: The unknown field is parametrized via a tensor-product B-spline expansion with trainable control coefficients, replacing the neural network in the physics-informed learning framework. Boundary conditions can be strongly imposed by fixing appropriate boundary control coefficients. The method is compared against standard PINNs under matched conditions (governing equations, collocation points, loss terms, optimizers).

Result: PI-Splines provide competitive and stable performance compared to neural-based physics-informed architectures on several benchmark problems, particularly excelling in scenarios requiring structured representations, locality, and parameter efficiency.

Conclusion: PI-Splines are a viable and advantageous alternative to neural networks for physics-informed learning when structured, interpretable, and parameter-efficient representations are desired.

Abstract: This work introduces Physics-Informed Splines (PI-Splines), a structured spline-based architecture for physics-informed learning. Instead of representing the solution of a differential equation with a neural network, PI-Splines directly parametrize the unknown field through a tensor-product B-spline expansion with trainable control coefficients. This formulation preserves the residual-based training paradigm of Physics-Informed Neural Networks while providing compact support, explicit smoothness control, analytical derivatives, and a direct geometric interpretation of the trainable parameters. When compatible with the spline representation, boundary conditions can be imposed strongly by fixing suitable boundary control coefficients. The proposed method is evaluated on several benchmark problems of increasing difficulty and compared with standard physics-informed frameworks under matched governing equations, collocation sets, loss terms, and optimization procedures, so as to isolate the effect of the approximation architecture. Numerical experiments show that PI-Splines provide a competitive and stable alternative to neural physics-informed architectures, particularly in settings where structured representations, locality, and parameter efficiency are desirable.

</details>


### [118] [CoG-Guided Weight Correction for Fault-Tolerant Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15753)
*Bahram Parchekani,Samira Nazari,Ali Azarpeyvand,Mohammad Hasan Ahmadilivani,Tara Ghasempouri,Jaan Raik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Center of Gravity (CoG) guided weight correction method to detect and correct hardware-induced weight faults in DNNs without retraining. It achieves significant fault tolerance improvements (up to 230x on LSTM networks and 49.55x on CNNs) at BER of 10^{-3} with negligible accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DNNs in safety-critical applications are vulnerable to hardware and memory faults that corrupt network weights and degrade reliability, necessitating a method to tolerate such faults without retraining or architectural modification.

Method: A Center of Gravity (CoG) guided weight correction method that restores faulty weights based on their spatial characteristics within each layer using distance-aware correction rules, without retraining or architectural changes.

Result: Fault tolerance improvements of up to 230x and 6.41x on LSTM-based networks (StageNet, MTFNet) and up to 49.55x and 20.79x on CNNs (ResNet-18, VGG-16) at BER of 10^{-3}, with negligible accuracy loss.

Conclusion: This is the first work to apply the CoG concept to neural network weight tensors for enhancing model reliability, demonstrating effective and efficient fault tolerance across both RNN and CNN architectures.

Abstract: Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) used in safety-critical applications are vulnerable to hardware and memory faults that corrupt network weights and degrade reliability. In this paper, we propose a Center of Gravity (CoG) guided weight correction method that restores faulty weights based on their spatial characteristics within each layer. The proposed approach detects and corrects weight faults using distance-aware correction rules, eliminating the need for retraining or architectural modification. The effectiveness of the proposed method in terms of the capability of tolerating hardware faults has been evaluated through performing fault injection at different Bit Error Rates (BERs).
  Experiments on safety-critical LSTM-based Networks, including StageNet for disease progression tracking and MTFNet for cardiac anomaly detection, demonstrate fault tolerance improvements of up to 230x and 6.41x, respectively, at a BER of 10^{-3}, with negligible accuracy loss. When extended to Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), the method achieves up to 49.55x and 20.79x improvements under comparable fault conditions on ResNet-18 and VGG-16, respectively. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work to apply the CoG concept to neural network weight tensors for enhancing model reliability.

</details>


### [119] [From Diffusion to Reaction-Diffusion: A Dynamical-Systems View of Oversmoothing in Hypergraph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15773)
*Zhiheng Zhou,Mengyao Zhou,Yancheng Chen,Dengyi Zhao,Xingqin Qi,Guiying Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates hypergraph oversmoothing from a dynamical-systems perspective, proposing a reaction-diffusion framework (HNRD) that introduces a reaction mechanism to compensate diffusion-induced dissipation and prevent representation collapse in deep hypergraph neural networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Higher-order couplings in hypergraph neural networks (HGNNs) enhance expressive power but intensify representation collapse (oversmoothing) in deep propagation due to strong multi-way feature mixing. Existing methods lack a principled dynamical framework to address this issue.

Method: The authors interpret message passing as an incidence-level diffusion process using hypergraph gradient and divergence operators. They analyze pure diffusion's exponential contraction of null-mode-free components and Dirichlet energy dissipation. Motivated by this, they propose HNRD, which adds a reaction mechanism acting on the transverse component to compensate diffusion-induced dissipation. Global well-posedness is established, and a forward-Euler discretization yields a practical HNRD layer with stability conditions.

Result: HNRD consistently outperforms representative hypergraph baselines on benchmark and synthetic heterophilic hypergraphs. Depth, robustness, and efficiency analyses show HNRD preserves stable performance and nonzero Dirichlet energy under deep propagation and perturbations.

Conclusion: The work provides a principled dynamical framework for designing deep hypergraph architectures that maintain higher-order expressiveness without representation collapse.

Abstract: Higher-order couplings enhance the expressive power of hypergraph neural networks (HGNNs), but they also intensify representation collapse in deep propagation due to strong multi-way feature mixing. This work investigates hypergraph oversmoothing from a dynamical-systems perspective and develops a reaction--diffusion framework for depth-resistant hypergraph learning. By defining hypergraph gradient and divergence operators, we interpret message passing as an incidence-level diffusion process. The analysis of pure diffusion shows that its continuous semiflow exponentially contracts the null-mode-free component of node representations and drives the Dirichlet energy to zero, revealing hypergraph oversmoothing as an intrinsic transverse-energy dissipation phenomenon. Motivated by this analysis, we propose Hypergraph Neural Reaction--Diffusion (HNRD), which introduces a reaction mechanism acting on the transverse component to compensate diffusion-induced dissipation and stabilize discriminative variations. We establish global well-posedness of the proposed dynamics and prove that the null-mode-free Dirichlet energy remains bounded away from zero. A forward-Euler discretization provides a practical HNRD layer with a stability condition for deep propagation. Experiments on benchmark and synthetic heterophilic hypergraphs demonstrate that HNRD consistently improves over representative hypergraph baselines. Depth, robustness, and efficiency analyses further show that HNRD preserves stable performance and nonzero Dirichlet energy under deep propagation and perturbations. These results provide a principled dynamical framework for designing deep hypergraph architectures that maintain higher-order expressiveness without representation collapse.

</details>


### [120] [Scaling Time Series Classification via XAI-Driven Data Reduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15774)
*Davide Italo Serramazza,Thach Le Nguyen,Georgiana Ifrim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: drXAI repurposes XAI attribution methods to perform data reduction for time series classification, achieving 80–90% feature reduction while maintaining accuracy and enabling resource-heavy models like ConvTran to scale to previously infeasible datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: XAI for time series has advanced algorithmically, but its practical utility for improving downstream task performance (e.g., scalability) remains under-explored. State-of-the-art TSC models like Transformers have quadratic complexity w.r.t. sequence length and linear complexity w.r.t. channels, making them computationally prohibitive for massive datasets.

Method: drXAI uses a fast, GPU-accelerated Hydra classifier to generate local attributions, aggregates them into global feature importance scores, and applies an automated elbow-cut heuristic for threshold-free salient feature selection. This reduces the dataset dimensionality while preserving classification-relevant information.

Result: On synthetic benchmarks, drXAI recovers ground-truth features where traditional baselines fail. On real-world data, it achieves 80–90% data reduction with classification accuracy comparable to full-dataset models. drXAI enables ConvTran to scale to datasets previously inaccessible due to memory constraints.

Conclusion: XAI can serve not only as an interpretability tool but also as a robust feature selection and scalability method for time series analysis, demonstrated by drXAI's ability to dramatically reduce data size while maintaining model performance.

Abstract: Explainable AI (XAI) for time series has seen significant algorithmic growth, but its utility in providing measurable performance gains for downstream tasks remains under-explored. This paper bridges this gap by introducing drXAI, a novel methodology that repurposes XAI attribution methods for effective data reduction in Time Series Classification (TSC). The core challenge in modern TSC is scalability; state-of-the-art models, such as Transformers, exhibit quadratic complexity relative to sequence length and linear complexity relative to the number of channels. This renders them computationally prohibitive for massive datasets. drXAI addresses this by using a fast, GPU-accelerated classifier (Hydra) to generate local attributions. We aggregate these into global feature importance scores and employ an automated elbow-cut heuristic to select the most salient features without requiring manual thresholds.
  We evaluate our approach on both synthetic and real-world univariate and multivariate datasets. On synthetic benchmarks, drXAI successfully recovers ground-truth features where traditional baselines fail. On real-world data, drXAI achieves between 80% and 90% data reduction while maintaining classification accuracy comparable to models trained on the full dataset. Most importantly, we show that drXAI allows resource-intensive models like ConvTran to scale to datasets that were previously inaccessible due to memory constraints. Our results show the benefits of using XAI not just for interpretability, but as a robust tool for feature selection and scalability in time series analysis. All our code and data are openly available.

</details>


### [121] [AquaAugmentor: A Novel Feature Augmentation Algorithm for Water Potability Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15775)
*Muntasir Tabasum,Al Zadid Sultan Bin Habib,Tanpia Tasnim,Md. Ekramul Islam,Md Younus Ahamed,Md Asif Bin Syed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces AquaAugmentor, a feature augmentation algorithm to improve machine learning and deep learning models for classifying water potability using chemical attributes, and evaluates performance gains in accuracy and AUC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurately classifying water quality is challenging due to complexity and variability of water source data; improving predictive performance is essential for health, economic development, and sustainability.

Method: A novel feature augmentation algorithm called AquaAugmentor is proposed to enhance predictive performance of ML/DL models on low-dimensional datasets. Models are evaluated with and without AquaAugmentor using water chemical attributes (pH, hardness, solids, chloramines, sulfate, etc.) and compared based on test accuracy and AUC score.

Result: The results highlight the strengths and limitations of AquaAugmentor, showing improved predictive performance in water potability classification based on test accuracy and AUC metrics.

Conclusion: The study contributes to ensuring safe water access and provides a framework for using machine learning in environmental quality assessments, aiding researchers, policymakers, and public health officials in informed decision-making.

Abstract: Access to potable water is crucial for health, economic development, and sustainability. However, accurately classifying water quality remains a significant challenge due to the complexity and variability of water source data. This paper addresses the challenge of predicting water potability through machine learning and deep learning algorithms. It introduces a novel feature augmentation algorithm, AquaAugmentor, to enhance the predictive performance of these models for low-dimensional datasets. Utilizing a dataset that includes chemical attributes of water, such as pH, hardness, solids, chloramines, sulfate, and others. This study evaluates the performance of the models with and without AquaAugmentor. Each model applied to classify water as potable or non-potable and its performance is then evaluated and compared based on test accuracy and AUC score. The results highlight the strengths and limitations of our proposed algorithm, providing insights into the most effective techniques for improving the predictive performance of water quality classification. This study contributes to the broader efforts of ensuring safe water access and serves as a framework for employing machine learning in environmental quality assessments. The findings aim to assist researchers, policymakers, and public health officials in making informed decisions based on reliable machine learning predictions.

</details>


### [122] [Knowledge-Assisted Multi-Graph Dependency Learning for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection in Multi-Stage Industrial Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15799)
*Jaeyeong Lee,Taeseong Yoon,Wonmo Koo,Heeyoung Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A knowledge-assisted multi-graph framework for multivariate time series anomaly detection in multi-stage industrial processes, using three complementary graphs (data-driven and process-knowledge-refined) with a multi-graph attention network to improve dependency modeling and anomaly detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GNN-based MTAD methods often overlook critical process knowledge, and even when considered, seamlessly incorporating it into models remains challenging, leading to suboptimal anomaly detection performance.

Method: The framework constructs three complementary graphs: one purely data-driven and two refined by integrating structural constraints from process knowledge, then employs a multi-graph attention network to leverage these graphs for accurate anomaly detection.

Result: Experiments on two real-world multi-stage industrial datasets show that incorporating process knowledge substantially enhances anomaly detection performance.

Conclusion: Explicitly incorporating process knowledge into graph learning via a multi-graph framework improves dependency modeling and anomaly detection in multi-stage industrial processes.

Abstract: Industrial processes often generate complex, interdependent time-series data from multiple sensors across multiple stages, forming complex dependencies among variables and process stages. Effective monitoring and timely anomaly detection of these time series through multivariate time series anomaly detection (MTAD) is crucial for preventing failures and ensuring the reliability of automated systems. Graph neural networks (GNNs) have advanced MTAD by leveraging data-driven graphs to model complex dependencies among variables, effectively capturing relational structures within multivariate time series to enhance anomaly detection performance. However, existing GNN-based approaches often overlook critical process knowledge, and even when this knowledge is considered, seamlessly incorporating it into existing models remains inherently challenging, leading to suboptimal performance. To address this limitation, we propose a knowledge-assisted multi-graph framework for modeling sensor dependencies in multi-stage industrial processes for MTAD, which explicitly incorporates process knowledge into graph learning to enhance dependency modeling and improve anomaly detection performance. Our method constructs three complementary graphs: one purely data-driven and two refined by integrating structural constraints derived from process knowledge. To effectively leverage these graphs for anomaly detection, we employ a multi-graph attention network, enabling a more accurate and robust representation of complex dependencies. Comprehensive experiments on two real-world, multi-stage industrial datasets demonstrate that incorporating process knowledge substantially enhances anomaly detection performance.

</details>


### [123] [QUADS: Stabilizing NVFP4 Reinforcement Learning for MoE via QUantization-error Alignment across Dual Sides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15810)
*Zhengyang Zhuge,Hao Yu,Xin Wang,Zheng Li,Yizhong Cao,Dayiheng Liu,Jianwei Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies that directly applying NVFP4 (4-bit floating-point) to Mixture-of-Experts RL rollout causes training collapse due to activation quantization errors, and proposes QUADS (Quantization-error Alignment Across Dual Sides)—combining Asymmetric Quantization-Aware Training on the trainer side and Residual Activation Compensation on the rollout side—to stabilize training, achieving BF16-level accuracy with ~16% higher throughput than FP8.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rollout generation is a major bottleneck in RL for MoE LLMs, motivating low-precision acceleration (e.g., FP8). The emerging NVFP4 format offers higher throughput but directly applying it to MoE RL rollout is impractical due to training collapse from activation quantization errors.

Method: QUADS (QUantization-error Alignment across Dual Sides): (1) On the trainer side, Asymmetric Quantization-Aware Training that fake-quantizes weights while keeping activations unquantized. (2) On the rollout side, Residual Activation Compensation that corrects high-error activation channels while maintaining native W4A4 GEMMs.

Result: QUADS achieves BF16-level accuracy, improves average pass@1 by 21.49 points over naive NVFP4 RL, and delivers ~16% higher rollout throughput than FP8 across several MoE RL benchmarks.

Conclusion: Activation error, not weight error, is the dominant source of FP4 RL instability. QUADS effectively stabilizes NVFP4 RL for MoE by aligning quantization errors on both trainer and rollout sides, enabling practical low-precision acceleration.

Abstract: Rollout generation is a major bottleneck in Reinforcement Learning (RL) for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) Large Language Models, motivating low-precision rollout acceleration such as FP8. As an emerging low-precision format, NVFP4 combines fine-grained scaling for accuracy preservation with native W4A4 FP4 GEMMs for higher throughput than FP8. However, we find that directly applying NVFP4 to MoE RL rollout is impractical. NVFP4 rollout with BF16 training collapses after roughly 150 steps, accompanied by rapidly growing rollout-trainer log-probability gaps. Through training-inference error analysis and controlled ablations, we identify activation error, rather than weight error, as the dominant source of FP4 RL instability: weights can be synchronized and aligned by a shared quantization-dequantization path, whereas activations are recomputed online and error is amplified by the coarse E2M1 grid. Therefore, to stabilize NVFP4 RL for MoE, we propose QUantization-error Alignment across Dual Sides (QUADS). On the trainer side, we introduce Asymmetric Quantization-Aware Training fake-quantizing weights while keeping activations unquantized for better alignment. On the rollout side, Residual Activation Compensation corrects high-error activation channels while preserving native W4A4 GEMMs. In our MoE RL experiments on several benchmarks, QUADS achieves BF16-level accuracy, improves average pass@1 by 21.49 points over naive NVFP4 RL, and delivers ~16% higher rollout throughput than FP8.

</details>


### [124] [Graph Coloring Approach to Solving Sudoku with Oscillatory Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15814)
*Filip Sabo,Aida Todri-Sanial*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes an improved Oscillatory Neural Network (ONN) solver for Sudoku puzzles by reformulating it as a Graph Coloring problem, introducing a computationally cheaper version and an additional constraint term, achieving nearly flawless accuracy on 4×4 and high accuracy on 9×9 Sudoku puzzles, outperforming existing HNN and ONN solvers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a more accurate ONN-based solver for constrained combinatorial optimization problems (specifically Sudoku) by improving upon existing Graph Coloring ONN solvers.

Method: The Sudoku problem is formulated as a Graph Coloring problem. The existing Graph Coloring ONN solver is modified to a computationally cheaper version, and an additional term is introduced to enforce Sudoku-specific constraints.

Result: The proposed solver significantly outperforms existing HNN and ONN solvers in accuracy, achieving nearly flawless accuracy on 4×4 Sudoku puzzles and high accuracy on 9×9 puzzles across varying numbers of unknown digits.

Conclusion: The ONN-based solver with a modified Graph Coloring formulation and additional constraint term provides a more accurate physics-based computing approach for solving Sudoku puzzles.

Abstract: Oscillatory Neural Networks (ONNs) present an attractive physics-based computing paradigm rooted in the dynamics of a network of typically fully coupled oscillators aiming to minimize an underlying energy function. In this paper, we propose an ONN-based solver for one well-known constrained combinatorial optimization problem, namely a Sudoku, by formulating the problem as a Graph Coloring problem. By modifying the already existing Graph Coloring solver to a computationally cheaper version and introducing an additional term ensuring the fulfillment of the Sudoku constraints, our solver is shown to significantly outperform the existing HNN- and ONN solvers in terms of accuracy. In particular, we are able to achieve nearly flawless accuracies on $4 \times 4$ as well as rather high accuracies on $9 \times 9$ Sudoku puzzles for different numbers of unknown digits.

</details>


### [125] [In-context learning of closed form solution to simple linear regression task using transformer with linear self-attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15819)
*Katsuyuki Hagiwara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper constructs a transformer with linear self-attention that in-context learns the least squares estimate using layer normalization to approximate the closed-form solution, rather than relying on gradient descent. Experiments show this implementation emerges in transformers trained with l1 regularization when the target is the least squares estimate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While prior work shows transformers can implement gradient descent for in-context learning of regression problems, it remains unclear whether these implementations are actually acquired through training. This paper explores alternative mechanisms beyond gradient descent.

Method: The authors construct a transformer with linear self-attention and use layer normalization to approximate the closed-form least squares solution, instead of gradient descent. They train with l1 regularization to induce this specific implementation.

Result: The proposed implementation using layer normalization for closed-form approximation is mainly observed in transformers trained with l1 regularization when the target output is the least squares estimate.

Conclusion: Transformers can acquire in-context learning mechanisms beyond gradient descent, specifically closed-form solutions via layer normalization, especially under l1 regularization.

Abstract: In-context learning is a remarkable property of transformers and has recently received a lot of interest. In many studies of in-context learning, it has been shown that transformers are capable of implementing solver for linear and non-linear regression problems, in which the most of them implement gradient descent algorithm. However, it is still unclear whether those implementations have actually been acquired through training. In this paper, we construct a transformer with linear self-attention, which in-context learns the least squares estimate in a simple regression task.
