The point here is that the closed form (analytical) solution is approximately obtained by using layer normalization rather than an approximate solution based on gradient descent algorithm. Then, we show an experimental example, in which our implementation is mainly used in the transformer trained with l1 regularization when the target output is the least squares estimate.

</details>


### [126] [Data-Native Global Optimization for Big Data K-means Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15835)
*Ravil Mussabayev,Rustam Mussabayev,Zukhra Yerdaliyeva,Kuldeyev Nursultan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Big-means++ is a scalable global-search algorithm for big data clustering that uses sampled surrogate landscapes, a flowing-incumbent strategy, geometric shaking, multi-agent asynchronous search, and automatic convergence detection to overcome the NP-hard MSSC problem underlying K-means, outperforming 11 competing algorithms on 22 datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The MSSC problem underlying K-means is NP-hard, and existing methods either get stuck in poor local minima or rely on prohibitively expensive metaheuristic hybrids, especially when data is arbitrarily tall (many or infinite observations in a fixed feature space).

Method: Big-means++ orchestrates local K-means refinements into a global search by: (1) using sample-induced surrogate landscapes (each sample defines a perturbed MSSC approximation); (2) a flowing-incumbent strategy that propagates centroid states across landscapes without rollback; (3) geometric shaking that varies sample size across resolution scales; (4) a competitive multi-agent system for asynchronous parallel exploration; and (5) automatic convergence detection for speed-quality control.

Result: Experiments on 22 datasets against 11 competing algorithms demonstrate the effectiveness, efficiency, and robustness of Big-means++.

Conclusion: Big-means++ achieves both scalability and global-search quality for big data clustering by turning sample-to-sample variation into a global-search mechanism, without requiring prohibitive metaheuristic hybrids.

Abstract: Big data clustering remains challenging: the Minimum Sum-of-Squares Clustering (MSSC) problem underlying K-means is NP-hard, and existing methods either reach poor local minima or require prohibitive metaheuristic hybrids. We target arbitrarily tall data: a fixed feature space may contain arbitrarily many, possibly infinitely many, observations, while the algorithm accesses only finite random samples. We propose Big-means++, an algorithm achieving scalability and global-search quality by curating inputs to MSSC optimization on big data. It orchestrates local K-means refinements into a data-native global search for big data clustering. Rather than optimizing the full-data MSSC objective, Big-means++ traverses sample-induced surrogate landscapes. Each sample defines a distinct empirical MSSC approximation with a perturbed local-optimum structure, turning sample-to-sample variation into a global-search mechanism. Unlike Big-means, a flowing-incumbent strategy propagates centroid state across empirical landscapes through K-means refinements on fresh samples without rollback to a best-so-far solution. This increases mobility and favors stable, high-quality configurations across approximations of the full-data structure. A new shaking mechanism varies sample size geometrically, broadening the surrogate landscapes explored across resolution scales, accounting for cluster imbalance, and improving solution quality. A competitive multi-agent system asynchronously explores independent sampled landscapes, transforming diverse stochastic trajectories into collective search intelligence. Automatic convergence detection stops each agent after attaining a high-quality solution but before further search risks degrading it, while providing a universal speed-quality control. Experiments on 22 datasets against 11 competing algorithms demonstrate the effectiveness, efficiency, and robustness of Big-means++.

</details>


### [127] [ContinuityBench: A Benchmark and Systems Study of Stateful Failover in Multi-Provider LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15899)
*Vishal Pandey,Gopal Singh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies that high API availability in LLM deployments does not ensure conversational continuity. It introduces two metrics (CPR and CLO) and proposes a stateful, multi-provider proxy with History-Forwarding to preserve conversational context during failover. Experiments (N=750) show 99.20% CPR vs. near-0% for stateless systems, along with analysis of failover latency and retry strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard stateless failover mechanisms maintain API uptime during provider outages but silently discard conversation history, severely disrupting user experience. This gap between API availability and conversational continuity needs rigorous quantification and resolution.

Method: The authors introduce two novel metrics (Continuity Preservation Rate CPR and Continuity Latency Overhead CLO), propose a stateful multi-provider proxy with History-Forwarding strategy to reconstruct conversational state across heterogeneous LLM endpoints, and release continuity-bench, an open evaluation harness for stress-testing context preservation under high-concurrency provider failures.

Result: Empirical evaluation (N=750 failover events) shows the stateful proxy achieves 99.20% CPR [95% CI: 98.27%, 99.63%] vs. near-0% for standard stateless architectures. Failover latency analysis identifies the critical need for asynchronous exponential backoff with jitter to prevent cascading retry storms.

Conclusion: The results provide a principled foundation for building robust, state-preserving multi-model inference systems, emphasizing that conversational continuity requires architectural support beyond simple stateless failover.

Abstract: In production large language model (LLM) deployments, high API availability guarantees do not equate to conversational continuity. When a primary provider experiences an outage or strict rate-limiting, naive stateless failover mechanisms successfully maintain uptime but silently discard conversation history, severely disrupting the user experience. To rigorously quantify and resolve this failure mode, we introduce two novel metrics: Continuity Preservation Rate (CPR) and Continuity Latency Overhead (CLO). We propose a stateful, multi-provider proxy architecture utilizing a History-Forwarding strategy to seamlessly reconstruct conversational state across heterogeneous LLM endpoints during failover events. Furthermore, we release continuity-bench, https://github.com/Vishal-sys-code/continuity-bench, an open evaluation harness designed to stress-test context preservation under high-concurrency provider failure conditions. Our empirical evaluation ($N=750$ failover events) demonstrates that our stateful proxy achieves a 99.20\% CPR [95\% CI: 98.27\%, 99.63\%], cleanly transferring deep conversational context to fallback providers, compared to a near-0\% preservation rate for standard stateless architectures. Finally, we characterize failover latency distributions, identifying the critical necessity of asynchronous exponential backoff with jitter to prevent cascading retry storms against strict-limit fallback APIs. Our results provide a principled foundation for building robust, state-preserving multi-model inference systems.

</details>


### [128] [A Semiparametric Framework for Stochastic Fundamental Diagram Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15907)
*Pengnan Chi,Xiaoliang Ma,Magnus Jansson,Magnus Nordenvaad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a semiparametric stochastic fundamental diagram (SFD) framework that uses specially designed functional forms to satisfy physical constraints on conditional flow moments while employing neural-network-based structures for flexibility. It derives moment-matching equations with proven unique solutions for location-scale distributions, extends to non-location-scale distributions, and demonstrates superior probabilistic accuracy and uncertainty quantification on real-world traffic data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SFD models struggle to simultaneously enforce rigorous physical constraints (e.g., boundary conditions, consistency with traffic flow theory) while maintaining enough flexibility to capture complex nonlinear traffic patterns, especially in congested regimes.

Method: The authors design functional forms that inherently satisfy physical constraints on the moments of the conditional flow distribution given density, integrate neural-network structures for empirical pattern learning, and derive a system of moment-matching equations to translate constraints into conditional distribution parameterization. Uniqueness is proven for location-scale families, with extensions to non-location-scale distributions via additional boundary constraints.

Result: On real-world traffic data, the proposed semiparametric SFD consistently outperforms representative baselines in probabilistic prediction accuracy and uncertainty quantification, particularly in congested traffic regimes where physical constraints are most critical.

Conclusion: The semiparametric framework offers a theoretically grounded, flexible, and well-posed foundation for stochastic traffic flow modeling, effectively bridging physical fidelity and data-driven flexibility.

Abstract: The stochastic fundamental diagram (SFD) provides a probabilistic description of the relationship between traffic density and flow or speed, enabling uncertainty-aware traffic modeling. However, existing stochastic models frequently struggle to accommodate rigorous physical constraints while retaining sufficient flexibility to capture complex nonlinear patterns. To address this, we propose a novel semiparametric SFD modeling framework by leveraging specially designed functional forms. These functions intrinsically satisfy physical constraints defined on the moments of the conditional flow distribution given traffic density while incorporating neural-network-based structures to capture complex empirical patterns. We derive a system of moment-matching equations to convert physical constraints into the parameterization of the conditional distribution, proving that a unique solution exists for the location-scale family of distributions, thereby guaranteeing model well-posedness. Furthermore, we demonstrate that the framework can be extended to non-location-scale distributions, including those requiring additional boundary constraints. Empirical evaluations on a real-world dataset reveal that our approach consistently outperforms representative baselines, delivering superior probabilistic accuracy and robust uncertainty quantification, particularly in congested regimes. Overall, the proposed framework provides a theoretically grounded and flexible foundation for stochastic traffic flow modeling.

</details>


### [129] [(MPO)$^2$: Multivariate Polynomial Optimization based on Matrix Product Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15916)
*Niccolò Ciolli,Anders Vestergaard Nørskov,Michael Kastoryano,Petr Taborsky,Morten Mørup*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: (MPO)² introduces a Matrix Product Operator-based framework for multivariate polynomial optimization that achieves feature order-independent, compact polynomial representations with structured operators, outperforming existing tensor decomposition methods on regression and classification tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multivariate polynomial models suffer from exponential growth of coefficient tensors with polynomial degree; existing tensorized approaches (CP decomposition has rank-limited expressivity, tensor train is feature order-dependent) limit their practical applicability.

Method: (MPO)² combines learned Matrix Product Operator (MPO) feature embeddings with compact polynomial weight tensors, enabling feature order-independent representations and supporting structured operators (projections, convolutions, masks for weight tensor symmetries).

Result: (MPO)² outperforms existing tensor decomposition-based polynomial models on regression and classification benchmarks.

Conclusion: (MPO)² offers a flexible and efficient alternative for polynomial function approximation, overcoming limitations of prior tensorized polynomial approaches.

Abstract: Central to machine learning and signal processing is the ability to perform universal function approximation and learn complex input-output relationships from limited numbers of observations. Multivariate polynomial models offer a natural way to express such relationships through multiplicative feature interactions, but their coefficient tensors grow exponentially in size with the polynomial degree. Existing tensorized polynomial models reduce this cost, yet canonical polyadic decompositions have rank-limited expressivity, and tensor train formulations are feature order dependent. We introduce Multivariate Polynomial Optimization based on Matrix Product Operators (MPO)$^2$, a framework that combines learned MPO feature embeddings with compact polynomial weight tensors. This yields feature order independent polynomial representations that can incorporate structured operators such as projections, convolutions, and masks for weight tensor symmetries. Across regression and classification benchmarks, (MPO)$^2$ improves over existing tensor decomposition based polynomial models and provides a flexible alternative for efficient polynomial function approximation.

</details>


### [130] [On the Failure of Boundary-Seeking Distillation in Bottlenecked Generative Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15919)
*Mohamed Amine Kina*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether boundary-seeking data-free knowledge distillation principles (like CAKE) apply to autoencoder distillation. The authors find that such objectives are ill-posed for bottlenecked generative architectures due to gradient conflicts from coupled feature-level classifiers, and propose manifold-aware synthesis as an alternative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine if the boundary-seeking principle used in classifier distillation (CAKE) can be extended to autoencoder distillation, and to understand the fundamental challenges of doing so.

Method: The authors reformulate continuous autoencoder reconstruction as a dense per-feature classification task to enable comparison with CAKE. They analyze the effects of applying contrastive boundary-seeking objectives to decoder outputs constrained by a shared low-dimensional bottleneck.

Result: Boundary-seeking objectives are ill-posed for bottlenecked generative architectures. Individually sampling contrastive targets for coupled decoder outputs violates the learned latent manifold geometry and causes severe gradient conflicts, unlike in single-instance classifier distillation.

Conclusion: Manifold-aware synthesis bypasses gradient conflicts and establishes an effective baseline for data-free generative distillation, while naive boundary-seeking is fundamentally flawed for autoencoders.

Abstract: Data-free knowledge distillation transfers the knowledge encoded in a teacher model to a student model without access to the original training data. Prior work such as Contrastive Abductive Knowledge Extraction (CAKE) achieves this for classifiers by synthesizing samples near the teacher's decision boundary. In this work, we investigate whether this boundary-seeking principle extends to autoencoder distillation through experiments on the MNIST dataset . To enable a direct comparison, we reformulate continuous reconstruction as a dense, per-feature classification task, allowing the decoder to output categorical logits. We show that boundary-seeking objectives are fundamentally ill-posed in bottlenecked generative architectures. CAKE operates on a single, instance-level objective, but a decoder acts as an array of tightly coupled, feature-level classifiers constrained by a shared low-dimensional bottleneck. Independently sampling contrastive targets for these coupled outputs violates the geometry of the learned latent manifold and produces severe gradient conflicts instead of informative boundary samples. Manifold-aware synthesis bypasses these conflicts entirely and establishes an effective baseline for data-free generative distillation.

</details>


### [131] [Knowledge-Guided Cross-Modal Fusion for Adult-to-Pediatric ECG Transfer via Label-Conditioned Contrastive Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15928)
*Xinran Liu,Yuwen Li,Hongxiang Gao,Heyang Xu,Jianqing Li,Zongmin Wang,Chengyu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PEACE is a knowledge-guided multimodal ECG-text framework pretrained on adult data (MIMIC-IV) that aligns pediatric ECG interpretation by decomposing diagnoses into axis-specific tokens (rhythm, morphology, ST-T) and using label-conditioned contrastive learning (LSBC) with curriculum adaptive fusion (CAF), achieving strong zero-shot and few-shot pediatric transfer (up to 91.56% macro AUC on ZZU-pECG) and outperforming global text fusion baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ECG interpretation requires age-sensitive criteria, but models pretrained on adult ECGs transfer poorly to pediatric populations due to scarce pediatric labels. Existing multimodal ECG-text methods align at the global sample level, entangling co-occurring diagnoses and limiting cross-age transfer.

Method: PEACE decomposes each diagnosis into rhythm, morphology, and ST-T axes, composing positive-label descriptors into three axis tokens and a fused embedding. A Label Query Network (LQN) uses diagnostic labels as queries to cross-attend over ECG tokens and axis tokens. Label Set aware Bidirectional Contrastive learning (LSBC) aligns pooled ECG features with fused embeddings for recordings sharing diagnoses. Curriculum Adaptive Fusion (CAF) gates alignment strength using smoothed classification loss and training progress.

Result: On ZZU-pECG, macro AUCs of 59.39% (zero-shot), 81.74% (50-shot), and 91.56% (full fine-tuning). Zero-shot substantially improves over domain adaptation baselines; 50-shot AUC comparable to DANN. On PTB-XL, 96.90% macro AUC over nine harmonized labels.

Conclusion: Label-conditioned knowledge alignment (decomposing diagnoses into structured axes) is the key driver of pediatric transfer gains, outperforming global text fusion approaches.

Abstract: Adult and pediatric electrocardiogram (ECG) interpretation relies on age-sensitive criteria, and models pretrained mainly on adult ECGs often transfer poorly to pediatric populations when pediatric labels are scarce. Existing multimodal ECG--text methods typically align waveforms and text at the global sample level, entangling evidence from co-occurring diagnoses and limiting transfer under this gap. We propose Pediatric-Adult ECG Alignment via Cross-modal Enhancement (PEACE), a knowledge-guided framework pretrained on the largely adult MIMIC-IV ECG corpus. PEACE describes each diagnosis along rhythm, morphology, and ST--T axes and, per recording, composes only positive-label descriptors into three axis tokens and a fused embedding. A label query network (LQN) uses diagnostic labels as queries to cross-attend over ECG tokens and axis tokens, while label set aware bidirectional contrastive learning (LSBC) aligns pooled ECG features with the fused embedding when recordings share diagnoses. Curriculum adaptive fusion (CAF) gates alignment strength according to smoothed classification loss and training progress, limiting disruption during early optimization. The knowledge branch is used only for training supervision; inference uses ECG signals alone. On ZZU-pECG, PEACE reaches macro average AUCs of 59.39%, 81.74%, and 91.56% under zero-shot, 50-shot, and full fine-tuning, with the clearest gains over foundation and knowledge-pretraining baselines under limited supervision; versus domain adaptation initializations, zero-shot improves substantially while 50-shot AUC is comparable to DANN. After fine-tuning on PTB-XL, PEACE reaches 96.90% macro average AUC over nine harmonized labels. Ablations confirm that label-conditioned knowledge alignment, rather than global text fusion, is the key driver of pediatric transfer gains.

</details>


### [132] [An Exploratory Study of Single Channel Surface Electromyography for Hand Gesture Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15972)
*Daanish Hindustani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study shows that ten hand gestures can be classified with up to 90% accuracy using a single sEMG channel and lightweight classifiers (NN, KNN, SVM) by extracting time-domain, frequency-domain, higher-order-crossing, and relative-intensity features, reducing redundancy via Pearson filtering, and applying LDA/PCA for dimensionality reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multichannel sEMG arrays and computationally intensive models limit practical deployment in low-power and embedded systems; thus, a single-channel, lightweight approach is needed for cost-effective gesture recognition.

Method: Raw sEMG signals were transformed into a comprehensive feature set (time-domain, frequency-domain, higher-order-crossing, relative-intensity). Features were filtered using Pearson correlation to remove redundancy, and dimensionality reduction (LDA, PCA) was selectively applied. Three classifiers (NN, KNN, SVM) were evaluated across four experiments.

Result: Combining time and frequency features with Pearson filtering and a compact feed-forward neural network achieved up to 90% classification accuracy for ten hand gestures using a single sEMG channel.

Conclusion: Single-channel sEMG systems paired with lightweight ML architectures are feasible for accurate, low-power, cost-effective gesture-recognition applications.

Abstract: Accurate hand gesture recognition using surface electromyography (sEMG) typically relies on multichannel sensor arrays and computationally intensive models. This limits practical deployment in low-power and embedded systems. This study investigates the feasibility of classifying ten hand gestures using a single sEMG channel combined with lightweight machine learning architectures. Raw sEMG signals were transformed into a comprehensive feature-based representation, including time-domain, frequency-domain, higher-order-crossing, and relative-intensity features. Feature redundancy was reduced using Pearson correlation filtering and the removal of highly correlated features, while dimensionality-reduction techniques (LDA and PCA) were applied selectively. Three classifiers, a feed-forward neural network (NN), k-nearest neighbors (KNN), and a support vector machine (SVM), were systematically evaluated across four experiments. Results demonstrate that combining time and frequency features with Pearson filtering and a compact NN can achieve up to 90 percent accuracy, even with limited temporal and spatial information. These findings highlight the potential of single-channel sEMG systems for cost-effective, low-power gesture-recognition applications.

</details>


### [133] [Presentation, Not Mechanism: A Render Confound in Deprecation-Aware Memory Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16019)
*Zhaoyang Jiang,Zhizhong Fu,Zicheng Li,Yunsoo Kim,Jiacong Mi,Xuanqi Peng,Fei Teng,Honghan Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Evaluations of structured memory for evidence revision are confounded by presentation changes; after controlling for render, coarse invalidation outperforms fine-grained RevisionLedger for current-state queries, with fine mechanism showing near-zero residual gain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems retrieve from self-revising records (issue threads, encyclopedias, policy logs, conversations), requiring not just relevance but knowing which claims remain in force, which were superseded, and when to abstain. Existing evaluations conflate mechanism changes with presentation changes.

Method: Introduced Evidence-State Revision framework; compared flat retrieval, coarse edge invalidation, and fine-grained RevisionLedger on 2,907 high-agreement questions from GitHub, multi-repo issues, Wikipedia, and DyKnow temporal streams. Used a render-matched control (same layout, deprecation disabled) to isolate the presentation confound.

Result: RevisionLedger appeared to beat flat baseline by +0.182, but almost all gain came from easier presentation; fine-grained mechanism residual was indistinguishable from zero (+0.021 to +0.025). After controlling presentation, coarse invalidation beat fine ledger by 0.084 for current-state queries. Provenance mainly needs retained invalidated evidence, not richer typing.

Conclusion: Memory evaluations should hold render fixed. Deprecation-aware systems should deploy the coarsest retained state that covers their queries.

Abstract: AI systems increasingly retrieve from records that revise themselves: issue threads, encyclopedic histories, policy logs, and long conversations. The challenge is not only finding relevant evidence, but deciding which claims remain in force, which were superseded, and when to abstain. Structured memories promise to solve this with typed edges, temporal updates, and conflict status, yet evaluations often change mechanism and prompt presentation together. We study this as Evidence-State Revision, comparing flat retrieval, coarse edge invalidation, and fine-grained RevisionLedger on 2,907 high-agreement questions from GitHub, multi-repo issue histories, Wikipedia, and DyKnow-style temporal streams. A render-matched control (same layout, deprecation disabled) reveals the central confound: when a value is changed and later restored, RevisionLedger appears to beat a flat baseline by +0.182, but almost all the gain comes from easier presentation; the fine-grained mechanism residual is indistinguishable from zero (+0.021 to +0.025 across two judge families). After presentation is controlled, coarse invalidation is the only mechanism that pays for current-state queries, beating the fine ledger by 0.084; the same query-sufficiency principle says provenance mainly needs retained invalidated evidence, not richer typing. Memory evaluations should hold render fixed, and deprecation-aware systems should deploy the coarsest retained state that covers their queries.

</details>


### [134] [Constrained Hebbian Learning Supports Efficient Representational Allocation under Structural Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16027)
*Patrick Inoue,Florian Röhrbein,Andreas Knoblauch*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hebbian learning, compared to backpropagation and DDTP, achieves a more favorable cost-performance trade-off under metabolic constraints by reducing task-information cost (CTI) while preserving functional performance in audiovisual associative learning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biological systems face anatomical and metabolic constraints (costly synaptic maintenance, limited connectivity) that favor low-redundancy neural codes. The study tests whether excitatory competitive Hebbian learning can support synaptic resource allocation under such constraints and achieve a better cost-performance regime than reference learning rules.

Method: Representational cost is quantified via mutual-information-based measures from the Variational Information Bottleneck. Fixed audiovisual embeddings from three benchmarks (AVE, Kinetics-Sounds, VGGSound100) isolate downstream associative plasticity. Hebbian learning is compared against DDTP and backpropagation (BP) under matched sparsity and architectural constraints.

Result: Hebbian learning achieves lower task-information cost (CTI) than sparse BP and DDTP, with CTI comparable to shallow nonnegative-weight BP. It shifts the cost-performance trade-off rather than uniformly improving accuracy, yielding lower CTI at comparable functional performance in several settings.

Conclusion: Hebbian learning is better interpreted as a mechanism for synaptic resource allocation under metabolic constraints rather than as a general strategy for maximizing audiovisual classification accuracy, as its advantage lies in cost efficiency rather than raw accuracy gains.

Abstract: Introduction: Biological systems face anatomical and metabolic constraints, including costly synaptic maintenance and limited connectivity. These constraints favor neural codes that compress behaviorally relevant information into low-redundancy patterns. We test whether an excitatory competitive Hebbian rule can support synaptic resource allocation under such constraints and whether the resulting representations occupy a more favorable cost-performance regime than reference learning rules.
  Methods: Representational cost is quantified using mutual-information-based measures derived from the Variational Information Bottleneck. Experiments use fixed audiovisual embeddings from three audiovisual benchmarks (AVE, Kinetics-Sounds, VGGSound100) to isolate downstream associative plasticity. Hebbian learning is compared with Dense Difference Target Propagation (DDTP) and backpropagation (BP) under matched sparsity and architectural constraints.
  Results: Hebbian learning achieves lower task-information cost (CTI) than sparse BP and DDTP in the main compressed comparisons, while reaching CTI values comparable to shallow BP with nonnegative weights. Rather than uniformly improving classification performance, Hebbian learning shifts the trade-off between task-relevant information and representational cost, yielding lower CTI at comparable functional performance in several settings.
  Discussion: The results indicate a cost-performance trade-off rather than uniform accuracy gains. For a given level of task-relevant information, Hebbian representations retain less input information while preserving functional performance, although accuracy is slightly reduced on some datasets. These findings support interpreting Hebbian learning as a mechanism for synaptic resource allocation rather than as a general strategy for maximizing audiovisual classification accuracy.

</details>


### [135] [CLaC@FinMMEval 2026 Task 3: Sentiment-Augmented Deep Reinforcement Learning for Active Trading -- An Alpha-Reward Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16028)
*Andrei Neagu,Eeham Khan,Leila Kosseim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a deep reinforcement learning system for daily trading decisions on Bitcoin and Tesla using news sentiment and market data. DDPG achieves the best test performance, with the DQL agent selected for deployment based on validation Sharpe ratio. Results show strong absolute returns (e.g., 54.96% for TSLA via DDPG) and reveal a generalization gap between validation (bull market) and test (bear market) regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a reinforcement learning-based trading agent that outperforms buy-and-hold for Bitcoin and Tesla using news sentiment and historical market data, as part of the CLEF 2026 FinMMEval Lab.

Method: Four deep RL algorithms (PG, PPO, DQL, DDPG) are compared on a discrete-action MDP formulation. State features include technical indicators, cyclical calendar encodings, and daily news sentiment from LLaMA 3.2 1B. An alpha reward (excess market return) and randomized episode start dates are used to reduce overfitting. Hyperparameters are optimized via Ray Tune (180 trials per pair) with early stopping and validation Sharpe-based selection.

Result: DDPG achieved the best overall test performance. For TSLA, DDPG and DQL achieved cumulative returns of 54.96% and 52.62% vs. 16.45% buy-and-hold. For BTC, DDPG achieved +1.58% while buy-and-hold declined -34.27%. A significant validation-to-test generalization gap was observed.

Conclusion: Deep RL methods, particularly DDPG and DQL, can substantially outperform buy-and-hold in both crypto and equity markets. However, policies selected during bull-market validation periods may generalize poorly to bear-market test regimes, indicating a need for more robust selection and out-of-distribution handling.

Abstract: This paper presents our system for Task 3 of the CLEF 2026 FinMMEval Lab, which requires daily long, flat, or short trading decisions for Bitcoin (BTC) and Tesla (TSLA) using news and historical market data. We formulate the problem as a discrete-action Markov Decision Process and compare four deep reinforcement learning algorithms: Policy Gradient (PG), Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), Deep Q-Learning (DQL), and Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (DDPG). The agents use technical indicators, cyclical calendar encodings, and daily news sentiment scores produced by LLaMA 3.2 1B. To reduce overfitting and align training with the objective of outperforming buy-and-hold, we introduce an alpha reward based on excess market return and randomize episode start dates. Hyperparameters are optimized with Ray Tune over 180 trials per algorithm-asset pair, with early stopping and model selection based on validation Sharpe ratio. On the CLEF Task 3 test set, DDPG achieves the strongest overall performance. DQL was selected a priori for the live endpoint because it obtained the highest validation Sharpe ratio, with selection performed without access to the test period. For TSLA, DDPG and DQL achieve cumulative returns of 54.96% and 52.62%, respectively, compared with 16.45% for buy-and-hold. For BTC, DDPG achieves a positive return of 1.58% while buy-and-hold declines by -34.27%. The results also reveal a substantial validation-to-test generalization gap, highlighting the difficulty of transferring policies selected in bull-market conditions to a bear-market regime.

</details>


### [136] [Revisiting data-driven dynamic security assessment with a tabular foundation model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16031)
*Olayiwola Arowolo,Maosheng Yang,Jochen Cremer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A tabular foundation model (TFM) is proposed for pre-fault dynamic security assessment that uses in-context learning to assess stability across multiple contingencies without retraining, requiring only ~120 labelled samples per contingency (vs. tens of thousands) and achieving ~90% Macro F1 on IEEE 68-bus system; it also generalizes to unseen contingencies using electrical distance coordinates with as few as 10 labelled samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ML-based pre-fault DSA approaches require large labelled databases and separate models per contingency, and generalize poorly to unseen contingencies.

Method: A tabular foundation model (TFM) using in-context learning (no retraining or hyperparameter tuning) with electrical distance coordinates (EDC) as continuous features for generalization to unseen contingencies.

Result: Single TFM achieves ~90% Macro F1 with only 120 labelled samples per contingency on IEEE 68-bus; for unseen contingencies, 10 labelled samples with EDC encoding matches a transfer learning oracle model.

Conclusion: This work paves the way for deploying foundation models in power system operations, drastically reducing data requirements and eliminating per-contingency model maintenance.

Abstract: Data-driven pre-fault dynamic security assessment (DSA) rapidly evaluates the dynamic risk of credible contingencies on a power system using machine learning. Existing approaches face two limitations. First, they require a large labelled database for training, with a separate model trained, tuned, and maintained for each contingency in a potentially long list of credible contingencies. Second, the trained models generalize poorly to unseen contingencies. This work addresses the limitations by using a tabular foundation model (TFM) that assesses stability through in-context learning, requiring no retraining or hyperparameter optimization. A single TFM can assess many contingencies at once, removing the need for one model per classifier. We also characterize when the use of electrical distance coordinates (EDC) as continuous features enables generalization of TFM to unseen contingencies and when they do not, demonstrating how a few labelled samples can reliably improve generalization. Through comprehensive case studies on the IEEE 68-bus system, we show that a single TFM attains an average Macro F1 score of about 90% with only 120 labelled samples per contingency, roughly two orders of magnitude fewer than conventionally assumed, without any model retraining or hyperparameter tuning. For new/unseen contingencies, we show that using just 10 labelled samples of the new contingency with EDC encoding matches the best achievable transfer learning oracle model, which requires fully labelled data and is not deployable in practice. Overall, this initial study paves the way towards developing and deploying foundation models for power system operations, with possible applications across multiple operational tasks.

</details>


### [137] [DELUGE: Towards Continental-Scale Daily Pluvial Flood Damage Prediction via Interpretable Conditioning on Foundation Model Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16050)
*Yuya Kawakami,Daniel Cayan,Dongyu Liu,Kwan-Liu Ma,Tom Corringham*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DELUGE is a multimodal deep learning framework for daily pluvial flood damage prediction at ~1 km resolution across the CONUS, using NFIP claims data (2017-2022). It introduces Value and Temporal Modulators for interpretability, outperforms tree-based baselines by 9-30% on dollar-weighted PR-AUC, and offers a transferable pattern for integrating foundation-model embeddings into geospatial tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pluvial flooding accounts for 45% of NFIP claims yet is harder to predict than riverine/coastal flooding. Existing approaches are limited to coarse resolution, regional domains, or computationally intensive process-based models unsuitable for daily continental-scale use.

Method: A multimodal deep learning framework with parametric Value Modulator and Temporal Modulator modules in the hydrometeorology branch, conditioned on terrain descriptors and AlphaEarth foundation-model embeddings. Models the top 100 highest-claim 75 km cells (~81% of total pluvial claims) using spatially and temporally corrected NFIP claims data (2017-2022).

Result: Under spatial block holdout, DELUGE outperforms tuned Random Forest, XGBoost, and LightGBM baselines by 9% to 30% on dollar-weighted PR-AUC, which emphasizes rare, high-cost claims.

Conclusion: The interpretable conditioning scheme is a transferable pattern for integrating foundation-model embeddings into other geospatial prediction tasks.

Abstract: Pluvial (rainfall-driven) flooding accounts for 45% of National Flood Insurance Program (NFIP) claims in the United States and is harder to predict than its riverine and coastal counterparts, with existing approaches limited to coarse resolution, regional domains, or computationally intensive process-based models unsuitable for daily continental-scale use. We present DELUGE, a multimodal deep learning framework for daily pluvial flood damage prediction at ~1 km resolution and national scale, trained on spatially and temporally corrected NFIP claims (2017-2022) and structured around the hazard, exposure, and vulnerability components of disaster risk. Rather than blanket coverage of the Conterminous United States (CONUS), we model the top 100 highest-claim 75 km cells, distributed nationwide and accounting for ~81% of total pluvial flood claims. Our architectural novelty is a pair of parametric modules in the hydrometeorology branch, a Value Modulator and a Temporal Modulator, conditioned on terrain descriptors and AlphaEarth foundation-model embeddings, that expose directly inspectable hydrological response parameters and provide architecture-level interpretability-by-design. Under a spatial block holdout, DELUGE outperforms tuned Random Forest, XGBoost, and LightGBM baselines by 9% to 30% on a dollar-weighted area under the precision-recall curve (PR-AUC), a metric that emphasizes the rare, high-cost claims of greatest operational interest. Beyond DELUGE, we argue this interpretable conditioning scheme is a transferable pattern for integrating foundation-model embeddings into other geospatial prediction tasks.

</details>


### [138] [When Model Merging Rivals Joint Multi-Task Reinforcement Learning: A Task-Vector Geometry Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16062)
*S. Aaron McClendon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper tests whether model merging—often used as a substitute for joint multi-task RL training—actually matches the joint training baseline. On AppWorld with Qwen3-8B specialists, merging (TIES, RAM+) matches joint RL performance (all variants statistically indistinguishable). The reason: task vectors are near-orthogonal (cosine 0.06–0.10) despite ~65% support overlap, so support/sign-based merging collapses to simple averaging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model merging is widely promoted as a replacement for joint multi-task training, but in RL settings it is essentially never tested against the joint-training baseline it claims to replace. The authors build this missing comparison.

Method: Train difficulty-1 and difficulty-2 Qwen3-8B specialists on the AppWorld agent benchmark with LOOP; merge them using TIES and RAM+; compare against a jointly trained model on the same data. Measure task vector geometry (cosine similarity, support overlap, calibrated against random-init floor and same-run ceiling).

Result: Merging matches joint RL on task-goal completion; all merge variants are statistically indistinguishable. Task vectors are near-orthogonal (cosine 0.06–0.10) with ~65% support overlap, and a small shared direction grows over training—confirmed as learning signal, not low-rank noise.

Conclusion: Because task vector direction and support are decoupled, support/sign-based merging methods (RAM, TIES) collapse to near-uniform averaging in this setting, explaining why merge method choice does not affect outcome.

Abstract: Model merging is promoted as a substitute for joint multi-task training, yet in the reinforcement-learning setting this substitution is essentially never tested against the baseline it claims to replace: methods merge independently released agents precisely because a joint model is unavailable. We build the missing comparison. Training difficulty-1 and difficulty-2 Qwen3-8B specialists on the AppWorld agent benchmark with LOOP, we merge them (TIES, RAM+) and pit the result against a jointly trained model on the same data. On task-goal completion, merging matches joint RL -- and every merge variant is statistically indistinguishable. To explain why merge method does not matter here, we measure the geometry of the specialists' task vectors, which carries no task-sampling noise: they are near-orthogonal (cosine 0.06 - 0.10) despite ~65% support overlap, a small, shared direction that grows over training and that we calibrate against a random-init floor and a same-run ceiling to confirm it reflects learning, not the low-rank parameterization. Because direction and support are decoupled, support and sign-based merging (RAM, TIES) collapse to near-uniform averaging. We release all code and statistics.

</details>


### [139] [Physics-Based Deep Spatiotemporal Hyperlocal Radar Nowcasting with a Multi-Variable U-Net for High-Resolution Precipitation Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16080)
*Akshay Sunil,Muhammed Rashid,Raja Sekhar Sivaraju,Sushma Nair,Subimal Ghosh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops a compact radar-only U-Net nowcasting framework for Mumbai that uses multi-elevation reflectivity, Doppler velocity, and kinematic proxy features (divergence, shear, vorticity) to predict 12 composite reflectivity fields up to 90 min lead time. A high-reflectivity attention module and physics-guided attribution improve convective core sensitivity. The model achieves CSI of 0.437, 0.332, and 0.193 at 90 min for ≥10, ≥20, ≥30 dBZ thresholds, outperforming persistence at longer lead times while running in seconds on a standard computer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urban flood management and real-time decision-making in Mumbai require accurate precipitation nowcasting over 10–90 min, but conventional NWP suffers from computational latency, and monsoon convection with land-sea interactions makes short-term prediction challenging in this region.

Method: An encoder-decoder U-Net using multi-elevation reflectivity, Doppler radial velocity, and radial-velocity-gradient proxy features (velocity magnitude, divergence-like, directional-shear, vorticity-like channels) trained on Mumbai Doppler radar observations from May to August 2023, with a high-reflectivity attention module and physics-guided attribution analysis.

Result: At 90 min lead time, CSI = 0.437 (≥10 dBZ), 0.332 (≥20 dBZ), 0.193 (≥30 dBZ). The model achieves lower RMSE and higher spatial correlation than persistence at longer lead times, and runs in seconds on a standard computer.

Conclusion: The compact radar-only nowcasting framework is effective for Mumbai's challenging monsoon conditions, providing fast, real-time predictions that outperform persistence without requiring full wind-field retrieval or costly NWP initialization.

Abstract: Precipitation nowcasting over the immediate 10-90 min period is important for flood management and real-time decision-making in urban regions. Conventional short-range forecasting with high-resolution numerical weather prediction requires frequent data assimilation, model initialization, and spin-up, introducing computational latency. Machine learning provides an alternative by learning storm evolution directly from high-frequency observations and producing forecasts quickly after training. This is particularly relevant for Mumbai, India, where monsoon convection, land-sea interactions, and localized intense rainfall make short-term prediction difficult. Here, we develop a compact radar-only nowcasting framework that combines multi-elevation reflectivity, Doppler radial velocity, and radial-velocity-gradient proxy features within an encoder-decoder U-Net. Using the most recent radar volume scan, the model predicts 12 future composite reflectivity fields at 7.5-min intervals up to 90 min lead time. The derived velocity magnitude, divergence-like, directional-shear, and vorticity-like channels represent kinematic signatures associated with convergence and boundary interactions without requiring full wind-field retrieval. A high-reflectivity attention module improves sensitivity to convective cores, and physics-guided attribution examines whether the learned sensitivities are meteorologically meaningful. The model is trained using Mumbai Doppler radar observations from May to August 2023 and evaluated on temporally independent events. At 90 min lead time, Critical Success Index values are 0.437, 0.332, and 0.193 for $\geq$10, $\geq$20, and $\geq$30 dBZ thresholds, respectively. Compared with persistence, the model gives lower RMSE and higher spatial correlation at longer lead times. Once trained, it runs on a standard computer, generating nowcasts within seconds for real-time use.

</details>


### [140] [Neural spectroscopy of AlphaFold2 reveals encoded protein conformational landscapes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16087)
*Kaustav Mehta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AlphaFold2's weights, when perturbed via Gaussian convolution, reveal physically meaningful conformational landscapes of proteins, showing that the model implicitly encodes structural constraints beyond its static structure prediction task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demonstrate that AlphaFold2's trained parameters are not merely prediction machinery but encode meaningful conformational organization that can be probed and characterized, revealing emergent physical insights about protein dynamics.

Method: Apply Scaled Gaussian Convolution (smoothing weight tensors with Gaussian filters and scaling) to perturb AlphaFold2's Evoformer weights, then analyze the resulting conformational landscapes for ubiquitin, KaiB, and alpha-synuclein, with matched-power noise controls.

Result: Ubiquitin's native contacts break in order consistent with decades of folding experiments. Five independently trained models consistently show KaiB's alternative fold is not recovered. For alpha-synuclein, models produce coherent but divergent landscapes. Noise controls confirm structured outputs are not artifacts.

Conclusion: AlphaFold2's weights encode structural constraints and conformational organization that emerge as a byproduct of static structure prediction training, not as an explicit objective. The proposed "neural spectroscopy" approach enables reading these implicit representations.

Abstract: AlphaFold2's 93 million parameters, shaped by the evolutionary record of protein structure encoded in the Protein Data Bank and in sequence alignments, are conventionally treated only as machinery for converting sequence to structure. We propose they are also a scientific object that can be analyzed directly: a learned encoding of protein conformational organization that can be probed and characterized. By smoothing the Evoformer's weight tensors with a Gaussian convolution and scaling the result, we show that the trained model produces physically structured conformational landscapes. Under perturbation, ubiquitin's native contacts break in the order established by decades of folding experiments. For KaiB, five independently trained models agree that the alternative fold is not recovered under perturbation. For alpha-synuclein, five models produce five different but coherent landscapes, mapping where the training signal has determined the representation and where it has not. Matched-power noise controls confirm that random corruption of equal magnitude produces debris, not conformations. The model learned to predict static structures; the conformational organization visible under perturbation was not an explicit training target, suggesting it emerged as a byproduct of that objective. AlphaFold2's weights appear to encode structural constraints, shaped by evolutionary and structural training data, that extend beyond what unperturbed inference reveals. We call the approach of reading them neural spectroscopy, and Scaled Gaussian Convolution one such protocol.

</details>


### [141] [DADiff: Diffusion-Driven Cross-Domain Policy Adaptation for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16090)
*Hanyang Chen,Anirudh Satheesh,Longchao Da,Hua Wei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DADiff is a diffusion-based framework that addresses dynamics mismatch in reinforcement learning by leveraging generative trajectory discrepancies between source and target domains, using reward modification and data selection variants for policy adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transferring policies across domains with dynamics mismatch is challenging in RL, especially when only limited target domain interactions are allowed. Existing methods using domain classifiers, value-guided filtering, or representation learning have limitations.

Method: DADiff uses a diffusion model to generate trajectories and estimates dynamics mismatch by comparing source and target domain generative trajectories. Two variants are developed: reward modification and data selection. Theoretical analysis shows performance difference is bounded by generative trajectory deviation.

Result: Extensive experiments across environments with various shifts show DADiff achieves superior performance compared to existing approaches in addressing dynamics mismatch.

Conclusion: The diffusion-based generative modeling perspective provides an effective way to handle dynamics mismatch in online dynamics adaptation, with theoretical guarantees linking trajectory deviation to performance bounds.

Abstract: Transferring policies across domains poses a vital challenge in reinforcement learning, due to the dynamics mismatch between the source and target domains. In this paper, we consider the setting of online dynamics adaptation, where policies are trained in the source domain with sufficient data, while only limited interactions with the target domain are allowed. There are a few existing works that address the dynamics mismatch by employing domain classifiers, value-guided data filtering, or representation learning. Instead, we study the domain adaptation problem from a generative modeling perspective. Specifically, we introduce DADiff, a diffusion-based framework that leverages the discrepancy between source and target domain generative trajectories in the generation process of the next state to estimate the dynamics mismatch. Both reward modification and data selection variants are developed to adapt the policy to the target domain. We also provide a theoretical analysis to show that the performance difference of a given policy between the two domains is bounded by the generative trajectory deviation. More discussions on the applicability of the variants and the connection between our theoretical analysis and the prior work are further provided. We conduct extensive experiments in environments with various shifts to validate the effectiveness of our method. The results demonstrate that our method provides superior performance compared to existing approaches, effectively addressing the dynamics mismatch. We provide the code of our method at https://github.com/hanyang-chen/DADiff-release

</details>


### [142] [Understanding Reasoning from Pretraining to Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16097)
*Jingyan Shen,Ang Li,Salman Rahman,Yifan Sun,Micah Goldblum,Matus Telgarsky,Pavel Izmailov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies how pretraining choices (model size, data) affect returns to RL post-training, using chess as a controlled testbed. It finds post-RL performance is predictable from pretraining loss, RL reward curve slope improves linearly with pretraining tokens, and RL amplifies SFT-preferred moves on easy puzzles but surfaces novel correct moves on hard puzzles. These findings transfer to math-domain text training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL post-training for LLMs is studied in isolation from pretraining, leaving two questions open: (1) how pretraining choices shape returns to RL compute, and (2) what RL actually does to the model. Standard LLM settings make this difficult due to uncontrolled pretraining corpora and prohibitive compute costs.

Method: Use chess as a controlled testbed; pretrain language models (5M to 1B params) on human chess games, supervised fine-tune on synthetic reasoning traces, then run RL on chess puzzles with verifiable rewards. Validate transfer by training a 1B model on math-domain text.

Result: Post-RL performance at given RL compute is well-predicted from pretraining loss; slope of RL reward curves improves approximately linearly with pretraining tokens. RL amplifies SFT-preferred correct moves on easy puzzles but surfaces novel correct moves on hard puzzles. These patterns transfer to math-domain training.

Conclusion: Provides a quantitative account of the pretraining-to-RL interface and a controlled testbed for studying reasoning across the full pretraining-to-post-training pipeline.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become central to improving large language models (LLMs) on complex reasoning tasks, yet RL post-training is largely studied in isolation from the pretraining that precedes it. As a result, two basic questions remain open: (1) how do pretraining choices (model size, data) shape the returns to RL compute, and (2) what does RL actually do to the model? These questions are difficult to study in the standard LLM setting: pretraining corpora are vast and uncontrolled, making it hard to attribute behaviors to pretraining versus RL, and systematic compute sweeps across both stages are prohibitively expensive. To address these challenges, we use chess as a controlled testbed for studying reasoning across the full pretraining-to-post-training pipeline. We follow the standard LLM training pipeline by pretraining language models from 5M to 1B parameters on human chess games, supervised fine-tuning on synthetic reasoning traces, and running RL on chess puzzles with verifiable rewards. Using this framework, we find that the post-RL performance at given RL compute level is well-predicted from the pretraining loss, and slope of the RL reward curves improves approximately linearly with the pretraining tokens. Beyond scaling, we find that RL does not simply sharpen the SFT policy: on easy puzzles it amplifies correct moves the SFT policy already preferred, while on hard puzzles it surfaces correct moves that were nearly absent under SFT. We further test whether our findings transfer beyond chess by training a 1B language model on math-domain text, where the same predictive pattern emerges: longer-pretrained checkpoints reach higher post-RL performance and improve faster under RL. In sum, we provide a quantitative account of the pretraining-to-RL interface and a controlled testbed for studying the science of reasoning across the full pretraining-to-post-training pipeline.

</details>


### [143] [The Honest Quorum Problem: Epistemic Byzantine Fault Tolerance for Agentic Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16109)
*Jun He,Deying Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Epistemic Byzantine Fault Tolerance (EBFT), a fault-tolerance model addressing the "Honest Quorum Problem" where protocol-compliant agentic validators can endorse semantically invalid transitions due to reasoning errors (epistemic faults). EBFT extends the Byzantine fault model with two additional bounds: eδ for coherent invalid endorsements and uε for unusable validator support, deriving quorum conditions for semantic validity, agreement, and liveness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional BFT consensus assumes protocol-compliant participants are semantically correct. However, agentic validators (e.g., LLM-based systems) can follow protocol faithfully but endorse invalid state transitions due to reasoning errors. Moreover, correlated epistemic faults across shared models/prompts/toolchains amplify this risk, making existing fault models inadequate for agentic infrastructure.

Method: EBFT augments the Byzantine fault bound with two confidence-indexed quantities: eδ bounds coherent invalid endorsements outside the Byzantine set (semantic safety risk), and uε bounds unusable validator support that degrades liveness. The paper derives quorum-threshold conditions for semantic validity, agreement, and liveness, and outlines a calibration methodology for estimating these budgets.

Result: The analysis shows that adding nominally distinct agents improves fault tolerance only when it measurably reduces the upper-tail concentration of invalid endorsements or unusable support. Standard BFT quorum conditions are insufficient to guarantee semantic validity in the presence of epistemic faults.

Conclusion: Epistemic faults represent a distinct failure mode not captured by traditional Byzantine fault models. EBFT provides a formal framework for reasoning about semantic safety and liveness in agentic distributed systems, with practical calibration guidance for threshold selection.

Abstract: State machine replication (SMR) and Byzantine fault-tolerant (BFT) consensus guarantee agreement despite a bounded number of arbitrary, colluding faulty participants. However, these guarantees rely on participants outside this set correctly executing the protocol's transition semantics. Agentic validators expose a weaker boundary: an authenticated, responsive, non-equivocating, and protocol-compliant reasoning participant may still endorse a semantically invalid transition due to reasoning errors.
  We call this failure mode an epistemic fault, and the collective phenomenon the Honest Quorum Problem (where "honest" means protocol-compliant, not semantically correct). Such a quorum can satisfy ordinary checks while forming a certificate for an invalid transition. Thus, agreement alone does not guarantee semantic validity or execution safety. Furthermore, because agentic validators often share model weights, training distributions, prompts, or toolchains, they are highly susceptible to correlated epistemic faults.
  We define Epistemic Byzantine Fault Tolerance (EBFT), a fault-tolerance model for agentic infrastructure and post-deterministic distributed systems. EBFT augments the conventional Byzantine fault bound with two separate, confidence-indexed quantities: $e_δ$ bounds coherent invalid endorsements outside the Byzantine set, and $u_ε$ bounds unusable validator support that degrades liveness. These quantities characterize semantic safety risk and liveness degradation independently. We derive quorum-threshold conditions for semantic validity, consensus agreement, liveness, and feasible threshold selection, and outline a calibration methodology for estimating these budgets. We show that adding nominally distinct agents improves fault tolerance only when it measurably reduces the upper-tail concentration of invalid endorsements or unusable support.

</details>


### [144] [When Do Multi-Agent Systems Help? An Information Bottleneck Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16133)
*Wendi Yu,Lianhao Zhou,Xiangjue Dong,Sai Sudarshan Barath,Declan Staunton,Byung-Jun Yoon,Xiaoning Qian,James Caverlee,Shuiwang Ji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides an information bottleneck perspective to explain when and why multi-agent systems (MAS) outperform single-agent systems (SAS). The key insight is that MAS uses bounded relay messages between isolated local contexts, creating a trade-off between compression (efficiency) and information loss (task-relevant data). An effective parameter β controls this balance, with MAS gains occurring when context reduction outweighs relay information loss. Experiments across 5 benchmarks and 3 model scales confirm: MAS helps for weaker models with near-sufficient relays but hurts for stronger models where compression causes net information loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The advantages of multi-agent systems over single-agent systems remain unclear and inconsistently observed across settings, motivating the need for a principled theoretical framework to explain when and why MAS helps or hurts.

Method: The authors formalize the MAS vs. SAS comparison from an information bottleneck perspective, introducing a parameter β that captures the trade-off between context compression and information loss in bounded relay messages. They conduct 18 controlled experiments across five benchmarks and three model scales to validate the theory.

Result: MAS consistently helps when relays are near-sufficient, especially for weaker models. MAS gains shrink or reverse when relays incur information loss, particularly for stronger models that already extract useful information from redundant context and thus gain little from compression.

Conclusion: Multi-agent design is fundamentally an information-bottleneck optimization problem. This perspective explains when bounded inter-agent communication helps or hurts, providing a principled framework for deciding between MAS and SAS architectures.

Abstract: LLM powered multi-agent systems (MAS) have emerged as a promising paradigm for complex tasks. However, their advantages over single-agent systems (SAS) remain unclear, with performance varying inconsistently across settings. Here, we provide an information bottleneck perspective on elucidating the differences between MAS and SAS. Specifically, our key observation is that a SAS accumulates its full reasoning trace in one shared context, while a MAS uses isolated local contexts connected by bounded relay messages. We show that, under infinite relay bandwidth, any SAS can be simulated by a MAS that transmits the full upstream context. Thus, the nontrivial advantage of MAS arises under bounded relays, where compression introduces a fundamental trade-off: reducing redundant context can improve efficiency, but may also incur loss of task-relevant information. We formalize this trade-off as an information bottleneck controlled by an effective parameter $β$, which captures how the balance shifts with model capability, and shows that MAS gains arise when context reduction outweighs relay information loss. We conduct 18 controlled experiments across five benchmarks and three model scales to validate our theoretical studies. We observe that MAS consistently helps when relays are near-sufficient, especially for weaker models. In contrast, MAS gains shrink or reverse when relays incur information loss, especially for stronger models that can already extract useful information from redundant context and thus gain little from compression. Our study shows that multi-agent design is fundamentally an information-bottleneck optimization problem. This perspective explains when bounded inter-agent communication helps or hurts.

</details>


### [145] [Improving Improved Kernel PLS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16138)
*Ole-Christian Galbo Engstrøm*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes two algorithmic improvements to IKPLS (Improved Kernel Partial Least Squares) — a faster method for computing X rotations (R) via direct evaluation instead of term-by-term accumulation, and a novel derivation showing Y loadings (Q) can be computed in O(M) instead of O(KM) operations. Both modifications produce identical outputs to original IKPLS and yield speedups of ~2× on CPU and ~6× on GPU, with up to 100× for isolated steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IKPLS algorithms 1 and 2 are among the fastest PLS calibration methods, but their computations of X rotations (R) and Y loadings (Q) can be accelerated further, especially to leverage modern parallel hardware.

Method: For R, term-by-term accumulation is replaced by a direct evaluation strategy that parallelizes better. For Q, the author identifies novel equivalences showing each Y loading can be derived from already-computed quantities, reducing complexity from Θ(KM) to Θ(M) when M=1 or 2≤M&lt;K. Both modifications provably yield identical W, P, Q, R, T matrices as original IKPLS.

Result: Benchmarks with NumPy (CPU) and JAX (GPU) show speedups of up to 100× for isolated steps and approximately 2× (CPU) and 6× (GPU) for entire fits.

Conclusion: The proposed improvements significantly accelerate IKPLS without altering the output, and are implemented in the open-source Python package ikpls.

Abstract: Improved Kernel Partial Least Squares (IKPLS) algorithms 1 and 2 are among the fastest PLS calibration algorithms. This article focuses on two shared steps, the computation of the $\mathbf{X}$ rotations, $\mathbf{R}$, and the $\mathbf{Y}$ loadings, $\mathbf{Q}$, and accelerates both. For $\mathbf{R}$, term-by-term accumulation is replaced by a direct evaluation strategy that requires the same number of multiplications but parallelizes better on modern hardware. For $\mathbf{Q}$, I identify - to the best of my knowledge, for the first time - equivalences showing that each $\mathbf{Y}$ loading is obtainable, up to explicitly derived constants, from quantities already computed earlier in the same iteration, and I exploit them in IKPLS to reduce the cost of each loading from $Θ\left(KM\right)$ to $Θ\left(M\right)$ operations whenever $M = 1$ or $2 \leq M < K$, with $K$ predictor variables (number of columns in $\mathbf{X}$) and $M$ response variables (number of columns in $\mathbf{Y}$). Both improvements provably yield exactly the same $\mathbf{W}$, $\mathbf{P}$, $\mathbf{Q}$, $\mathbf{R}$, and $\mathbf{T}$ as the original algorithms. Benchmarks with NumPy (CPU) and JAX (GPU) show speedups of up to two orders of magnitude for the isolated steps and of approximately $2\times$ (CPU) and $6\times$ (GPU) for entire fits. Both improvements are implemented in the free, open-source Python package \texttt{ikpls}.

</details>


### [146] [PRISA: Proactive Infrastructure LiDAR Framework for Intersection Safety Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16156)
*Tam Bang,Hussam Abubakr,Emiliano de la Garza Villarreal,Truc Phuong Nguyen,Austin Harris,Toru Hirano,Mina Sartipi,Yunfei Xu,Hoang H. Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PRISA is a modular infrastructure LiDAR framework for real-time, privacy-preserving intersection safety monitoring. It uses roadside sensors with automatic site-specific training data curation, trajectory prediction, and dual surrogate safety evaluation (TTC for longitudinal conflicts, PPET for crossing/VRU conflicts). Evaluated on R-LiViT dataset and deployed on NVIDIA Jetson AGX Thor at a live intersection, achieving 194 ms end-to-end latency over a 2.4-second horizon.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urban intersections are hazardous due to complex multi-agent interactions involving vehicles and VRUs (pedestrians, cyclists). There is a need for continuous, real-time monitoring systems that can anticipate conflicts before they become crashes, while preserving privacy and functioning in low-light conditions.

Method: PRISA consists of two layers: (1) a sensing and perception layer using privacy-preserving, low-light-robust roadside LiDAR sensors, and (2) a plug-and-play risk assessment module. The module automatically curates site-specific training data from accumulated perception outputs to train a trajectory prediction model without manual annotation, then deploys it for continuous motion forecasting and dual surrogate safety evaluation using TTC (longitudinal) and PPET (crossing/VRU) metrics.

Result: Evaluated on the R-LiViT dataset and deployed on NVIDIA Jetson AGX Thor at a live intersection in Chattanooga, TN. PPET-based assessment achieves 194 ms end-to-end latency over a 2.4-second predictive horizon, with TTC-based detection and perception meeting real-time constraints.

Conclusion: PRISA demonstrates practical feasibility for proactive, real-time multi-agent intersection safety monitoring at the edge, offering a privacy-preserving and annotation-free solution.

Abstract: Urban intersections are among the most hazardous locations in road networks, posing significant risks to vehicles and vulnerable road users (VRUs) such as pedestrians and cyclists. The complexity of multi-agent interactions demands continuous, real-time monitoring systems capable of anticipating conflicts before they escalate into crashes. We present PRISA, a modular infrastructure LiDAR framework leveraging privacy-preserving, low-light-robust roadside sensors for long-term traffic observation and real-time risk detection at the edge. The framework comprises two core components: a sensing and perception layer and a plug-and-play risk assessment module. The latter automatically curates site-specific training data from accumulated perception outputs to train a trajectory prediction model without manual annotation. It then deploys the trained model for continuous motion forecasting and dual surrogate safety evaluation, using Time-to-Collision (TTC) for longitudinal conflicts and Predicted Post-Encroachment Time (PPET) for crossing and VRU-involved interactions. PRISA is evaluated on the public R-LiViT dataset and deployed on an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Thor at a live signalized intersection in Chattanooga, Tennessee. PPET-based assessment operates at 194~ms end-to-end latency over a 2.4-second predictive horizon, with TTC-based detection and perception remaining within real-time constraints, demonstrating practical feasibility for proactive multi-agent intersection safety monitoring.

</details>


### [147] [When Does Muon Help Agentic Reinforcement Learning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16169)
*Kai Ruan,Jinghao Lin,Zihe Huang,Ziqi Zhou,Qianshan Wei,Xuan Wang,Hao Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Muon optimizer outperforms AdamW in sparse-reward agentic RL (ALFWorld), boosting final success from 0.290 to 0.546 (+88%) with GiGPO, and reaching 0.901 with GraphGPO at low learning rates, showing that optimizer choice matters for RL post-training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Muon is known to be competitive with AdamW for large-scale pre-training, but its effectiveness for reinforcement-learning (RL) post-training, especially in sparse-reward agentic settings, has not been studied.

Method: Conducted matched single-seed comparisons between Muon and AdamW on ALFWorld using Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct under GiGPO, GRPO, and GraphGPO, applying Muon only to hidden weight matrices and varying advantage estimators and learning rates.

Result: Muon with GiGPO raised final-window validation success from 0.290 to 0.546 (+88%) while AdamW controls retained no post-update success. At 1e-5, GraphGPO Muon reached 0.901 success, improved normalized validation AUC from 0.399 to 0.556, and reached 0.5 and 0.75 success 30 and 60 updates earlier.

Conclusion: Muon can benefit agentic RL, and the policy optimizer, advantage estimator, and learning rate should be studied jointly. Multi-seed and cross-task validation remain open.

Abstract: Muon is competitive with AdamW in large-scale pre-training, but its value for reinforcement-learning (RL) post-training remains unclear. We study vanilla Muon in sparse-reward agentic RL through matched single-seed comparisons with AdamW on ALFWorld using Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct. Under Group-in-Group Policy Optimization (GiGPO), applying Muon only to hidden weight matrices raises final-window validation success from 0.290 to 0.546 (+88%); high-rate AdamW controls retain no post-update success. The effect depends on the advantage estimator and learning rate. At 3e-5, Muon improves GRPO from 0.161 to 0.268, whereas GraphGPO's late-window gap narrows near saturation. At 1e-5, GraphGPO Muon reaches 0.901, raises normalized validation AUC from 0.399 to 0.556, and reaches 0.5 and 0.75 success 30 and 60 updates earlier, respectively. These exploratory results show that Muon can benefit agentic RL and motivate studying the policy optimizer, advantage estimator, and learning rate jointly. Multi-seed and cross-task validation remain open.

</details>


### [148] [Behaviour-Conditioned Neural Processes for Adaptive Residential Short-Term Load Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16168)
*Ramin Soleimani,Andrea Visentin,Dirk Pesch*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a behaviour-conditioned Attentive Neural Process (ANP) for residential short-term load forecasting that embeds inferred household behavioural structure into the probabilistic forecasting mechanism. Using a discrete latent variable for behaviour representation and a continuous latent variable for shared uncertainty, the model is trained with weak supervision from clustering-derived labels. Experiments on the SGSC dataset show 7.9% MAE and 6.9% CRPS improvements over ANP, with best gains under limited context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Residential STLF is challenging due to heterogeneous, temporally variable household demand shaped by diverse behavioural routines. Existing approaches often use behavioural structure only as an external grouping signal rather than embedding it within the forecasting mechanism.

Method: Behaviour-conditioned Attentive Neural Process framework: discrete latent variable for behavioural structure inferred from context; continuous latent variable for shared functional uncertainty; clustering-derived weak supervision for training; test-time conditioning via context-inferred class distributions without ground-truth labels.

Result: Best variant achieves 7.9% MAE and 6.9% CRPS reduction over ANP. Largest gains under limited context. Lower RMSE than fixed-window baselines across all horizons with competitive MAE.

Conclusion: Single-model, uncertainty-aware forecasting across heterogeneous households, contexts, and horizons is feasible by embedding behavioural structure into Neural Process-based probabilistic models.

Abstract: Residential short-term load forecasting (STLF) is challenging because household demand is heterogeneous, temporally variable, and shaped by diverse behavioural routines. This work investigates whether inferred behavioural structure can be embedded within the forecasting mechanism of a Neural Process-based probabilistic model, rather than used only as an external grouping signal, for context-conditioned residential STLF. We propose a behaviour-conditioned Attentive Neural Process framework that treats each load profile as a forecasting task. Behavioural structure is represented by a discrete latent variable inferred from the available context and used for behaviour-conditioned decoder conditioning, while a continuous latent variable captures shared functional uncertainty across heterogeneous profiles. To enable conditioning without ground-truth behavioural labels, clustering-derived information provides weak supervision during training, whereas test-time conditioning relies only on context-inferred class distributions. Experiments on the Smart Grid, Smart City (SGSC) dataset use user-disjoint train/validation/test splits, variable context lengths, and multi-step forecast horizons, with comparisons against a label-agnostic ANP baseline and fixed-window deterministic STLF baselines. The proposed variants improve MAE and CRPS over ANP across horizons and context settings, with the largest gains under limited context. The best-performing variant achieves average reductions of 7.9% in MAE and 6.9% in CRPS relative to ANP. Compared with fixed-window baselines, this variant achieves lower RMSE across all evaluated horizons while maintaining competitive MAE, suggesting fewer large prediction deviations under heterogeneous consumption patterns. These results support single-model, uncertainty-aware forecasting across heterogeneous households, contexts, and horizons.

</details>


### [149] [Physics-enhanced reinforcement learning for real-time optimal control of dynamical systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16177)
*Matteo Tomasetto,Nicolò Botteghi,Gabriele Bruni,Andrea Manzoni*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PEARL is a physics-enhanced reinforcement learning paradigm that combines automatic differentiation and adjoint-based sensitivity analysis to efficiently control high-dimensional, parametric dynamical systems, demonstrating superior sample efficiency and generalization on unsteady flow navigation problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL algorithms are sample-inefficient and suffer from the curse of dimensionality when applied to high-dimensional dynamical systems with sparse sensors/actuators, limiting their practical deployment for nonlinear control.

Method: PEARL introduces an actor-adjoint algorithm that uses automatic differentiation for short-horizon policy gradients and adjoint-based sensitivities for long-term returns approximated via neural networks, leveraging differentiable dynamics of the environment.

Result: PEARL outperforms state-of-the-art RL algorithms on parametric navigation in unsteady flows, achieves high sample efficiency, generalizes across scenarios, and scales to high-dimensional state/action spaces without dimensionality reduction or multi-agent strategies.
