Yet this work has not produced a market that rewards trustworthiness. Firms that invest seriously in safety, fairness, and oversight cannot consistently prove to consumers, regulators, and shareholders that their systems go beyond the bare minimum of compliance. What is missing is a way for society to recognize or compare the difference. The result is a trust gap: a structural condition in which responsible development efforts happen inside organizations but produce no external, independently recognized and verifiable signal of trustworthy outcomes. We argue this gap is sustained in part because of a focus on responsible AI (a matter of internal process) as opposed to trustworthy AI (a matter of independently verifiable real-world outcomes), and that it persists because of three compounding failures: (1) the market cannot distinguish trustworthy systems from their imitations; (2) evaluation targets models and outputs rather than deployed sociotechnical systems and their outcomes; (3) the measurement ecosystem is oriented toward avoiding harm rather than demonstrating benefit. Reviewing existing AI governance instruments and comparing them to certification regimes in healthcare, sustainability, and security, we show that none integrate a governance baseline, independently verified positive-outcome evidence, and market signaling in a single framework. We propose independent, outcome-oriented certification as the connective layer that can close the trust gap, complementing regulation and internal governance by making trustworthiness measurable, comparable, and commercially rewarded.

</details>


### [175] [SciForge: An AI-Native, Multimodal Workbench for Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16038)
*SciForge Team,Zhangyang Gao,Minghao Fang,Yifei Liu,Hanhui Yang,Xinyu Gu,Shixiang Tang,Siqi Sun,Lei Bai,Cheng Tan,Mengdi Liu,Hao Wu,Shuizhou Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SciForge is an open-source multimodal AI workbench for scientific research that preserves heterogeneous research artifacts as a coherent, auditable state. It features goal-scoped governance, translate-then-reason multimodal processing, evidence governance for provenance, collaborative team science, and practical real-world applications demonstrated through eight end-to-end cases including gene discovery and de novo protein design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific work increasingly involves diverse artifacts (papers, code, datasets, model outputs, etc.) but existing general-purpose AI assistants fail to preserve these objects as a coherent, auditable research state, hindering reproducibility and traceability.

Method: SciForge is built on five pillars: (i) goal-scoped scientific decision governance with review gates; (ii) translate-then-reason approach for multimodal input via domain translators; (iii) evidence governance with provenance chains and audit findings; (iv) collaborative team science with multi-role decision governance; and (v) real-world application scenarios. The system combines a thin interaction layer, contextual research capability patterns, an Agent Runtime and Workflow Engine, an Evidence-DAG audit sidecar, and a Scientific Model Router.

Result: Eight end-to-end user cases are demonstrated, including multi-day agentic research sprints for gene discovery, AI-guided de novo protein design, molecular optimization, and genome-to-BGC discovery. The system currently runs as a desktop application with mobile supervision support.

Conclusion: SciForge presents a research-native AI workbench that modularizes agent-accessible services while reserving the GUI for human judgment. It enables auditable, goal-oriented, multimodal, and collaborative scientific research. Future releases will deepen team collaboration. The system is open-source.

Abstract: Scientific work increasingly spans heterogeneous artifacts -- papers, code, datasets, scientific file formats, model outputs, figures, manuscripts, and team decisions -- yet general-purpose AI assistants rarely preserve these objects as a coherent, auditable research state. We present SciForge, a multimodal research-native AI workbench that reserves the graphical interface for human judgment while search, parsing, model routing, workflow execution, plotting, writing, and presentation generation run as modular agent-accessible services. SciForge is built around five pillars: (i) \emph{goal-scoped scientific decision governance} for \textbf{goal-oriented} research, with review gates and shared review surfaces; (ii) \emph{translate-then-reason} for \textbf{multimodal} input, routing scientific objects through domain translators before the agent reasons; (iii) \emph{evidence governance} for \textbf{auditable} traceability, linking claims to provenance chains and audit findings; (iv) \emph{collaborative team science} for \textbf{collaborative} research, enabling multi-role decision governance, with shared team workspaces planned for future releases; and (v) \emph{real-world application scenarios} for \textbf{practical} impact, demonstrated through eight end-to-end user cases, with flagship demonstrations including multi-day agentic research sprints for gene discovery, AI-guided de novo protein design, molecular optimization, and genome-to-BGC discovery. The system combines a thin interaction layer, contextual research capability patterns, an Agent Runtime and Workflow Engine, an Evidence-DAG audit sidecar and a Scientific Model Router. SciForge currently runs as a desktop application, with mobile supervision support; future releases will deepen team collaboration. The system is open-source and available at https://github.com/AGI4Sci/SciForge

</details>


### [176] [Harmonizing AI Safety Thresholds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16112)
*Wilber Sean Anterola,Matthew Ball,Luis F. Lafuerza,Markov Grey*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops a methodology for deriving harmonized capability thresholds across three risk domains (cyber misuse, biological misuse, automated AI R&D) to address inconsistencies in frontier AI safety standards. For misuse risks, it uses an expected-harm-based risk-modeling approach; for AI R&D, it bases thresholds on observed AI progress rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier AI companies have published capability thresholds that differ substantially, making verification and comparison difficult. Without common minimum thresholds, risk mitigation may be inconsistent, creating a potential race to the bottom in safety standards.

Method: For misuse risks (cyber and biological), the authors take expected harm as the key primitive and use an explicit risk-modeling approach accounting for risk channels and model release conditions. For automated AI R&D, they base the proposed threshold on the observed rate of AI progress rather than expected harm.

Result: A methodology for deriving harmonized thresholds across three risk domains, expanding upon prior work and highlighting existing empirical gaps and limitations.

Conclusion: Common minimum thresholds are needed to prevent inconsistent risk mitigation and a race to the bottom in safety standards; the proposed methodology addresses this for cyber misuse, biological misuse, and automated AI R&D risks.

Abstract: Frontier AI companies have published capability thresholds that differ substantially, making it difficult for third parties to verify whether a threshold has been crossed or to compare requirements across companies. Moreover, without common minimum thresholds, risk mitigation may be inconsistent, creating a potential race to the bottom in safety standards. We develop a methodology for deriving harmonized thresholds across three risk domains. For misuse risks (cyber and biological), we take expected harm as the key primitive and use an explicit risk-modeling approach that accounts for risk channels and model release conditions. For automated AI R&D, we base our proposed threshold on the observed rate of AI progress rather than expected harm. Our analysis expands upon prior work and highlights existing empirical gaps and limitations.

</details>


### [177] [CRAFT: Clustering Rubrics to Diagnose Weak LLM Capabilities and Generate Targeted Fine-Tuning Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16122)
*Vipul Gupta,Zihao Wang,Razvan-Gabriel Dumitru,MohammadHossein Rezaei,Aakash Sabharwal,Yunzhong He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CRAFT converts rubric-based evaluation into hierarchical capability diagnoses, generating targeted finetuning data that outperforms prompt-level clustering and random baselines across finance and legal domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations identify where models fail (weak examples/topics) but not why they fail, leaving underlying capability failures implicit. The goal is to produce actionable, model-specific capability diagnoses that directly guide post-training data generation.

Method: CRAFT treats each grading criterion as a capability probe, extracts capability descriptions from prompt-rubric pairs, clusters them into a hierarchical capability tree, scores the target model at each node, and dynamically selects low-performing nodes at the granularity where failures are clearest. These weak capabilities then direct targeted SFT data generation.

Result: On four open-source models across finance and legal domains with 13 held-out benchmarks, CRAFT achieves the strongest finance domain average for all four models under repeated temperature decoding. In legal, it is strongest for three of four models and within variance bands on the fourth.

Conclusion: Diagnosing weaknesses at the level of rubric criteria yields a sharper picture of a model's capability failures and produces measurably better models after finetuning, compared to prompt-level clustering or untargeted data generation.

Abstract: Evaluations should do more than measure a models current performance. They should tell us what to fix for the next model iteration and provide a way to generate targeted post training data. Most evaluation pipelines identify weak examples, topics, or categories, but they leave the underlying capability failure implicit: they say where a model fails, not why. We introduce CRAFT, a method that converts any rubric based evaluation dataset into a model specific diagnosis of weak capabilities. CRAFT treats each grading criterion as a capability probe: it extracts a capability description from every prompt rubric pair, clusters these descriptions into a hierarchical capability tree, scores the target model at every node, and selects low performing nodes dynamically across tree levels, at the granularity where each failure is clearest. The selected weak capabilities then direct the generation of targeted supervised finetuning data. Holding the data generation, finetuning, and evaluation setup fixed, we compare CRAFT against prompt level EvalTree clustering and untargeted random generation on four open source models, two professional domains (finance and legal), and 13 held out benchmarks disjoint from the diagnostic data. CRAFT achieves the strongest finance domain average for all four models under repeated temperature decoding; on legal domain, it is strongest for three of four models and remains within the decoding variance bands of the best baseline on the fourth. Diagnosing weaknesses at the level of rubric criteria, rather than prompts or categories, thus yields both a sharper picture of what a model cannot do and measurably better models after finetuning on that diagnosis.

</details>


### [178] [Beyond a Single Direction: Chain-of-Thought Disrupts Simple Steering of Refusal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26772)
*Kia-Jüng Yang,Dominik Meier,Jiachen Zhao,Terry Ruas,Bela Gipp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Refusal in large reasoning models (LRMs) depends on both residual stream activations and chain-of-thought (CoT), making activation steering alone ineffective (39% refusal reversal) but much more effective when the CoT is removed (70%) or regenerated under steering (94%). The CoT can independently carry and reconstruct compliance signals even after steering is removed (48% effect retention), revealing a joint encoding mechanism that makes LRMs robust to activation-level interventions but exposes a new attack surface via CoT manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how refusal control mechanisms differ in large reasoning models (LRMs) compared to standard instruction-tuned LLMs, particularly due to the presence of chain-of-thought (CoT) traces that introduce a dynamic internal state complicating alignment interventions.

Method: Activation steering experiments on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-LLaMA-8B under three conditions: (1) steering with fixed CoT, (2) steering with CoT removed entirely, (3) two-stage intervention where CoT is regenerated under activation steering, followed by evaluating the steering effect when only the new CoT is used without continued steering.

Result: Activation steering reverses refusal in only 39% of cases with fixed CoT, 70% without CoT, and 94% in two-stage intervention where CoT is regenerated under steering. The regenerated CoT alone retains 48% of the compliance effect even after steering is removed.

Conclusion: Refusal in LRMs is jointly encoded in both residual stream activations and CoT traces. This dual encoding makes LRMs more robust to activation-level interventions alone, but the CoT's ability to independently carry and reconstruct compliance signals introduces a novel attack surface for potential alignment subversion.

Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) generate chain-of-thought (CoT) traces before producing final outputs, introducing a dynamic internal state that may complicate control mechanisms such as refusal. Unlike instruction-tuned LLMs, where refusal is mediated by a single directional subspace, refusal in large reasoning models (LRMs) additionally depends on the CoT. In DeepSeek-R1-Distill-LLaMA-8B, activation steering reverses refusal in only 39% of cases when the CoT is kept fixed, but removing the CoT entirely increases this to 70%, indicating that the CoT actively reinforces refusal. In a two-stage intervention where the model regenerates its CoT under activation steering, refusal is reversed in 94% of cases, while the resulting CoT alone retains 48% of this effect even after steering is removed. This suggests that the CoT can carry and reconstruct the compliance signal independently. These findings indicate that refusal in LRMs is jointly encoded in residual stream activations and CoT. This joint activation makes LRM more robust against activation-level interventions alone, but exposes CoT to a possible alternative surface attack.

</details>


<div id='cs.GR'></div>

# cs.GR [[Back]](#toc)

### [179] [Rendering 3D Gaussians on a Graph Processor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15951)
*Nicholas Fry,Ignacio Alzugaray,Mark Pupilli,Paul H. J. Kelly,Andrew J. Davison*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: First implementation of 3D Gaussian rendering on an IPU (1,472 tiles, SRAM-only), using BSP model and NEWS-grid routing, with analysis of bottlenecks (inter-tile bandwidth, SRAM capacity, workload imbalance) and implications for DRAM-free architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether SRAM-only, tile-based architectures (exemplified by IPUs) can efficiently execute 3D Gaussian Splatting rendering, and to investigate constraints that approximate on-sensor processing architectures.

Method: Each tile owns a screen-space framebuffer region; Gaussian primitives are routed via Manhattan-distance hops on a NEWS grid, then distributed to overlapping neighbours in an expanding tree pattern. Computation follows the BSP model with compile-time-defined inter-tile communication.

Result: Identified key bottlenecks: inter-tile bandwidth, per-tile SRAM capacity, and workload imbalance from non-uniform Gaussian density. Demonstrated exploitation of spatial and temporal locality via local data transfer between cores.

Conclusion: Suggests that direct inter-SM communication on GPUs could reduce DRAM access, and raises broader questions for future on-sensor and DRAM-free architectures.

Abstract: We present the first implementation of a 3D Gaussian renderer on an Intelligence Processing Unit (IPU), comprising 1,472 independent tiles with only on-chip SRAM; constraints that approximate properties of efficient sensor-processor architectures. Our input scenes are 3D Gaussian maps from real-world sequences. Each tile 'owns' a screen-space region of the framebuffer; Gaussian primitives are routed to destination tiles via Manhattan-distance hops on a north-east-west-south (NEWS) grid, then distributed to overlapping neighbours in an expanding tree pattern. Computation follows the IPU's Bulk Synchronous Parallel (BSP) model, with inter-tile communication defined at compile time. We show this hardware allows us to exploit spatial and temporal locality by enabling local data transfer between cores. We evaluate the bottlenecks in this SRAM-only implementation: inter-tile bandwidth, per-tile SRAM capacity, and workload imbalance from non-uniform Gaussian density. We analyse how these constraints affect performance and render quality. This exploration raises broader questions for conventional GPUs and 3D representations, suggesting that direct inter-SM (streaming multiprocessor) communication might offer ways to reduce DRAM access in GPU kernels. We discuss these implications for the future of on-sensor and DRAM-free architectures. Project page: https://nmjfry.github.io/ipu-3dgs/

</details>


<div id='cs.MM'></div>

# cs.MM [[Back]](#toc)

### [180] [Proof-Carrying Multimodal Timelines: Finite-Trace Modal Certificates for Video-Audio Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15285)
*Faruk Alpay,Hamdi Alakkad*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: The paper presents a finite-trace modal monitoring framework for video-audio consistency that produces reproducible certificates (with trace hash, verdict, violating indices, defect scores, etc.) rather than opaque clip-level scores, enabling local temporal failure diagnosis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal video systems expose only clip-level scores, hiding local temporal inconsistencies (e.g., speech-speaker mismatch, audio-visual event misalignment). A fine-grained, interpretable, and reproducible method is needed to diagnose where and why a video becomes inconsistent.

Method: The approach formulates video-audio consistency as finite-trace modal monitoring over synchronized visual, audio, and subtitle/OCR atoms. A formula library defines five consistency properties (speech-speaker agreement, audio-visual event agreement, subtitle-video agreement, scene continuity, edit-induced temporal shock). A certificate records trace hash, formula identifier, verdict, violating indices, defect score, first counterexample window, execution engine, and certificate hash. The artifact decodes MP4 video, extracts CLIP visual atoms and AST audio atoms, runs dense counterfactual perturbation sweeps, and outputs CSV traces and JSON certificates.

Result: The system produces structured, verifiable certificates that localize temporal failures (violating indices, counterexample windows) and defect scores, enabling independent reconstruction by a finite-trace checker once the atom trace is fixed.

Conclusion: The certificate is not a proof of detector correctness but a reproducible witness for trace-based consistency checking, allowing transparent diagnosis of local temporal inconsistencies in multimodal video.

Abstract: Multimodal video systems often expose clip-level scores while hiding the local temporal failure that makes a video inconsistent. We formulate video-audio consistency as finite-trace modal monitoring over synchronized visual, audio, and subtitle/OCR atoms. A formula library specifies speech-speaker agreement, audio-visual event agreement, subtitle-video agreement, scene continuity, and edit-induced temporal shock. A certificate records the trace hash, formula identifier, verdict, violating indices, defect score, first counterexample window, execution engine, and certificate hash. The certificate is not a proof that a detector is correct; it is a reproducible witness that the finite-trace checker can independently reconstruct once the atom trace is fixed. The artifact decodes real MP4 video, extracts CLIP visual atoms, extracts AST audio atoms, runs dense counterfactual perturbation sweeps, emits CSV traces and JSON certificates, and renders manuscript figures from those files.

</details>


### [181] [AV-JEPA: Extending LeJEPA to Audio-Visual Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15295)
*Benjamin Robson,Santeri Mentu,Wenshuai Zhao,Arno Solin*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: AV-JEPA extends LeJEPA to audio-visual self-supervised learning using early-fusion Vision Transformer and modality dropout, achieving competitive performance on VGGSound and AudioSet with a clean architecture lacking decoder, EMA teacher, complex losses, or contrastive negatives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an elegant and minimalistic multimodal extension of LeJEPA for audio-visual self-supervised learning that avoids complex components like decoders, EMA teachers, multi-term losses, and contrastive negatives.

Method: Uses an early-fusion Vision Transformer with modality dropout as masking; trains the model to align embeddings of global and per-modality local views; employs the SIGReg objective for theoretically optimal distribution.

Result: Competitive classification performance: 57.1% top-1 on VGGSound and 32.7 mAP on AudioSet; supports zero-shot audio-video retrieval out of the box.

Conclusion: AV-JEPA achieves cross-modal alignment in latent space with a remarkably clean architecture while delivering strong results on audio-visual benchmarks.

Abstract: We present AV-JEPA, an elegant multimodal extension of LeJEPA to audio-visual self-supervised learning. Using an early-fusion Vision Transformer and modality dropout as masking, the model is trained to align the embeddings of global and per-modality local views, while the SIGReg objective encourages a theoretically optimal distribution. This achieves cross-modal alignment in the latent space, resulting in a remarkably clean architecture with no decoder, EMA teacher, complex multi-term losses, or contrastive negatives. The proposed AV-JEPA backbone delivers competitive classification performance on VGGSound (57.1% top-1) and AudioSet (32.7 mAP) and supports zero-shot audio-video retrieval out of the box.

</details>


### [182] [MLLM-DataEngine: Closing the Loop of Multimodal Instruction Tuning Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15299)
*Zhiyuan Zhao,Bin Wang,Linke Ouyang,Yiqi Lin,Pan Zhang,Xiaoyi Dong,Jiaqi Wang,Conghui He*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: MLLM-DataEngine is a closed-loop system that iteratively analyzes model weaknesses, generates targeted incremental datasets, and improves MLLMs without human intervention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing instruction fine-tuning dataset collection methods are separate from model benchmarking, leading to poor targeting and inefficient capability improvement in MLLMs. The authors aim to bridge data generation, model training, and evaluation in a unified loop.

Method: (1) Adaptive Bad-case Sampling module to analyze model weaknesses from evaluation results; (2) Use GPT-4 with representative in-context examples and rich information to generate high-quality incremental data targeting specific capability deficits; (3) Iterative closed-loop of data generation → model training → evaluation.

Result: MLLM-DataEngine boosts MLLM capabilities in a targeted and automatic manner without human participants, outperforming prior separate data collection methods.

Conclusion: The proposed closed-loop system is an effective, general-purpose solution for MLLM data curation, with code, data, and model publicly released.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose MLLM-DataEngine, a novel closed-loop system that bridges data generation, model training, and evaluation. Within each loop iteration, the MLLM-DataEngine first analyzes the weakness of the model based on the evaluation results, then generates a proper incremental dataset for the next training iteration, and enhances the model capability iteratively. Compared with previous instruction fine-tuning dataset collection methods which are separate from the benchmarking, MLLM-DataEngine shows better targeting and can improve MLLMs's capabilities more effectively. Firstly, we propose an Adaptive Bad-case Sampling module, which can effectively analyze model weakness based on the benchmarking results and adjust the generation of incremental datasets flexibly. Secondly, in order to ensure high-quality data for specific capability types, the most representative in-context examples and abundant information are provided to GPT-4, which helps GPT-4 fully comprehend the model's weakness and further guarantees high-quality generated data. Through extensive experiments, we find MLLM-DataEngine could boost the MLLMs capability in a targeted and automatic manner without human participants. We hope MLLM-DataEngine could be a general solution for the following MLLMs data curation. Code, data, and model are available at https://github.com/opendatalab/MLLM-DataEngine.

</details>


### [183] [SPEED: One-Step Pixel Diffusion for High-quality Video Frame Interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15585)
*Zihao Zhang,Haoyu Zhao,Siqian Yang,Yidi Wu,Yudong Jiang,Zuxuan Wu*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: SPEED is a one-step pixel diffusion framework for Video Frame Interpolation that addresses limitations of latent diffusion (detail loss) and multi-step sampling (high memory/latency) through progressive multi-stage architecture with dynamic patch scaling, Noise-Update-Only Attention, and Drift-aware Timestep Sampling, achieving SOTA results with 63.3% faster inference and 10.6% lower memory usage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion-based VFI methods suffer from two critical limitations: (1) latent diffusion loses fine-grained details when reconstructing from latent to pixel space, and (2) multi-step sampling causes prohibitive memory consumption and inference latency.

Method: SPEED proposes: (1) a progressive multi-stage architecture with dynamic patch scaling for learning multi-scale motion, structural, and appearance representations; (2) a Noise-Update-Only Attention mechanism to prevent semantic degradation of clean condition frames while reducing computational overhead by ~50%; (3) a Drift-aware Timestep Sampling strategy with tailored training objective for direct pixel-space prediction, enabling one-step inference.

Result: On SNU-FILM, SPEED reduces LPIPS by 8.8%, delivers 63.3% faster inference, and 10.6% lower memory usage. On 4K benchmarks, it surpasses prior methods by up to 51.5% in LPIPS.

Conclusion: SPEED achieves state-of-the-art performance in Video Frame Interpolation by enabling efficient one-step pixel diffusion, overcoming the detail loss and high computational cost of prior diffusion-based approaches.

Abstract: Despite the success of diffusion models in Video Frame Interpolation (VFI), existing methods still suffer from two critical limitations. First, latent diffusion inevitably loses fine-grained details when reconstructing images from latent representations back to the pixel space. Second, multi-step sampling incurs prohibitive memory consumption and inference latency. To address these issues, we propose SPEED, a one-step pixel diffusion framework for high-quality VFI. Specifically, SPEED employs a progressive multi-stage architecture with dynamic patch scaling to effectively learn multi-scale motion, structural, and appearance representations. Furthermore, we propose a novel Noise-Update-Only Attention mechanism to prevent semantic degradation of the clean condition frames while reducing the computational overhead by nearly 50%. Besides, we introduce a Drift-aware Timestep Sampling strategy coupled with a tailored training objective to directly predict images in the pixel space, enabling one-step inference without compromising the quality of the generated frames. Extensive experiments show that SPEED achieves state-of-the-art performance. On SNU-FILM, SPEED reduces LPIPS by 8.8% while delivering 63.3% faster inference and 10.6% lower memory usage. On challenging 4K benchmarks, it further surpasses prior methods by up to 51.5% in LPIPS.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [184] [CHRONO-RESOLUTION: A Dependency Resolution Dataset at Release Points for npm, PyPI, and crates.io Packages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15315)
*Imranur Rahman,Jill Marley,Ranindya Paramitha,Laurie Williams*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents CHRONO-RESOLUTION, a dataset providing dependency resolution at release points for npm, PyPI, and crates.io packages, enriched with vulnerability data from OSV, to enable temporal dependency analysis in software ecosystems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dependency resolution at past points in time is impossible with current package management tools, which limits software evolution analysis and dynamic metric design in software ecosystems.

Method: The authors developed a methodology to construct dependency resolution at historical release points of packages, enriched with vulnerability data from OSV, and applied it to create the CHRONO-RESOLUTION dataset for npm, PyPI, and crates.io.

Result: CHRONO-RESOLUTION dataset was constructed, offering dependency resolution at release points for three major software ecosystems (npm, PyPI, crates.io) with integrated vulnerability information.

Conclusion: The released dataset enables practitioners and researchers to analyze the state of ecosystem dependency graphs at release points, supporting software evolution studies and dynamic metric computation.

Abstract: Dependency resolution at a specified point in time in the past can provide insight into software evolution in software ecosystems and facilitate the design of dynamic metrics (e.g., dependency freshness, dependency update rhythm). However, dependency resolution at specified points in time is not possible in major software ecosystems due to a lack of support from package management tools. The goal of this paper is to aid practitioners and researchers in analyzing the state of the ecosystem dependency graph at release points using an enriched dataset with dependency resolution at release points for npm, PyPI, and crates.io packages. In this paper, we present a methodology to construct dependency resolution at release points of packages in software ecosystems, which we enrich with vulnerability data from OSV. We apply our methodology to construct CHRONO-RESOLUTION, a dataset of dependency resolution at release points for npm, PyPI, and crates.io packages, and release it for future research.

</details>


### [185] [Two-Path Status Verification for Outbound Enterprise Messaging Pipelines: Webhook and Scheduled Polling Fallback Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15529)
*Devam Gupta*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a two-path status verification architecture for outbound enterprise messaging pipelines that addresses webhook delivery failures by combining a real-time event-driven primary path with a scheduled polling fallback that reconciles message status via provider API queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Outbound enterprise messaging pipelines face a fundamental reliability challenge: delivery status callbacks (webhooks) from messaging providers are subject to network failures, endpoint unavailability, and provider-side retry exhaustion, resulting in stale status records in the CRM system of record. A naive single-path architecture that relies exclusively on webhooks leaves a population of messages permanently in an intermediate state when callbacks fail.

Method: The architecture uses a two-path approach: (1) a primary path where real-time webhooks are received by a REST endpoint and published as internal events for asynchronous record update, and (2) a fallback path using a configurable scheduled polling job that detects records in transitional status after a configurable interval and queries the provider's status API directly to reconcile state. The design includes deduplication via idempotent upsert, a sync failure detection mechanism, and platform resource-limit considerations.

Result: The paper describes the event-driven primary path, the scheduler-based fallback, and the platform resource-limit considerations that shape each design decision. It is generalized from patterns observed in production CRM-native messaging systems built on multi-tenant platform-as-a-service infrastructure.

Conclusion: A two-path architecture (real-time webhook + scheduled polling fallback) provides resilience against webhook delivery failures in enterprise messaging pipelines, ensuring no messages remain permanently stuck in an intermediate state, with idempotent upsert handling deduplication.

Abstract: Outbound enterprise messaging pipelines face a fundamental reliability challenge: delivery status callbacks (webhooks) from messaging providers are subject to network failures, endpoint unavailability, and provider-side retry exhaustion, resulting in stale status records in the CRM system of record. A naive single-path architecture that relies exclusively on webhooks leaves a population of messages permanently in an intermediate state when callbacks fail. This paper presents a two-path status verification architecture, generalized from patterns observed in production CRM-native messaging systems built on multi-tenant platform-as-a-service infrastructure. The primary path uses a real-time webhook received by a REST endpoint, which publishes an internal event for asynchronous record update. The fallback path uses a configurable scheduled polling job that detects records still in transitional status after a configurable interval and queries the provider's status API directly to reconcile state. We describe the event-driven primary path, the scheduler-based fallback, deduplication via idempotent upsert, the sync failure detection mechanism, and the platform resource-limit considerations that shape each design decision.

</details>


### [186] [Understanding Agent-Reactive Bugs at the Model-Harness Boundary: An Empirical Study of LLM Agent Issue Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15684)
*Jingyi Chen,Songqiang Chen,Hengcheng Zhu,Jialun Cao,Jiasi Shen,Shing-Chi Cheung*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: First empirical study on Agent-Reactive (AR) bugs in LLM agents—bugs arising from the interaction between LLM responses and harness code. Analyzed 255 bug reports from Codex, Gemini-CLI, LangChain, and CrewAI, constructing a two-axis taxonomy of symptoms and LLM triggers. Found AR bugs often manifest as silent errors, are hard to detect due to lack of test oracles, and are difficult to reproduce due to LLM stochasticity. Revealed a mismatch between user-proposed fixes (harness-side guardrails) and developer responses (attributing to LLM or slow adoption), motivating oracles, reproduction support, and fault-localization techniques for AR bugs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior studies attributed LLM agent failures to limited model capabilities or harness-side defects (e.g., outdated APIs), but did not characterize bugs that emerge only from the joint interaction of LLM responses and harness code—termed Agent-Reactive (AR) bugs. This gap motivates the first dedicated empirical study of AR bugs.

Method: Manual analysis of 255 bug reports from four LLM agent systems (Codex, Gemini-CLI, LangChain, CrewAI). Constructed a two-axis taxonomy covering observable symptoms and the LLM behaviors that trigger AR bugs. Also analyzed fixes proposed by users vs. implemented by developers.

Result: Many AR bugs manifest as silent errors without well-defined test oracles. LLM response stochasticity complicates reproduction. Users advocate harness-side guardrails, while developers often attribute bugs to the LLM or are slow to adopt user-proposed fixes.

Conclusion: Highlights challenges specific to LLM agents, motivating the need for designing test oracles, reproduction support, and fault-localization techniques tailored to AR bugs.

Abstract: LLM agents span command-line interfaces (e.g., Codex) and agent frameworks (e.g., LangChain), integrating backend LLMs with harness code that parses model outputs, controls agent loops, and manages context. Both the harness and LLM-generated responses jointly shape an agent's execution. This architecture gives rise to bugs that cannot be readily understood by inspecting either component alone, because some bugs occur only when a particular LLM response elicits an abnormal reaction from the agent. Prior empirical studies of agent bugs have largely attributed failures either to limited model capabilities or to harness-side defects, such as outdated APIs and configuration misalignment, without characterizing these AR bugs. We conduct the first empirical study focused on agent-reactive (AR) bugs. Through manual analysis of 255 bug reports from Codex, Gemini-CLI, LangChain, and CrewAI, we construct a two-axis taxonomy covering observable symptoms and the LLM behaviors that trigger them. Our findings show that many AR bugs manifest as silent errors without well-defined test oracles, which makes detection difficult. The stochasticity of LLM responses further complicates bug reproduction. We additionally examine fixes proposed by users and implemented by developers. This analysis exposes a mismatch: users frequently advocate harness-side guardrails, whereas developers may attribute the issue to the LLM or respond slowly to user-proposed fixes. These findings point to the need for mechanisms that help users and developers understand the root causes and resolutions of AR bugs. Overall, the study highlights challenges specific to LLM agents and motivates the design of test oracles, reproduction support, and fault-localization techniques for AR bugs.

</details>


### [187] [Verified LLM-Driven Synthesis for Concept Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15718)
*Alcino Cunha*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper formalizes concept-based software design with reactions for synchronization, presents a CEGIS-style LLM-driven synthesis procedure for generating reaction designs that satisfy safety invariants, and evaluates natural-language prompts and positive/negative scenarios for steering synthesis, showing scenario-guided synthesis and LLM-driven scenario elicitation recover intended designs more consistently than invariants alone or prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable automatic verification of safety invariants in concept-based software systems and synthesize reaction designs that satisfy those invariants, while addressing the underconstrained nature of invariant-only synthesis by steering toward user-intended designs.

Method: Formal semantics for concepts and reactions; CEGIS-style LLM-driven synthesis for reaction designs; two steering approaches: natural-language prompts and positive/negative scenarios; LLM-driven scenario elicitation technique for early design exploration.

Result: Invariant-only synthesis reached verified designs quickly but produced inconsistent and sometimes implausible designs. Scenario-guided synthesis recovered intended designs more consistently than natural-language prompting, though minimal scenarios risk overfitting. LLM-driven scenario elicitation recovered intended designs in most variants with enough scenarios, but missed behaviors and non-determinism prevented reliable coverage.

Conclusion: Scenario-guided approaches, especially LLM-driven scenario elicitation, better constrain synthesis toward intended designs than invariants or prompts alone, but challenges remain in achieving full coverage due to non-determinism and missed behaviors.

Abstract: Concept Design structures software systems around concepts: user-facing, self-contained units of functionality with a focused purpose. Concepts are composed into applications using synchronization rules called reactions, which specify how actions in one concept trigger actions in others. This paper first gives a formal semantics for concepts and reactions, enabling automatic verification of safety invariants in applications developed with this methodology. It then presents a CEGIS-style, LLM-driven synthesis procedure for generating reaction designs that satisfy such invariants. Because many different designs can satisfy the same invariant, we study two ways of steering synthesis toward the user's intended design: natural-language prompts and positive/negative scenarios. We also propose an LLM-driven scenario elicitation technique to support early design exploration. In an evaluation on three applications and twelve design variants using one LLM configuration, invariant-only synthesis reached verified designs quickly but often produced inconsistent designs across runs, some of which were implausible, showing that invariants alone underconstrain the design task. Scenario-guided synthesis recovered intended designs more consistently than natural-language prompting, although minimal scenarios can lead to overfitting. LLM-driven scenario elicitation, where the user classifies proposed scenarios rather than authoring them from scratch, recovered the intended designs in most variants when enough scenarios were elicited, but missed behaviors and non-determinism prevented reliable coverage in all cases.

</details>


### [188] [GapForge: Directed Compiler Fuzzing via Coverage-Gap Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15762)
*Mingxuan Zhu,Qingyuan Liang,Junjie Chen,Zhihong Xue,Dan Hao*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: GapForge is an LLM-based test generation technique that targets coverage gaps in large compilers (GCC, LLVM) by prioritizing undercovered files, performing path-difference analysis on uncovered line spans, and using coverage feedback to guide prompt synthesis. It achieves 68.13% and 69.11% coverage on core modules, outperforming eight state-of-the-art techniques, and discovers 12 real-world compiler failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern compiler codebases (GCC, LLVM) are large and complex, leading to persistent long-tail coverage gaps that survive across releases. Existing test generation techniques produce limited coverage because they ignore target code characteristics or provide only coarse guidance, leaving substantial compiler regions insufficiently tested.

Method: GapForge is a three-step LLM-based technique: (1) coverage-driven file prioritization favoring large, undercovered files; (2) path-difference analysis pairing uncovered line spans with covered context to infer fine-grained triggering requirements (program structures and compilation options); (3) prompt synthesis from these requirements and previously failed prompts, using coverage feedback for next-round selection.

Result: On GCC 14.3.0 and LLVM 19.1.0, GapForge achieves 68.13% and 69.11% coverage on core modules within 72 hours, surpassing WhiteBox by 24,736 and 19,798 additional lines. It discovers 12 real-world compiler failures (5 GCC, 7 LLVM) including 8 crashes and 4 miscompilations.

Conclusion: GapForge demonstrates that targeted, region-level LLM-based test generation with coverage gap reasoning significantly outperforms existing techniques for large-scale compiler testing, effectively addressing long-tail coverage gaps and uncovering real-world bugs.

Abstract: Modern compiler codebases (e.g., GCC and LLVM) are large and complex, making comprehensive coverage across diverse code regions highly challenging. Most existing test generation techniques ignore characteristics of the target code, producing test programs that exercise only a limited subset of it. Consequently, substantial compiler regions remain insufficiently tested, leaving persistent long-tail coverage gaps that survive across releases. Even existing white-box techniques achieve limited coverage on large-scale compilers. To improve compiler coverage, especially for hard-to-reach edge regions, we present GapForge, a targeted LLM-based test generation technique that reasons about coverage gaps. Unlike program-driven techniques that generate diverse inputs without modeling which regions they exercise, and unlike whole-file summarization that yields coarse guidance, GapForge treats coverage gaps as explicit region-level targets in three steps. First, it prioritizes files via coverage-driven scoring that favors large, undercovered files. Second, it pairs each uncovered line span with its enclosing covered context and performs path-difference analysis to infer fine-grained triggering requirements: the program structures and compilation options needed to reach the uncovered region. Third, it synthesizes prompts from these requirements and previously failed prompts, using coverage feedback to guide next-round selection. On GCC 14.3.0 and LLVM 19.1.0, GapForge significantly outperforms eight state-of-the-art techniques. Within 72 hours, it achieves 68.13% and 69.11% coverage on core compiler modules in GCC and LLVM, surpassing the white-box technique WhiteFox by 24,736 and 19,798 additional lines, respectively. Moreover, GapForge discovers 12 real-world compiler failures (5 in GCC, 7 in LLVM), including 8 crashes and 4 miscompilations, with each component contributing to its performance.

</details>


### [189] [Making Agent-Mediated Contributions Governable: A Project-Level Governance Manifest for Open-Source AI Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15769)
*Jinjin Gao,Luyang Li,Shufen Guo,Ligang He,Xiaoning Sun*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper identifies a governance tension in OSS where AI-generated contributions outpace maintainer review capacity. Through a 50-repo audit, it finds no project-wide governability infrastructure. The authors propose the Agent Governance Manifest (AGM), a repository-hosted boundary resource linking contributor-side evidence preparation with maintainer-side verification. Evaluations show AGM significantly improves risk-label recovery (37/38 vs. 15/37) and perceived review support (6.14 vs. 3.27), with strong structural validity in feasibility checks. The work theorizes a three-layer framework of agent-readability, traceability, and governability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative AI and coding agents scale OSS contribution generation faster than maintainers can assess risk, evidence, and accountability, creating a central governance tension that existing solutions (agent-readability, traceability) do not fully address.

Method: Diagnostic audit of 50 GitHub repositories; development of Agent Governance Manifest (AGM); controlled reviewer-side evaluation (15 participants, 75 task-level outputs); contributor-side feasibility check (15 participants, 45 tasks).

Result: AGM-supported materials improved exact risk-label recovery (37/38 vs. 15/37) and perceived review support (6.14 vs. 3.27 on 1-7 scale). In feasibility check, all 45 final packages correctly represented core governance state, with 41 passing strict structural validation.

Conclusion: The study develops a three-layer framework (agent-readability, traceability, governability), theorizes agent-mediated contributions as governable boundary objects, and advances compliance-enabling digital innovation governance while preserving maintainer decision authority.

Abstract: Generative AI and coding agents are intensifying a central governance tension in open-source software (OSS): they scale contribution generation faster than maintainers can assess risk, evidence, and accountability. Existing responses improve agent-readability and traceability, but project rules must also organize contribution-specific risk, evidence, accountability, and review-gate states. We theorize this organizational arrangement as project-side governability infrastructure. A diagnostic audit of 50 GitHub repositories finds widespread general governance artifacts, observable agent-readability, and fragmented AI-governance cues, but no project-wide arrangement that coordinates shared rules, preparation obligations, verification rights, and maintainer decision authority across AI-mediated contribution workflows. We develop the Agent Governance Manifest (AGM) as a repository-hosted boundary resource and bidirectional governance contract linking contributor-side evidence preparation with maintainer-side verification. In a controlled reviewer-side evaluation with 15 participants and 75 task-level outputs, AGM-supported materials improved exact risk-label recovery (37/38 vs. 15/37) and perceived review support (6.14 vs. 3.27 on a 1-7 scale). In a contributor-side feasibility check, 15 participants completed 45 tasks; all final packages represented the core governance state correctly, and 41 passed strict structural validation. The study develops a three-layer framework of agent-readability, traceability, and governability, theorizes agent-mediated contributions as governable boundary objects, and advances compliance-enabling digital innovation governance while preserving maintainer decision authority.

</details>


### [190] [READU: Inconsistency-Driven Just-in-Time Detection and Repair of README Bugs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15780)
*Doehyun Baek,Kilian Krampf,Michael Pradel*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: READU is an inconsistency-driven technique that detects and repairs README bugs in repository-level documentation by checking consistency against source code and external dependencies, achieving 75% precision on 6,000 commits with automated patch synthesis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Incorrect repository-level documentation (README bugs) causes runtime errors and wastes developer time, yet existing approaches struggle due to the loose connection between prose, code, and source of truth.

Method: READU applies a high-recall commit filter, runs parallel internal (source code) and external (dependencies) consistency checkers, uses an alert judge to filter false positives, and automatically synthesizes documentation patches.

Result: On 6,000 commits from 6 repositories (Linux, Spring Boot, etc.), READU detected 244 true positives at 75% precision, costing under $0.01 and under 1 minute per commit; 217 out of 244 bugs were correctly repaired. 66 bugs were reported, with 44 confirmed and 26 fixed.

Conclusion: Inconsistency-driven detection and automated repair of README bugs is effective, scalable, and cost-efficient for real-world repositories.

Abstract: Repository-level documentation, such as READMEs, is often the first point of contact between users and a repository. When this documentation is incorrect, users may encounter runtime errors or waste their time debugging. We call such mistakes in repository-level documentation README bugs. Addressing README bugs is challenging because documentation mixes prose with code, its connection to the source of truth is loose, and finding a bug still leaves developers to craft a repair. This paper presents READU, an inconsistency-driven technique for just-in-time detection and repair of README bugs. The key insight behind READU is that README bugs often manifest as inconsistencies between documentation and another source of truth: either repository-internal facts, such as source code, or repository-external facts, such as external dependencies. READU applies a high-recall commit filter, runs internal and external consistency checkers in parallel, uses an alert judge to remove false positives, and automatically synthesizes documentation patches. On 6,000 recent commits from six popular repositories including Linux and Spring Boot, READU detects 244 true positives with 75% precision, while consuming less than $0.01 and less than one minute per commit, on average. Of these true positives, READU correctly repairs 217. We report 66 found README bugs, of which (so far) 44 are confirmed and 26 are fixed.

</details>


### [191] [In the Driver's Seat: A Multi-Company Study on the Reality of Autonomous Driving System Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15820)
*Qunying Song,Yuan Gao,Johannes Betz,Dietmar Pfahl,Mohammad Reza Mousavi,Federica Sarro*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: An interview study with ADS experts from nine companies across six countries reveals current scenario-based and X-in-the-loop testing practices, major challenges (scenario realism, coverage, simulation fidelity, acceptance criteria), and proposes an evidence-centered closed-loop testing framework for future automated, data-driven ADS testing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ADS testing lacks well-established standards for scenario selection, performance evaluation, and acceptance criteria despite rapid deployment, motivating the need to understand current industrial practices and challenges.

Method: Conducted an interview study with experts from nine companies across six countries, followed by thematic analysis to synthesize practices, challenges, solutions, and trends.

Result: Current practices focus on scenario-based and X-in-the-loop testing; major challenges include scenario realism, coverage, simulation fidelity, and acceptance criteria; proposed an evidence-centered closed-loop testing framework.

Conclusion: Future ADS testing will become more automated, data-driven, and transparent; the proposed framework provides actionable guidance for practice and research.

Abstract: Autonomous driving systems (ADS) are rapidly advancing and increasingly deployed in real-world applications. This creates growing demands for effective testing to ensure system functionality and safety. However, ADS testing remains complex and lacks well-established standards for scenario selection, performance evaluation, and acceptance criteria. To better understand current ADS testing practices and challenges, we conducted an interview study with experts working on ADS development and testing in nine companies from six different countries. Through thematic analysis, we synthesized industrial testing practices, challenges, potential solutions, future trends, and proposed an evidence-centered closed-loop testing framework for ADS testing. Our findings show that current practices primarily focus on scenario-based and X-in-the-loop testing approaches, supported by diverse tools, metrics, benchmarks, and testing strategies. The participants highlighted major challenges related to scenario realism, scenario coverage, simulation fidelity, and acceptance criteria, while also discussing potential solutions such as the use of AI, world models, and end-to-end approaches. Furthermore, participants envisioned future ADS testing to become more automated, data-driven, and transparent across the industry. Overall, this study provides a comprehensive industry-grounded overview of ADS testing, proposes an evidence-centered closed-loop testing framework to provide actionable guidance for ADS testing, and outlines important directions for future research and practice.

</details>


### [192] [Agentic Synthesis against Counterexample-Supplemented Sketches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15854)
*Muness Castle,Eric Rubeck*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CatSynth uses agentic synthesis against counterexample-supplemented sketches to iteratively evolve a code-shaped sketch by incorporating approved counterexamples, reducing rework (9 Developer calls, 719 lines churn vs 15 calls, 2,394 lines for replay-all) while achieving comparable or better withheld-case pass rates (19/21 vs 15/21).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coding agents can fix a failing example without preserving the domain rule that made it fail, causing repeated plausible mistakes across generations. Current methods lack a mechanism to explicitly capture and retain the governing policy discovered during implementation.

Method: Agentic synthesis against counterexample-supplemented sketches: a human provides a partial code-shaped sketch; a coding agent generates the initial implementation; when a concrete failure exposes missing/mistaken policy, an operator approves the corrected behavior; the agent revises the sketch and repairs/regenerates code for that counterexample; full provenance is preserved; a selected regression set gates each revision; periodic clean regeneration tests whether the evolved sketch carries the learned policy.

Result: In a run with GPT-5.4-mini, 8/14 frozen cases became counterexamples. Rebuilding from the evolved sketch passed 19/21 withheld cases vs 15/21 from initial sketch with replay-all. Retaining code required 9 Developer calls & 719 lines churn vs 15 calls & 2,394 lines for replay-all, passing 18/21 withheld cases.

Conclusion: The evolved sketch carried reviewed policy and retaining code reduced rework in this run, but results are limited to one model and one reveal order, so general superiority or correctness beyond encoded checks is not established.

Abstract: Coding agents can fix a failing example without preserving the domain rule that made it fail, so later generations can repeat the same plausible mistake. We present agentic synthesis against counterexample-supplemented sketches, a repository-native method for systems whose governing policy is discovered during implementation. A human starts with a partial, code-shaped sketch, and a coding agent generates the first implementation. When a concrete failure exposes missing or mistaken policy, an operator explicitly approves the corrected behavior and rule. The agent then revises the sketch and repairs or regenerates code and prompt surfaces for that one counterexample. The full archive preserves provenance; a selected regression set gates each revision before the next candidate is revealed; and periodic clean regeneration tests whether the evolved sketch, rather than prompt history or accumulated examples, carries the learned policy.
  We demonstrate the method with CatSynth, a synthetic browser application and captured coding-agent experiment. In one open-world run with GPT-5.4-mini, 8 of 14 frozen candidate cases became counterexamples. The rebuild controls inherited that promotion schedule, and all three paths passed the 8 accepted cases. Rebuilding from the evolved sketch passed 19 of 21 withheld cases, compared with 15 of 21 when rebuilding from the initial sketch and replaying all accepted examples. Retaining code across counterexamples required 9 Developer calls and 719 lines of cumulative artifact churn, versus 15 calls and 2,394 lines for replay-all, and passed 18 of 21 withheld cases. These results provide inspectable evidence that the evolved sketch carried reviewed policy and that retaining code reduced rework in this run; with one model and one reveal order, they do not establish general superiority or correctness beyond the encoded checks.

</details>


### [193] [Trans-Domain Digital Twin: Conceptual Foundations, Architecture, and Research Outlook](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15908)
*Mansoorali Amiri*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper proposes a trans-domain digital twin framework that connects heterogeneous domain twins through aligned shared states, explicit coupling, temporal coordination, joint decision-making, and feedback adaptation, offering a seven-layer architecture, orchestration core, compliance conditions, and formal operational model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cross-domain digital twin approaches focus on comparison, reuse, semantic mapping, standardization, and interoperability but lack operational connections among domain states, errors, objectives, constraints, decisions, and controls. This creates a gap for systems comprising heterogeneous domains where risks and control consequences cross boundaries.

Method: The paper defines a trans-domain digital twin as an operational formulation along the Composite/Federated Digital Twin Systems continuum. It introduces a seven-layer conceptual architecture, trans-domain orchestration core, minimum compliance conditions, a general operational formalism, progressive fast-meso-slow loops, and a single-episode offline training mechanism linked to bounded online adaptation.

Result: The framework provides conceptual validation and evaluation criteria, a maturity model, a reference deployment architecture, and requirements for runtime safety, provenance, versioning, and model lifecycle management. It is conceptually mappable to existing standards for digital twins, model exchange, distributed simulation, and smart transducers.

Conclusion: Formal compliance and operational effectiveness must be examined through independent benchmarks, uncertainty quantification, ablation testing, and field validation. The framework requires further empirical validation to confirm its practical viability.

Abstract: Complex systems comprise heterogeneous domains whose states, uncertainties, risks, and control consequences can cross domain boundaries. Existing cross-domain digital twin approaches broadly focus on comparison, reuse, semantic mapping, standardization, and interoperability, but do not inherently require operational connections among domain states, errors, objectives, constraints, decisions, and controls. This article proposes the trans-domain digital twin as an operational formulation along the continuum of Composite/Federated Digital Twin Systems. This approach connects heterogeneous domain twins through an aligned shared state, explicit coupling of data, models, states, errors, objectives, and controls, heterogeneous temporal coordination, joint decision-making, and feedback-based adaptation. The proposed framework presents a seven-layer conceptual architecture, a trans-domain orchestration core, minimum compliance conditions, a general operational formalism, progressive fast-meso-slow loops, and a single-episode offline training mechanism linked to bounded online adaptation. It also describes conceptual validation and evaluation criteria, a maturity model, a reference deployment architecture, and requirements for runtime safety, provenance, versioning, and model lifecycle management. The framework is conceptually mappable to standards for digital twins, model exchange, distributed simulation, and smart transducers; however, its formal compliance and operational effectiveness must be examined through independent benchmarks, uncertainty quantification, ablation testing, and field validation.

</details>


### [194] [TARS: A Theory-of-Mind Agent for Personalized In-IDE Code Comprehension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15948)
*Leopoldo Todisco,Antonio Della Porta,Stefano Lambiase,Fabio Palomba*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TARS is an LLM-powered VS Code agent that uses Theory of Mind to profile developer expertise and adapt code explanations accordingly, reducing task completion time by 26% and lowering cognitive load in a controlled experiment with 18 participants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Code comprehension is time-consuming, and existing LLM assistants produce one-size-fits-all explanations that ignore developer context and require disruptive copy-paste workflows.

Method: TARS integrates into VS Code as an LLM agent with a lightweight Theory of Mind paradigm to profile developer expertise, role, and preferences; adapts explanation depth and tone; grounds responses in project documentation via Retrieval-Augmented Generation.

Result: In a controlled experiment with 18 participants on non-trivial Java snippets, TARS users completed tasks 26% faster, reported lower cognitive load, and found explanations meaningfully adapted to their profiles.

Conclusion: Adapting code explanations to developer profiles via Theory of Mind and RAG improves comprehension efficiency and reduces cognitive load, validating the approach for IDE-integrated LLM agents.

Abstract: Code comprehension is one of the most time-consuming tasks in software engineering, yet most LLM-based assistants produce explanations that ignore who is asking and force developers into a disruptive copy-paste workflow. We present TARS, an LLM-powered agent integrated into Visual Studio Code that supports program comprehension through autonomous explanations anchored directly to the code under analysis. Built around a lightweight Theory of Mind paradigm, TARS profiles a developer's expertise, role, and stylistic preferences, then adapts the depth and tone of its explanations accordingly, grounding them in project documentation via Retrieval-Augmented Generation. To evaluate TARS, we conducted a controlled experiment with 18 participants on non-trivial Java snippets. Participants using TARS completed tasks 26\% faster, reported lower cognitive load, and found the explanations meaningfully adapted to their profiles.

</details>


### [195] [DiffTestGen: Change-Directed LLM-Based Testing for Exposing Behavioral Differences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16024)
*Huimin Hu,Cristian Cadar,Michael Pradel*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: DiffTestGen is a novel LLM-based differential testing approach that exposes behavioral differences between old and new program versions by combining static call graph analysis, project documentation, and iterative union coverage feedback. It achieves 78.2% success on 463 PRs with 90.7% union coverage, outperforming baselines by exposing 99 more PRs and increasing coverage by 12.5-15.6 percentage points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing change-directed test generation approaches fail to trigger behavioral differences for many code changes, limiting their ability to verify that behavioral changes occur as intended during software evolution.

Method: DiffTestGen uses (1) static call graph analysis and project documentation to identify valid entry points and guide the LLM toward changed code, and (2) iterative refinement of a novel union coverage metric (combining coverage of modified code in both old and new versions) via targeted coverage feedback to the LLM.

Result: On 463 PRs across two datasets, DiffTestGen exposes behavioral differences in 78.2% of PRs with 90.7% average union coverage, outperforming baselines by 99 more PRs and 12.5-15.6 percentage points higher coverage. Integrated with Testora, it detects regression bugs missed by existing approaches.

Conclusion: DiffTestGen significantly advances change-directed differential testing by effectively combining LLM generation with static analysis and coverage-guided iterative feedback, enabling better detection of behavioral differences and regression bugs in evolving software.

Abstract: As software evolves over time, it is important to ensure that any behavioral changes occur as intended by developers. A promising approach for this goal is to generate tests that expose behavioral differences between the old and new versions of a program. However, current approaches fail to trigger behavioral differences for many code changes. This paper presents~DiffTestGen, a novel change-directed, LLM-based differential testing approach specifically designed to expose behavioral differences introduced by a code change. The approach is enabled by two key contributions: First, DiffTestGen leverages static call graph analysis and project documentation to identify valid entry points for test generation and to guide the LLM toward reaching the changed code. Second, DiffTestGen iteratively improves our newly introduced union coverage metric, which combines coverage of modified code in the old and the new version, by providing targeted coverage feedback to the LLM. We evaluate DiffTestGen on two datasets comprising a total of 463 PRs. DiffTestGen exposes behavioral differences in 78.2% of the PRs while achieving an average union coverage of 90.7%. Compared with the baselines, DiffTestGen exposes 99 more PRs overall and increases code coverage by 12.5% and 15.6% percentage points, respectively. By integrating DiffTestGen with the Testora regression detector, we show that the identified behavioral differences can be used to detect regression bugs missed by the best existing approaches.

</details>


### [196] [What Does It Take to Research with AI? A Rapid Review of Competencies to Train LLM-Literate Researchers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16083)
*Danilo Monteiro Ribeiro,Ronnie de Souza Santos,Rodrigo Siqueira,Breno Andrade,Rafael Batista Duarte,Gilberto Hida,Julia Alencar,Gustavo Pinto*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A rapid review of 40 articles identified 8 key competencies researchers need to use LLMs critically, with domain expertise and oversight of AI outputs being the most prevalent, suggesting that preparing researchers requires integrated epistemic, ethical, and methodological skills beyond technical instruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing adoption of LLMs in scientific research created a need to understand what competencies researchers and graduate students require to use these tools critically and responsibly.

Method: Rapid review analyzing 194 articles (2022–2025) from Elicit and Google Scholar, with 40 selected via independent dual screening (Gwet AC1: 0.76–0.83) for competency extraction and thematic analysis.

Result: Eight competencies identified: domain expertise and oversight of AI outputs (n=123), metacognition and decision making about AI use (n=55), ethics and academic integrity (n=53), prompt engineering for research (n=38), reproducibility of AI use (n=29), and AI literacy and technical knowledge (n=16), with the latter identified as a risk factor when absent.

Conclusion: Preparing researchers to use LLMs requires an integrated set of epistemic, ethical, and methodological competencies centered on human accountability, with direct implications for graduate program design and AI literacy initiatives.

Abstract: The growing adoption of Large Language Models in scientific research has created a need to understand what competencies researchers and graduate students require to use these tools critically and responsibly. This rapid review analyzed 194 articles retrieved from Elicit and Google Scholar (2022 to 2025), from which 40 were selected for competency extraction and thematic analysis following independent dual screening (Gwet AC1: 0.76 to 0.83). Eight competencies were identified. The most prevalent was domain expertise and oversight of AI outputs (n = 123), encompassing subject matter mastery, systematic skepticism, source verification, and researcher accountability. Other key competencies include metacognition and decision making about AI use (n = 55), ethics and academic integrity (n = 53), prompt engineering for research (n = 38), and reproducibility of AI use (n = 29). AI literacy and technical knowledge (n = 16) was explicitly identified as a risk factor when absent, with domain expertise treated as a prerequisite for meaningful critical evaluation. The findings suggest that preparing researchers to use LLMs goes beyond technical instruction, requiring an integrated set of epistemic, ethical, and methodological competencies centered on human accountability for the knowledge produced. These results have direct implications for the design of graduate programs and AI literacy initiatives.

</details>


### [197] [Comparison of Energy System Optimization Software and Evaluation of Selected Frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16121)
*Pedro Caixeta,David Gawron,Hüseyin K. Çakmak,Haozhen Cheng*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study evaluates five energy system optimization software tools (REMix, MTRESS, COMANDO, OEMOF, HOMER PRO) against defined criteria, finding REMix best for investment path optimization, COMANDO for atypical components, OEMOF for standard open-source optimization, and HOMER PRO for rapid synthesis with minimal programming. MTRESS analysis was incomplete due to insufficient information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Selecting the most suitable software tool for energy system optimization is complex due to diverse objectives and requirements. This study aims to address this by evaluating five tools to identify their most appropriate use scenarios.

Method: The authors conducted an extensive review of literature, documentation, tutorials, and example models for REMix, MTRESS, COMANDO, OEMOF, and HOMER PRO. They developed a set of comparison criteria and used them to evaluate each tool's suitability for different scenarios.

Result: REMix excels in investment path optimization. COMANDO is best for systems with atypical components. OEMOF is the top open-source choice for standard optimization problems. HOMER PRO suits users seeking rapid synthesis optimization with limited programming experience. MTRESS analysis could not be completed due to insufficient information.

Conclusion: Different software tools are suited for different energy optimization scenarios, and a one-size-fits-all approach is not appropriate. The findings provide guidance for practitioners selecting tools based on their specific needs.

Abstract: Optimizing energy systems is a crucial step toward achieving a carbon-neutral future, with software tools playing a major role in the process. However, selecting the most suitable tool for specific optimization challenges can be complex, given the diverse objectives and requirements of various energy systems. In this study, we aim to address this issue by evaluating five preselected software tools-REMix, MTRESS, COMANDO, OEMOF, and HOMER PRO-to identify the scenarios for which they are most suitable. To achieve this, we conducted an extensive review of literature, documentation, tutorials, and example models, and developed a set of comparison criteria that were subsequently used to evaluate these tools. Our analysis suggests that REMix is particularly effective for scenarios where investment path optimization is required. COMANDO excels in systems with atypical components. OEMOF is the best-suited open-source tool for standard optimization problems. HOMER PRO is recommended for users seeking rapid synthesis optimization, especially those with limited programming experience. Due to difficulties in obtaining sufficient information on MTRESS, we were unable to complete the analysis for this tool.

</details>
